    A complete field manual

    Every kind of microorganism, explained properly.

    Bacteria, archaea, viruses, fungi, protozoa, algae — the organisms that ran this planet for three billion years before anything else arrived, and still run most of it. This is the long version: structure, metabolism, genetics, classification, disease, and use.

    1030Bacterial & archaeal cells on Earth
    1031Virus particles — most abundant entity known
    3Domains of cellular life
    ~1%Of microbial species ever grown in culture
    I · FoundationsScale, history, microscopy, classification, physiology II · BacteriaStructure, metabolism, every major phylum, pathogens III · ArchaeaThe third domain and life at the physical limits IV · VirusesBaltimore classes, phages, viroids, prions V · FungiYeasts, moulds, mycoses, antifungals, industry VI · ProtistsProtozoa, microalgae, and microscopic animals VII · Microbes & the WorldMicrobiome, immunity, AMR, food, ecology, frontiers

    Foundations of Microbiology

    Microbiology is the study of life too small to resolve with the unaided human eye — conventionally, anything below roughly 100–200 μm, the limit at which a person with good vision can just distinguish a speck. That single operational definition conceals an extraordinary sprawl: it takes in the three domains of cellular life, the acellular viruses and viroids, the misfolded proteins we call prions, and a scattering of genuinely microscopic animals. Microbes constitute the overwhelming majority of Earth's biological diversity and something on the order of 1030 individual cells — a number that dwarfs the estimated 1022 stars in the observable universe. They invented photosynthesis, oxygenated the atmosphere, run the nitrogen and sulfur cycles, and became the mitochondria and chloroplasts inside every plant and animal. This pillar establishes the vocabulary, the measurements, the history, the instruments, and the classification logic on which every later section depends.

    1. What Is a Microorganism?

    A microorganism (or microbe) is an organism that is individually too small to be seen without magnification. The definition is pragmatic rather than phylogenetic: it groups together organisms that share no recent common ancestry and differ in fundamental cell architecture. A microbiologist's remit therefore spans:

    • Bacteria — prokaryotic, peptidoglycan-walled, ester-linked lipid membranes. Escherichia coli, Bacillus subtilis, Mycobacterium tuberculosis.
    • Archaea — prokaryotic in organisation but with ether-linked isoprenoid lipids, pseudomurein or S-layer walls, and eukaryote-like transcription machinery. Methanocaldococcus jannaschii, Halobacterium salinarum, Sulfolobus acidocaldarius.
    • Microbial eukaryotes — protists (Paramecium, Plasmodium falciparum, Giardia intestinalis), fungi (Saccharomyces cerevisiae, Candida albicans, Aspergillus fumigatus), and microalgae (Chlamydomonas reinhardtii, diatoms such as Thalassiosira pseudonana).
    • Acellular agents — viruses, viroids, satellite RNAs, and prions.

    The boundary cases

    Viruses. A virus is an obligate intracellular genetic parasite: a nucleic acid genome (DNA or RNA, single- or double-stranded) inside a protein capsid, sometimes wrapped in a host-derived lipid envelope. Viruses possess no ribosomes, generate no ATP, and cannot grow or divide — they are replicated by a host's machinery. Whether they are "alive" is a definitional argument rather than an empirical one. The discovery of giant viruses has blurred the line considerably: Mimivirus (isolated from a cooling tower in Bradford, England, in 1992; recognised as a virus in 2003) is 750 nm across with a 1.18 Mb genome encoding ~979 proteins including aminoacyl-tRNA synthetases; Pandoravirus salinus (2013) reaches 1 μm with a 2.5 Mb genome. These are larger and more gene-rich than Mycoplasma genitalium, a free-living bacterium with a 580 kb genome and 525 genes.

    Prions. Prions contain no nucleic acid at all. They are misfolded conformers of a normal host protein — in mammals, PrPC (cellular prion protein) refolded into the β-sheet-rich PrPSc — that template the same misfolding onto their normal counterparts, propagating an infectious conformation. Stanley Prusiner coined the term in 1982 and received the 1997 Nobel Prize in Physiology or Medicine. Prion diseases include scrapie in sheep, bovine spongiform encephalopathy (BSE), and in humans kuru, Creutzfeldt–Jakob disease, and fatal familial insomnia. Prions resist autoclaving at standard 121°C cycles; decontamination requires 1 N NaOH or 134°C for 18 minutes.

    Microscopic animals. Tardigrades ("water bears", phylum Tardigrada, 0.05–1.2 mm) and rotifers (phylum Rotifera, 0.1–0.5 mm) are true multicellular animals with nervous systems, guts, and gonads — yet they are smaller than many protists and are routinely encountered on a microscope slide. Tardigrades survive desiccation via anhydrobiosis, replacing cellular water with trehalose and intrinsically disordered CAHS proteins, and withstand 5,000–6,000 Gy of gamma radiation (a human LD50 is about 4 Gy). Bdelloid rotifers have reproduced asexually for tens of millions of years and incorporate roughly 8–10% foreign DNA acquired horizontally. Neither is conventionally taught as "microbiology", which shows the definition is about size, not biology.

    Giant bacteria. At the other extreme, some prokaryotes are visible to the naked eye. Thiomargarita magnifica, described from mangrove sediments in Guadeloupe in 2022 (Volland et al., Science), forms filaments averaging ~1 cm and reaching 2 cm — roughly 5,000 times the volume of a typical bacterium. It solves the surface-area problem with a vast central vacuole occupying ~73% of the volume, confining cytoplasm to a thin peripheral rind, and it packages its DNA into hundreds of thousands of membrane-bound "pepins" — a compartmentalisation previously thought to be exclusively eukaryotic. Its relative Thiomargarita namibiensis ("sulfur pearl of Namibia", described 1999) reaches 750 μm as spheres. Epulopiscium spp. (now Epulopiscium fishelsoni and relatives), symbionts in the gut of surgeonfish, reach 600 μm × 80 μm, carry up to 200,000–700,000 genome copies by extreme polyploidy, and reproduce by forming live daughter cells internally rather than by binary fission.

    Why the definition frays at the edges

    "Microorganism" is a convenience of human perception, not a natural category. Some bacteria are macroscopic; some animals are microscopic; viruses may not be organisms at all; prions are not even nucleic-acid-based. The useful modern framing is that microbiology is defined by its methods — microscopy, culture, sterile technique, and sequence-based community analysis — rather than by a clean taxonomic boundary.

    2. Scale, Size, and the Physics of Being Small

    Two units dominate. A micrometre (μm, micron) is 10−6 m — one thousandth of a millimetre. A nanometre (nm) is 10−9 m, one thousandth of a micrometre. A human hair is 60–100 μm thick; a human red blood cell is 7–8 μm; a typical bacterium such as E. coli is a rod 1–2 μm long by 0.5 μm wide. Most viruses fall between 20 and 300 nm. The full span from the smallest virus to the largest bacterium covers six orders of magnitude in linear dimension — roughly the ratio between a grain of sand and a football stadium.

    Why cells cannot simply be any size they like

    Cell size is bounded above by geometry and physics, not by convention. As a sphere of radius r grows, its surface area increases as 4πr2 while its volume increases as (4/3)πr3. The surface-area-to-volume ratio is therefore 3/r — it falls in inverse proportion to radius. Since nutrients enter and wastes leave across the membrane (an area-dependent process) while metabolic demand scales with cytoplasmic volume, a cell that doubles its radius halves its membrane supply per unit of demand. A 1 μm coccus has an S/V of about 6 μm−1; a 100 μm sphere has 0.06 μm−1, a hundredfold disadvantage.

    The second constraint is diffusion. Molecular transport in cytoplasm is not directed; it is a random walk described by the Einstein relation, in which mean displacement scales with the square root of time: t ≈ x2/(2D). With a small-metabolite diffusion coefficient of roughly D = 500 μm2/s in water, crossing 1 μm takes about a millisecond, but crossing 1 mm takes about 1,000 seconds — nearly 17 minutes. Diffusion is superb at bacterial scale and hopeless at millimetre scale. Prokaryotes lack the motor-protein-driven cytoskeletal transport that lets large eukaryotic cells beat this limit.

    Giant bacteria therefore cheat rather than defy the rules. Thiomargarita and Epulopiscium keep their metabolically active cytoplasm as a thin shell — often 0.5–2 μm thick — around a large inert vacuole, so no molecule ever has to diffuse far. They also multiply their genomes: a single Epulopiscium cell may carry hundreds of thousands of chromosome copies so that transcription can be local rather than centralised. Smallness has its own floor: a cell must contain at least a genome, ribosomes, and the machinery to use them. Estimates put the minimum viable free-living cell at 200–300 nm diameter and roughly 200–300 essential genes — a figure tested directly by JCVI-syn3.0 (2016), a synthetic minimal cell with 473 genes, of which 149 had no known function at the time.

    A useful mental anchor: if an E. coli cell were scaled to the size of a person (~1.75 m), a poliovirus would be about 5 cm across, a ribosome about 3.5 cm, a water molecule half a millimetre, and Thiomargarita magnifica would stand roughly 35 km tall.

    3. A History of Microbiology

    The intellectual arc runs from speculation about invisible seeds of disease, through the invention of instruments that made microbes visible, to the demonstration that they cause disease, to the molecular revolution that revealed most of them had never been seen at all.

    YearFigureContribution
    1546Girolamo FracastoroDe Contagione et Contagiosis Morbis proposes that disease spreads by seminaria contagionis ("seeds of contagion") transmitted by direct contact, by fomites (his coinage), or at a distance
    1665Robert HookeMicrographia; coins "cell" from cork sections; first published drawing of a microorganism (the fungus Mucor)
    1674–1683Antonie van LeeuwenhoekSingle-lens microscopes at 200–270×; describes "animalcules" in pond water (1674), then in dental plaque (1683) — the first observation of bacteria
    1668Francesco RediCovered-meat experiment refutes spontaneous generation of maggots
    1765–1776Lazzaro SpallanzaniSealed, prolonged-boiled infusions remain sterile; Needham counters that boiling destroys a "vital force" and sealing excludes air
    1796Edward JennerCowpox inoculation against smallpox — vaccination before any germ theory
    1847Ignaz SemmelweisChlorinated-lime handwashing at Vienna General Hospital cuts puerperal fever mortality from ~18% to ~1–2%
    1854John SnowBroad Street pump; epidemiological demonstration of waterborne cholera
    1859–1861Louis PasteurSwan-neck flask experiments settle the spontaneous generation debate; also fermentation as microbial, pasteurisation (1864), anthrax and rabies vaccines (1881, 1885)
    1867Joseph ListerCarbolic acid (phenol) antisepsis in surgery; published in The Lancet, reducing amputation mortality from ~46% to ~15%
    1876–1884Robert KochBacillus anthracis life cycle (1876); solid media and pure culture; tubercle bacillus (1882); Vibrio cholerae (1884); postulates formalised 1884/1890
