    1884Hans Christian GramThe differential stain that still divides the bacterial world
    1892 / 1898Dmitri Ivanovsky / Martinus BeijerinckTobacco mosaic agent passes a Chamberland filter; Beijerinck names it contagium vivum fluidum and establishes virology; Loeffler & Frosch identify foot-and-mouth virus (1898)
    1890s–1900sBeijerinck, WinogradskyEnrichment culture, chemolithotrophy, nitrogen fixation — the birth of environmental microbiology
    1910Paul EhrlichSalvarsan (arsphenamine) for syphilis — the first designed antimicrobial, "magic bullet"
    1928 / 1940–1945Alexander Fleming; Florey & ChainPenicillium notatum contaminant lyses staphylococci on a plate at St Mary's, London; Oxford team purifies penicillin and proves clinical efficacy; Nobel Prize 1945
    1944 / 1952Avery–MacLeod–McCarty; Hershey–ChaseDNA identified as the transforming principle in Streptococcus pneumoniae; phage experiments confirm DNA as genetic material
    1977Carl Woese & George Fox16S rRNA cataloguing reveals the Archaea as a third domain of life
    1983–1985Kary MullisPCR — makes culture-independent detection routine
    1985–1990Norman Pace and colleaguesDirect rRNA gene cloning from seawater; environmental sequences with no cultured representative
    1995TIGR / Venter, Smith, FraserHaemophilus influenzae Rd — first complete genome of a free-living organism (1.83 Mb); Mycoplasma genitalium follows the same year
    2004–presentVenter, Banfield, HMP, Tara OceansSargasso Sea shotgun metagenomics (2004); acid mine drainage community genomics; Human Microbiome Project (2007–2016); Earth Microbiome Project; thousands of metagenome-assembled genomes from uncultured lineages
    2015 / 2017Lokiarchaeota; Asgard superphylumMetagenomic recovery of archaea bearing eukaryotic signature proteins from Arctic sediments near Loki's Castle vents

    The swan-neck flask

    Pasteur's decisive experiment answered Needham's objection directly. He boiled nutrient broth in flasks whose necks had been drawn out into long S-shaped curves that remained fully open to the air. Air could enter freely; dust and the microbes it carried settled in the bend and never reached the broth. The flasks stayed sterile indefinitely — some of Pasteur's originals remain clear at the Institut Pasteur today. Tilting a flask so the broth touched the trapped dust in the neck, or snapping the neck off, produced growth within days. This isolated the single variable that mattered: not air, not a vital force, but particulate contamination. In 1864 Pasteur declared at the Sorbonne that "never will the doctrine of spontaneous generation recover from the mortal blow of this simple experiment."

    Koch's postulates — and where they break

    Formalised around 1884, the postulates supplied the logical criteria for attributing a disease to a specific organism:

    1. The organism must be found in abundance in all individuals suffering the disease, and absent from healthy individuals.
    2. It must be isolated and grown in pure culture.
    3. The cultured organism must cause the disease when introduced into a healthy susceptible host.
    4. It must be re-isolated from the experimentally infected host and shown identical to the original.

    Modern limitations of Koch's postulates

    Unculturable organisms. Mycobacterium leprae has never been grown in axenic culture; Treponema pallidum resisted continuous in-vitro culture until 2018. Postulate 2 simply fails. Asymptomatic carriers. Neisseria meningitidis, Streptococcus pneumoniae, and Staphylococcus aureus colonise healthy people at rates of 10–40%, violating postulate 1 — Koch himself noted this for V. cholerae. No animal model. Many human pathogens (e.g. Neisseria gonorrhoeae, HIV in most species) have no naturally susceptible non-human host, and deliberate human infection is usually unethical. Polymicrobial and multifactorial disease. Periodontitis, bacterial vaginosis, and much of inflammatory bowel disease reflect community shifts, not a single agent. Viruses and prions were unknown as categories in 1884. Stanley Falkow's molecular Koch's postulates (1988) reframed the logic at the gene level: a virulence gene should be present in pathogenic strains and absent or inactive in avirulent ones; specific inactivation of that gene should reduce virulence; and restoring the wild-type allele should restore it. Fredricks and Relman (1996) proposed a further sequence-based set for uncultured agents, requiring that pathogen nucleic acid be present in diseased tissue, correlate with lesions, and decline with recovery.

    4. Microscopy, Resolution, and Staining

    Magnification is trivially easy; resolution — the ability to distinguish two adjacent points as separate — is the real limit. Ernst Abbe formalised this in 1873. The minimum resolvable distance is

    d = λ / (2 n sin θ) = λ / (2 NA)

    where λ is the illuminating wavelength, n the refractive index of the medium, θ the half-angle of the collected cone of light, and NA = n sin θ the numerical aperture. With green light (λ ≈ 550 nm), air (n = 1.0) and a good dry objective (NA ≈ 0.95), d ≈ 290 nm. Immersion oil raises n to ~1.515, giving NA up to 1.4 and d ≈ 200 nm — the practical floor of light microscopy, and the reason a 100× objective must be oiled. Magnification beyond that ("empty magnification") enlarges a blur. Electron microscopy escapes the limit by using electrons: at 100 kV the de Broglie wavelength is ~0.0037 nm, and although lens aberrations mean real instruments achieve far less than the theoretical limit, sub-ångström resolution is now routine in aberration-corrected TEM.

    TechniqueTypical resolutionPrincipleBest for
    Brightfield light microscopy~200 nm (oil, NA 1.4)Absorption contrast; usually needs stainStained smears, colony morphology, routine diagnostics
    Phase contrast (Zernike, 1934; Nobel 1953)~200 nmConverts optical path-length differences into amplitude differences via a phase ringLive unstained cells, motility, endospores as bright refractile bodies
    Darkfield~200 nm; detects objects below itOnly obliquely scattered light enters the objective; bright object on black fieldThin spirochaetes such as Treponema pallidum (0.15 μm wide)
    DIC (Nomarski)~200 nmSheared polarised beams recombine; gradient of refractive index gives pseudo-3D reliefThick live specimens, protists, optical sectioning
    Widefield fluorescence~200 nm lateralFluorophore excitation and Stokes-shifted emission, isolated by filtersImmunofluorescence, FISH, GFP fusions, live/dead staining
    Confocal laser scanning~180 nm lateral, ~500 nm axialPinhole rejects out-of-focus light; point scanning builds optical sectionsBiofilm architecture, 3D reconstruction, thick tissue
    STED20–50 nmDoughnut-shaped depletion beam quenches fluorescence outside a sub-diffraction centreLive-cell super-resolution of cytoskeletal and membrane structures
    PALM / STORM10–30 nmStochastic activation of sparse single fluorophores, centroid fitting, thousands of frames reconstructedSingle-molecule localisation, chemotaxis receptor arrays, divisome proteins
    TEM~0.05–0.2 nmElectrons transmitted through ultrathin (50–100 nm) sections; heavy-metal contrastInternal ultrastructure, virion morphology, negative staining
    SEM~1–10 nmScanned beam; secondary electrons imaged from a metal-coated surfaceSurface topography, biofilms, appendages, spore ornamentation
    Cryo-EM / cryo-electron tomography~2–4 nm in situ; <0.2 nm for averaged single particlesVitrification in milliseconds without fixatives or stains; tilt series reconstructed into a 3D volumeNative-state macromolecular machines: flagellar motors, secretion systems, chemoreceptor arrays, intact virions

    Cryo-EM deserves special note: the 2017 Nobel Prize in Chemistry (Dubochet, Frank, Henderson) recognised the technique, and the "resolution revolution" driven by direct electron detectors after 2012 has made near-atomic structures routine. Cryo-electron tomography now images intact bacterial cells frozen mid-process, revealing structures such as the type III secretion injectisome and the 45 nm flagellar basal body in situ.

    Staining

    Most bacterial cytoplasm has a refractive index close to that of water and is essentially invisible in brightfield. Stains supply contrast. Simple stains use a single basic dye — methylene blue, crystal violet, safranin, carbol fuchsin — whose positively charged chromophore binds the net-negative bacterial surface, revealing shape, size, and arrangement but not composition. Negative staining with nigrosin or India ink stains the background rather than the cell, avoiding heat fixation and shrinkage.

    The Gram stain (Hans Christian Gram, Berlin, 1884) remains the single most important test in clinical bacteriology, typically giving actionable information within 10 minutes of a specimen arriving.

    StepReagentTimeGram-positiveGram-negative
    1. Primary stainCrystal violet~60 sPurplePurple
    2. MordantGram's iodine (I2/KI)~60 sPurple — large CV–I complex formsPurple — complex also forms
    3. Decolouriser95% ethanol or acetone–alcohol5–15 s (critical)Remains purple — thick peptidoglycan dehydrates and traps the complexColourless — outer membrane lipids dissolve, thin peptidoglycan releases the complex
    4. CounterstainSafranin (or basic fuchsin)~60 sStays purple (safranin masked)Pink/red

    The mechanism turns on wall architecture. Gram-positive cells (Staphylococcus aureus, Bacillus subtilis, Clostridioides difficile) have a 20–80 nm multilayered peptidoglycan sacculus laced with teichoic acids; ethanol dehydrates and shrinks its pores, physically trapping the crystal violet–iodine complex. Gram-negative cells (E. coli, Pseudomonas aeruginosa, Neisseria) have only 2–7 nm of peptidoglycan in a periplasm beneath an outer membrane rich in lipopolysaccharide; alcohol dissolves that outer membrane and the complex washes straight out. Over-decolourisation is the classic error, turning Gram-positives falsely negative; cultures older than 24 h and dying Bacillus or Clostridium cells become Gram-variable. Some organisms are unstainable: Mycoplasma has no wall at all, Chlamydia is intracellular and tiny, and Mycobacterium resists the stain because of its waxy envelope.

    StainTargetKey reagentsResultExample organisms
    Acid-fast (Ziehl–Neelsen, 1882–83)Mycolic acids (60–90 carbon) in the envelopeCarbol fuchsin with heat; 3% HCl in ethanol; methylene blue counterstainAcid-fast cells red on blue backgroundMycobacterium tuberculosis, M. leprae, Nocardia (partially), Cryptosporidium oocysts
    Kinyoun ("cold" acid-fast)SameHigher-phenol carbol fuchsin, no heatRed on blueSame; safer for routine labs
    Auramine–rhodamineMycolic acidsFluorochrome, viewed under fluorescenceYellow-green glowing rodsHigh-throughput TB screening; more sensitive than ZN
    Endospore (Schaeffer–Fulton)Dipicolinic acid–calcium spore core, keratin-like coatMalachite green with steam; water rinse; safraninGreen spores, red vegetative cellsBacillus anthracis, B. cereus, Clostridium botulinum, C. tetani
    Capsule (negative + simple)Polysaccharide (rarely polypeptide) glycocalyxIndia ink or nigrosin background, plus a cell stainClear halo around a stained cell on a dark fieldCryptococcus neoformans, Klebsiella pneumoniae, Streptococcus pneumoniae, B. anthracis (poly-D-glutamate)
    Flagella (Leifson / Gray)Flagellar filaments (~20 nm, below light resolution)Tannic acid mordant thickens the filament before dyeVisible flagella; reveals monotrichous, lophotrichous, amphitrichous, peritrichous arrangementPseudomonas (polar), Proteus and E. coli (peritrichous), Vibrio (single polar sheathed)
    Quellung reactionCapsular polysaccharide serotypeType-specific antiserumCapsule appears swollen and refractileSerotyping S. pneumoniae, H. influenzae

    5. Classification, Phylogeny, and the Species Problem

    Until 1977, life was sorted by morphology and physiology into five kingdoms, with all prokaryotes lumped together as "Monera". Carl Woese and George Fox destroyed that picture by treating molecules as documents of evolutionary history. Comparing oligonucleotide catalogues of small-subunit ribosomal RNA, they found that the methanogens were as distant from familiar bacteria as either was from eukaryotes. The result was the three-domain system: Bacteria, Archaea, Eukarya.

    FeatureBacteriaArchaeaEukarya
    NucleusAbsentAbsentPresent
    Membrane lipidsEster-linked, straight-chain fatty acids, glycerol-3-phosphateEther-linked, branched isoprenoids, glycerol-1-phosphate; some monolayer tetraethersEster-linked fatty acids
    Cell wallPeptidoglycan (murein) with D-amino acidsPseudomurein, S-layer, or none — never true peptidoglycanCellulose (plants), chitin (fungi), or none
    Ribosome70S (16S + 23S/5S)70S but eukaryote-like proteins and factors80S (18S + 28S/5.8S/5S)
    RNA polymeraseOne, 4–5 subunitsOne, 8–12 subunits; TATA-binding protein and TFIIB homologueThree, 12–17 subunits each
    Initiator tRNAFormylmethionineMethionineMethionine
    HistonesAbsent (nucleoid-associated proteins instead)Present in most lineagesPresent
    Sensitive to streptomycin/chloramphenicolYesNoNo (cytoplasmic ribosomes)
    Introns in protein genesRareRareCommon (spliceosomal)
    MethanogenesisNeverOnly hereNever

    Why rRNA works as a molecular chronometer

    The 16S rRNA gene (~1,542 nt in E. coli; 18S in eukaryotes) satisfies an unusually demanding set of criteria simultaneously. It is universal — every cellular organism has it, so any two organisms can be compared. It is functionally constant, always performing the same job in translation, so sequence change reflects time rather than a change of role. It is large enough to carry statistical signal but small enough to sequence cheaply. Crucially, it is a mosaic of conserved and variable regions: nine hypervariable regions (V1–V9) drift quickly and resolve close relationships, while the intervening conserved stretches let a single pair of universal PCR primers (such as 27F/1492R, or 515F/806R for the V4 region) amplify from essentially any prokaryote. Finally, it is rarely transferred horizontally, because it is deeply embedded in the ribosome's interaction network. Its limitations are real: it seldom resolves species reliably (E. coli and Shigella are indistinguishable at 16S; several Bacillus species differ by under 1%), many genomes carry 1–15 slightly divergent rRNA operon copies, and rate variation across lineages violates a strict clock. Modern phylogenomics therefore uses concatenated sets of 40–120 conserved single-copy marker proteins (the basis of the Genome Taxonomy Database, GTDB).

    Naming: binomial nomenclature

    Bacterial and archaeal names are governed by the International Code of Nomenclature of Prokaryotes; fungi and protists by the ICN and ICZN respectively. Rules in practice: the name is a Latin binomial, Genus species; the genus is capitalised, the specific epithet never is, even when derived from a person or place (Yersinia pestis, Legionella pneumophila); the whole binomial is italicised (or underlined in handwriting); after first mention the genus is abbreviated (E. coli, S. aureus) provided no ambiguity arises; strain designators are appended in Roman type and not italicised (Escherichia coli K-12 MG1655, Staphylococcus aureus USA300, Bacillus subtilis 168). Valid publication requires a type strain deposited in two culture collections in different countries and publication in the International Journal of Systematic and Evolutionary Microbiology. Uncultivated taxa known only from sequence take the provisional status "Candidatus" — e.g. "Candidatus Pelagibacter ubique" before cultivation, "Candidatus Lokiarchaeum".

    Below species sit informal ranks: strain (a specific isolate descended from a single colony), serovar/serotype (distinguished by surface antigens — Salmonella enterica serovar Typhimurium, or the >2,600 Salmonella serovars catalogued in the Kauffmann–White scheme; E. coli O157:H7 names its O-antigen and H-flagellar antigen), biovar (biochemical/physiological differences, e.g. Yersinia pestis biovars Antiqua, Medievalis, Orientalis), pathovar (host range in plant pathogens, e.g. Pseudomonas syringae pv. tomato), and morphovar.

    The prokaryotic species problem

    The biological species concept — interbreeding populations reproductively isolated from others — is useless for organisms that reproduce clonally and exchange genes promiscuously across genus and even domain boundaries. Prokaryotic taxonomy has therefore settled on pragmatic, quantitative thresholds. The classical standard was DNA–DNA hybridisation (DDH) ≥ 70% plus ≤ 5°C difference in melting temperature. Because DDH is laborious and non-cumulative, it has been replaced by genome-sequence proxies: Average Nucleotide Identity (ANI) ≥ 95–96% between shared genes, which maps almost exactly onto 70% DDH, and digital DDH (dDDH) ≥ 70%. Genus boundaries approximate 60–80% AAI (average amino acid identity), and 98.7% 16S identity is a common screening threshold below which two isolates are almost certainly different species. These are conventions with a solid empirical basis, not natural discontinuities — and in some lineages the genetic diversity is genuinely continuous.

    Horizontal gene transfer and pangenomes

    Horizontal gene transfer (HGT) moves DNA between contemporaries rather than from parent to offspring, via three classical routes: transformation (uptake of naked environmental DNA by naturally competent cells such as Streptococcus pneumoniae, Haemophilus influenzae, Neisseria gonorrhoeae, Bacillus subtilis — the phenomenon Griffith described in 1928), transduction (phage-mediated; generalised or specialised, and the route by which Corynebacterium diphtheriae and E. coli O157 acquired their toxin genes), and conjugation (plasmid- or ICE-encoded pilus-mediated transfer, the principal vehicle for multidrug resistance). Additional routes include gene transfer agents and membrane vesicles.

    The consequence is that a "tree of life" built from one gene may disagree sharply with one built from another. Roughly 5–20% of a typical bacterial genome is of recent foreign origin. This forced a redefinition of what a genome even is. The pangenome concept (Tettelin et al., 2005, from eight Streptococcus agalactiae genomes) splits gene content into the core genome present in all strains of a species, the accessory/dispensable genome present in some, and strain-specific unique genes. E. coli is the classic example: any single strain carries roughly 4,000–5,500 genes, the species core is only about 1,500–3,000, but the species pangenome exceeds 20,000 gene families and continues to grow with every new genome sequenced — an open pangenome. Species with restricted niches, such as Bacillus anthracis or Mycobacterium tuberculosis, have closed pangenomes that saturate quickly.

    Asgard archaea and eukaryogenesis

    The origin of the eukaryotic cell is the deepest open question the three-domain framework raised. In 2015 a metagenome from marine sediment near the Loki's Castle hydrothermal vents (Arctic Mid-Ocean Ridge, ~2,350 m depth) yielded "Candidatus Lokiarchaeum", an archaeon carrying an unprecedented complement of eukaryotic signature proteins — actins, profilins, gelsolins, ESCRT-III components, and over 90 small Ras-superfamily GTPases. Sister lineages followed: Thorarchaeota, Odinarchaeota, Heimdallarchaeota, together the Asgard superphylum, with Heimdallarchaeia currently the closest known relatives of eukaryotes. In 2020 a Japanese team led by Hiroyuki Imachi reported Candidatus Prometheoarchaeum syntrophicum strain MK-D1 after twelve years of anaerobic bioreactor cultivation — a slow-growing (doubling time 14–25 days) Lokiarchaeote that extends long branching protrusions and lives syntrophically with a sulfate reducer and a methanogen. This supports "two-domain" (eocyte) models in which eukaryotes arose from within the Archaea, and the entangle–engulf–endogenise (E3) model of how a host archaeon came to enclose an alphaproteobacterial partner.

    Endosymbiotic theory

    Proposed by Konstantin Mereschkowski (1905/1910) and revived with decisive evidence by Lynn Margulis in 1967 ("On the Origin of Mitosing Cells"), the theory holds that mitochondria descend from an engulfed alphaproteobacterium (related to Rickettsiales and the free-living Rickettsia-like lineages) and plastids from an engulfed cyanobacterium (closest to Gloeomargarita lithophora). The evidence is consistent and multi-stranded:

    Line of evidenceMitochondriaChloroplasts
    GenomeOwn circular DNA; human mtDNA 16,569 bp, 37 genes; Reclinomonas americana retains 97Own circular DNA, typically 120–160 kb, 100–250 genes
    Ribosomes70S, prokaryote-type70S, prokaryote-type
    Antibiotic sensitivityInhibited by chloramphenicol, tetracycline (basis of some drug toxicity)Inhibited by streptomycin, chloramphenicol
    MembranesDouble; inner membrane contains cardiolipin, a bacterial lipid, and lacks cholesterolDouble (three or four in secondary plastids of euglenids and dinoflagellates — evidence of nested engulfment)
    DivisionBinary fission using FtsZ homologues in some protists; Drp1/dynamin in animalsBinary fission using bacterial FtsZ ring
    Phylogeny of rRNABranches within AlphaproteobacteriaBranches within Cyanobacteria
    Gene transfer to nucleus>98% of the ancestral genes relocated; ~1,000–1,500 nuclear-encoded proteins importedMost genes relocated; proteins re-imported via TOC/TIC translocons

    Living analogues make the process concrete: Paulinella chromatophora acquired a cyanobacterial endosymbiont independently roughly 90–140 million years ago, giving a second, much more recent primary plastid; and insect endosymbionts such as Buchnera aphidicola (aphids, ~640 kb genome) or Carsonella ruddii (psyllids, 160 kb, 182 genes) show extreme genome reduction in progress.

    6. Universal Microbial Physiology

    Every organism must answer three questions: where does energy come from, where do electrons come from, and where does carbon come from? Combining the answers yields the nutritional grid.

    CategoryEnergy sourceElectron donorCarbon sourceExample organisms
    Photoautotroph (photolithoautotroph)LightInorganic (H2O, H2S, H2)CO2Synechococcus, Prochlorococcus marinus, Chlorobium tepidum (H2S, anoxygenic), diatoms, Chlamydomonas
    PhotoheterotrophLightOrganicOrganic compoundsRhodospirillum rubrum, Rhodopseudomonas palustris, Chloroflexus aurantiacus, Halobacterium salinarum (bacteriorhodopsin)
    ChemolithoautotrophInorganic chemical oxidationInorganic (NH4+, NO2−, H2S, S0, Fe2+, H2)CO2Nitrosomonas europaea (NH3→NO2−), Nitrobacter, Acidithiobacillus ferrooxidans (Fe2+), Beggiatoa, Methanocaldococcus jannaschii (H2 + CO2)
    Chemolithoheterotroph (mixotroph)Inorganic oxidationInorganicOrganicSome Beggiatoa, many Hydrogenophaga
    ChemoorganoheterotrophOrganic chemical oxidationOrganicOrganicE. coli, S. aureus, Saccharomyces cerevisiae, Bacteroides fragilis, virtually all fungi, protozoa, and animals

    Sergei Winogradsky's discovery of chemolithoautotrophy in the 1880s (in Beggiatoa and nitrifying bacteria) was conceptually revolutionary: it showed that life could be built entirely from inorganic chemistry, independent of sunlight — the principle that underpins deep-sea vent and deep-subsurface ecosystems.

    Oxygen relationships

    Oxygen is simultaneously the best terminal electron acceptor available (the O2/H2O couple sits at +0.82 V) and a source of toxic reactive species — superoxide (O2•−), hydrogen peroxide, and hydroxyl radical. An organism's oxygen class reflects whether it can use O2 and whether it possesses superoxide dismutase (SOD), catalase, and peroxidases.

    ClassUses O2?Tolerates O2?Detox enzymesGrowth in thioglycolate tubeExamples
    Obligate aerobeRequiredYesSOD + catalaseTop onlyMycobacterium tuberculosis, Pseudomonas aeruginosa, Micrococcus luteus, Bacillus subtilis
    Facultative anaerobePreferred, not requiredYesSOD + catalaseThroughout, densest at topE. coli, Staphylococcus aureus, Salmonella, Saccharomyces cerevisiae
    Aerotolerant anaerobeNo — ferments onlyYesSOD, usually no catalaseEven throughoutStreptococcus pyogenes, Lactobacillus, Cutibacterium acnes (aerotolerant)
    MicroaerophileRequired, at 2–10%Only at low tensionLimited SOD/catalaseNarrow band below surfaceCampylobacter jejuni, Helicobacter pylori, Borrelia burgdorferi
    Obligate anaerobeNoNo — often killed in minutesAbsent or minimalBottom onlyClostridioides difficile, Clostridium botulinum, Bacteroides fragilis, Methanobrevibacter smithii

    The growth curve and generation time

    A batch culture of bacteria passes through four phases. In lag phase cell number is constant but the cells are intensely active, synthesising ribosomes, enzymes, and repairing damage; its length depends on inoculum age and the difference between old and new media. In log (exponential) phase the population doubles at a constant interval and physiology is at its most reproducible — this is where cells are most susceptible to antibiotics targeting wall synthesis, and the phase used for all quantitative work. In stationary phase division equals death as nutrients deplete and waste accumulates; cells become smaller, tougher, and express the RpoS-dependent general stress regulon, and secondary metabolites (including most antibiotics) are produced. In death (decline) phase viable count declines exponentially, though a resistant subpopulation may persist for months in "long-term stationary phase".

    Growth mathematics is straightforward. If N0 is the starting number, n the number of generations, and N the final number, then N = N0 × 2n. Taking logs, n = (log N − log N0) / log 2 = 3.3 (log N − log N0). The generation time g = t/n.

    Worked example: E. coli at 20 minutes

    Start with a single cell (N0 = 1) doubling every 20 minutes. In 1 hour there are 3 generations → 8 cells. In 10 hours, 30 generations → 230 ≈ 1.07 × 109 cells — about the density of an overnight broth culture. In 24 hours, 72 generations → 272 ≈ 4.7 × 1021 cells, which at ~10−12 g per cell is roughly 4,700 tonnes. In 48 hours the mass would exceed that of the Earth. Real cultures stop at ~109–1010 cells/mL because nutrients run out — exponential growth is always transient. Contrast Mycobacterium tuberculosis at 15–20 hours per generation (hence 6–8 week cultures) and M. leprae at ~14 days, or the deep-subsurface communities with estimated doubling times of centuries to millennia.

    Physical growth ranges

    Every organism has a cardinal triad for each physical variable: a minimum, an optimum, and a maximum. Growth rate rises gradually to the optimum then falls off steeply, because enzyme denaturation is abrupt.

    VariableClassRange / optimumExample organisms
    TemperaturePsychrophile−20 to 20°C; opt. ≤ 15°CPsychrobacter, Colwellia psychrerythraea (growth to −12°C), Polaromonas vacuolata
    Psychrotroph / psychrotolerant0 to 35°C; opt. 20–30°CListeria monocytogenes (grows at 4°C — why refrigeration does not make food safe), Pseudomonas fluorescens
    Mesophile15–45°C; opt. 25–40°CE. coli (37°C), S. aureus, most pathogens and most soil bacteria
    Thermophile45–80°C; opt. 55–65°CThermus aquaticus (source of Taq polymerase, Yellowstone, 1969), Geobacillus stearothermophilus (autoclave biological indicator)
    Hyperthermophile> 80°CPyrococcus furiosus (100°C), Pyrolobus fumarii (113°C), strain 121 (121°C), Methanopyrus kandleri 116 (122°C at 20 MPa — the record)
    pHAcidophileopt. pH < 5.5, some at pH 0Picrophilus torridus (opt. pH 0.7), Acidithiobacillus ferrooxidans, Helicobacter pylori (survives gastric pH via urease)
    NeutrophilepH 5.5–8Most bacteria, including all common pathogens
    Alkaliphileopt. pH > 8.5, up to 12Bacillus alcalophilus, Natronomonas pharaonis (soda lakes)
    Salinity / water activity (aw)Non-halophile< 1% NaCl; aw > 0.98E. coli
    Halotolerant / moderate halophile2–15% NaClStaphylococcus aureus (to 10–15%, basis of mannitol salt agar), Vibrio spp.
    Extreme halophile / xerophile15–30% NaCl; aw to 0.61Halobacterium salinarum, Haloferax, Halococcus; the xerophilic fungus Xeromyces bisporus at aw 0.61
    PressureBarotolerant / piezophile10–70 MPaPhotobacterium profundum SS9, Shewanella benthica
    Obligate piezophile (hyperpiezophile)> 70 MPaColwellia MT41 (Mariana Trench, ~110 MPa), Pyrococcus yayanosii
    RadiationRadioresistantSurvives 5,000–15,000 GyDeinococcus radiodurans — reassembles a shattered genome within hours

    Biofilms and quorum sensing

    The planktonic single cell of the textbook is the exception. In nature perhaps 80–99% of microbial biomass lives in biofilms — surface-attached communities encased in a self-produced extracellular polymeric substance (EPS) of polysaccharides, extracellular DNA, proteins, and lipids, typically 50–90% of the biofilm's dry mass. Development proceeds through reversible attachment, irreversible attachment, microcolony formation, maturation into mushroom-shaped towers threaded by water channels, and finally dispersal. Biofilm cells tolerate antimicrobials at 10–1,000 times the concentration that kills their planktonic counterparts, through restricted penetration, nutrient- and oxygen-limited slow growth, and the presence of dormant persister cells. Clinically this underlies dental plaque, catheter and prosthetic-joint infections, native-valve endocarditis, and the chronic P. aeruginosa colonisation of cystic fibrosis airways; the CDC/NIH estimate that biofilms are involved in 65–80% of human bacterial infections.

    Quorum sensing is the density-dependent signalling that coordinates such group behaviour. Cells secrete a small autoinducer that accumulates in proportion to population density; above a threshold it binds a receptor and switches on genes only worth expressing communally. The founding example is Aliivibrio fischeri, whose LuxI synthase makes the acyl-homoserine lactone 3-oxo-C6-HSL and whose LuxR receptor activates the luxCDABE luciferase operon — producing the light organ of the Hawaiian bobtail squid Euprymna scolopes. The main chemical languages are: acyl-homoserine lactones in Gram-negatives (P. aeruginosa uses the las and rhl circuits plus the PQS quinolone system to control elastase, rhamnolipid, and pyocyanin); modified oligopeptides in Gram-positives (the agr system governing S. aureus toxin production; ComX-driven competence in B. subtilis); and autoinducer-2, a furanosyl borate diester made via LuxS, which functions as an interspecies signal. Quorum quenching — degrading autoinducers with lactonases or acylases, or blocking receptors — is an active anti-virulence drug strategy that avoids the strong selection for resistance that bactericidal antibiotics impose.

    7. How Microbes Are Studied

    Robert Koch's laboratory contributed the two techniques that made bacteriology a science: solid growth media, allowing separation of a mixed sample into discrete colonies, and the pure culture, a population descended from a single cell. Agar — suggested to Walther Hesse by his wife Fanny Angelina Hesse in 1881, from its use in preserving jams — was the key material: a galactose polymer from red algae that melts at 95°C, remains liquid down to about 42°C (allowing organisms to be mixed in), solidifies below that, and is digested by almost no bacterium.

    Medium typeDefinitionReal examplesWhat it achieves
    Defined (synthetic, minimal)Every chemical component and concentration knownM9 minimal salts with glucose; Davis minimal; glucose–salts mediumNutritional requirement studies, auxotroph screening, isotope labelling, reproducible physiology
    Complex (undefined)Contains digests of unknown exact compositionNutrient broth, LB (Luria–Bertani: tryptone, yeast extract, NaCl), trypticase soy agar, brain–heart infusionGeneral cultivation of fastidious and unknown organisms
    SelectiveContains inhibitors that suppress unwanted organismsMacConkey (bile salts + crystal violet → Gram-negatives only); mannitol salt agar (7.5% NaCl → staphylococci); Sabouraud dextrose agar (pH 5.6, high sugar → fungi); Thayer–Martin (VCN antibiotics → Neisseria); XLD and Hektoen for enterics; CCFA for C. difficileRecovers a target organism from a heavily mixed sample such as stool or throat swab
    DifferentialContains an indicator that makes colonies of different organisms look differentBlood agar (α-, β-, γ-haemolysis); EMB agar (E. coli gives green metallic sheen); Hektoen (H2S blackening)Presumptive identification directly from plate appearance
    Selective and differentialBoth at once — the commonest clinical formatMacConkey: bile salts select Gram-negatives, lactose + neutral red differentiates lactose fermenters (pink E. coli) from non-fermenters (colourless Salmonella, Shigella, Pseudomonas)One plate performs isolation and preliminary ID
    EnrichmentLiquid medium whose conditions favour outgrowth of a minority organism before platingSelenite F and tetrathionate broth for Salmonella; alkaline peptone water for Vibrio cholerae; Winogradsky column and nitrogen-free media for diazotrophs (Beijerinck's method)Detects organisms present at very low initial numbers
    Transport / reducingPreserves viability or excludes oxygenCary–Blair, Amies; thioglycolate broth; anaerobic chambers with 5% H2/10% CO2/85% N2Specimen transport; cultivating strict anaerobes

    Pure culture technique. The streak plate dilutes an inoculum mechanically across the agar surface in three or four sectors, flaming or changing the loop between them, until single cells are deposited far enough apart to give isolated colonies. Pour plates and spread plates give countable colonies for enumeration, reported as colony-forming units (CFU) per mL — a deliberately cautious unit acknowledging that a chain, cluster, or clump yields one colony. Standard plate counts use dilutions producing 25–250 colonies per plate for statistical validity. Aseptic technique — flaming loops, working near a Bunsen flame or in a laminar-flow cabinet, autoclaving at 121°C and 15 psi for 15–20 minutes — is the invisible foundation of all of it.

    The great plate count anomaly

    Named by Staley and Konopka in 1985, this is the persistent, enormous discrepancy between the number of cells seen under a microscope in an environmental sample and the number that form colonies on a plate. In seawater, soil, and sediment, plate counts recover roughly 0.001–1% of the direct microscopic count; the widely quoted figure is that ~99% of environmental microbes have never been cultured. The reasons are several: media are far too rich (many oligotrophs are killed by nutrient concentrations that a plate provides in abundance); organisms depend on partners for siderophores, vitamins, or the removal of metabolic end products; growth may be far too slow for a 48-hour incubation; some cells are in a viable-but-nonculturable (VBNC) state; and required signals, electron acceptors, or pressures are absent. Whole phyla — the "candidate phyla radiation", TM7/Saccharibacteria, OP11, and most of the Asgard archaea — are known almost entirely from sequence.

    Reclaiming the unculturable. The iChip (isolation chip; Nichols, Epstein and Lewis, 2010) addresses this directly: a soil sample is diluted so that individual cells occupy each of hundreds of miniature diffusion chambers sealed with semipermeable membranes, and the device is returned to the original soil. Nutrients and growth factors diffuse in from the natural environment while the cells stay separate. Recovery rates rise to 40–50% of cells rather than under 1%, and the method delivered Eleftheria terrae and its novel lipid II-binding antibiotic teixobactin in 2015 — the first genuinely new antibiotic class from soil in decades.

    16S/18S amplicon sequencing

    PCR-amplify a hypervariable region (commonly V3–V4 or V4 with 515F/806R primers; ITS for fungi), sequence the amplicons, cluster into OTUs at 97% identity or resolve exact amplicon sequence variants (ASVs) with DADA2/Deblur, and assign taxonomy against SILVA, Greengenes2, or GTDB. Cheap and deep, but taxonomy-only: it tells you who is there, not what they can do, and is subject to primer bias and rRNA copy-number distortion.

    Shotgun metagenomics

    Sequence all DNA in a sample without PCR targeting, then assemble contigs and bin them by tetranucleotide frequency and coverage into metagenome-assembled genomes (MAGs). Delivers functional gene content, strain-level variation, plasmids, phages, and antibiotic-resistance genes. Landmarks: the Sargasso Sea study (Venter et al., 2004, ~1.2 million new genes), Tara Oceans, and the >250,000 MAGs now in public catalogues.

    Single-cell genomics

    Sort individual cells by flow cytometry or microfluidics, lyse, amplify the genome by multiple displacement amplification with φ29 polymerase, and sequence. Yields single-amplified genomes (SAGs) that link genes unambiguously to one organism — something metagenomic binning can blur — at the cost of uneven coverage and chimera formation.

    MALDI-TOF mass spectrometry

    Matrix-assisted laser desorption/ionisation time-of-flight profiles a colony's abundant ribosomal proteins into a species-specific spectral fingerprint, matched against a reference database. It has transformed clinical labs since roughly 2010: identification in minutes for pennies, replacing days of biochemical testing.

    PCR and qPCR

    Kary Mullis's 1983 invention amplifies a target sequence exponentially using a thermostable polymerase (Taq, from Thermus aquaticus). Quantitative PCR tracks accumulation in real time via SYBR Green or TaqMan probes, giving quantification across seven orders of magnitude; RT-qPCR handles RNA targets and became the global standard for SARS-CoV-2 diagnosis.

    Imaging and activity methods

    FISH and CARD-FISH visualise specific taxa in situ with labelled rRNA probes; stable-isotope probing (13C, 15N) identifies which community members actually consume a substrate; nanoSIMS measures isotope uptake in single cells; metatranscriptomics and metaproteomics report what is expressed rather than merely encoded.

    Together these methods restructured the field. Classical microbiology asked what an isolate could do in a tube; modern microbiology asks what a community does in place, and reserves cultivation for the moment a hypothesis needs testing on a living organism. Both remain essential — a genome predicts metabolism but never proves it, and Koch's insistence on the pure culture still supplies the only decisive experiments.

    Conventions used throughout this encyclopedia: species names in italics with genus abbreviated after first use; temperatures in °C; distances in μm and nm; population densities as CFU/mL for viable counts and cells/mL for direct counts; genome sizes in base pairs (bp), kilobases (kb), or megabases (Mb).

    Bacteria

    Bacteria are single-celled organisms of the domain Bacteria: prokaryotes whose genetic material sits free in the cytoplasm rather than inside a membrane-bound nucleus, whose cell walls are (with a handful of exceptions) built from peptidoglycan, and whose ribosomes sediment at 70S. They are the most numerous cellular organisms on Earth — roughly 1030 individual cells, outweighing all animals combined — and they occupy essentially every habitat where liquid water exists, from 3 km of ocean sediment to the surface of your cornea. They invented photosynthesis, oxygenated the atmosphere, fix the nitrogen that feeds the biosphere, cause plague and cholera and tuberculosis, and ferment cheese, yoghurt, vinegar and soy sauce. This section is the longest in this encyclopedia because bacteria deserve it.

    1. Overview: what a bacterium is

    A bacterium is a prokaryote — literally "before the kernel". The defining negative feature is the absence of a nuclear envelope; the defining positive features are a compact circular (usually) chromosome, 70S ribosomes, a peptidoglycan sacculus, and reproduction by binary fission rather than mitosis. Bacteria are distinguished from the other prokaryotic domain, Archaea, by fundamental chemistry rather than by appearance: bacterial membranes use ester-linked straight-chain fatty acids on sn-glycerol-3-phosphate, archaeal membranes use ether-linked isoprenoid chains on sn-glycerol-1-phosphate; bacteria make peptidoglycan, archaea make pseudomurein, S-layers or nothing; bacterial RNA polymerase has a simple 5-subunit core (α2ββ′ω) while the archaeal enzyme resembles eukaryotic RNA Pol II.

    Size is the first thing to internalize. A typical bacterium is 0.5–5 μm long: Escherichia coli is a rod about 2 μm × 0.5 μm, weighing about 1 picogram wet and containing roughly 2–3 million protein molecules. But the range is enormous. Mycoplasma genitalium is around 0.2–0.3 μm across with a 580 kb genome and 470-odd genes. At the other extreme Thiomargarita magnifica, described from Caribbean mangroves, forms filaments up to 2 cm long — visible to the naked eye and about 5,000 times larger than a "normal" bacterium; Epulopiscium fishelsoni, a surgeonfish gut symbiont, reaches 600 μm and is polyploid with tens of thousands of genome copies.

    Scale anchors: a ribosome is ~20 nm; a flagellar filament ~20 nm thick but up to 15 μm long; the Gram-negative outer membrane and inner membrane are each ~7 nm; a Gram-positive peptidoglycan layer 20–80 nm; a human red blood cell (7 μm) would just about span three E. coli laid end to end.
    Table 1. Bacteria in numbers
    QuantityValueComment
    Total cells on Earth~1030 (4–6 × 1030 prokaryotes)Most live in the deep subsurface and marine sediments, not the surface
    Total prokaryotic carbon~70 gigatonnes CComparable to all plants' carbon in some estimates; ~35× all animals
    Cells in one gram of soil108–1010, thousands of speciesMost never cultured in the laboratory
    Cells per mL of seawater (surface)~106Prochlorococcus alone ~1027 cells globally
    Cells in/on a human body~3.8 × 1013Roughly 1:1 with human cells; ~0.2 kg wet mass, mostly colonic
    Fastest doubling timeVibrio natriegens ~10 min; E. coli ~20 minM. tuberculosis ~15–20 h; M. leprae ~14 days
    Genome size range112 kb (Candidatus Nasuia) to 14.8 Mb (Sorangium cellulosum)E. coli K-12: 4.64 Mb, ~4,300 genes
    Named species vs. estimated~20,000 validly published; estimates 106–101216S rRNA surveys reveal vast uncultured diversity
    Depth/temperature limits≥2.5 km below seafloor; growth to 122 °C (archaeon Methanopyrus)Bacterial record ~100–110 °C (Geothermobacterium, Aquifex ~95 °C)

    Bacteria are also astonishingly old. Stromatolites — laminated carbonate structures built by microbial mats — date to 3.48 billion years ago in the Pilbara of Western Australia, and isotopically light carbon in Greenland rocks hints at life by 3.7 Ga. Cyanobacterial oxygenic photosynthesis triggered the Great Oxidation Event about 2.4 billion years ago, permanently rewriting the planet's chemistry and making eukaryotic aerobic life possible. Both mitochondria (from an alphaproteobacterium related to Rickettsia) and chloroplasts (from a cyanobacterium) are domesticated bacteria living inside every plant and animal reading this page.

    Prokaryote is a shape of organization, not a clade

    "Prokaryote" groups Bacteria and Archaea by what they lack. Molecular phylogeny shows Archaea are actually closer relatives of eukaryotes than of bacteria — eukaryotes appear to have arisen from within the Asgard archaea. The word remains useful for cell architecture, but it is not a taxonomic rank.

    2. Morphology and arrangement

    Bacterial shape is genetically determined, heritable, and maintained by the peptidoglycan sacculus together with cytoskeletal proteins: MreB (an actin homolog) directs the lateral wall synthesis that makes a rod a rod, FtsZ (a tubulin homolog) forms the division ring, CreS (crescentin, an intermediate-filament homolog) bends Caulobacter crescentus into its comma. Delete mreB from a rod and it rounds up into a coccus. Shape matters ecologically: it governs surface-to-volume ratio and nutrient uptake, motility through viscous media, sedimentation, attachment, and susceptibility to protozoan grazing.

    Table 2. Bacterial cell shapes
    ShapeDescriptionRepresentative species
    CoccusSpherical or ovoid, 0.5–1.5 μmStaphylococcus aureus, Streptococcus pneumoniae (lancet-shaped diplococcus), Neisseria gonorrhoeae (kidney-bean diplococcus)
    Bacillus (rod)Cylindrical with rounded or squared endsEscherichia coli, Bacillus anthracis (square ends, "boxcar"), Salmonella enterica
    CoccobacillusVery short plump rod, easily mistaken for a coccusHaemophilus influenzae, Acinetobacter baumannii, Bordetella pertussis, Brucella
    VibrioCurved rod, comma-shaped, less than one full turnVibrio cholerae, Campylobacter jejuni (gull-wing when paired), Caulobacter crescentus
    SpirillumRigid helix; motility by external flagellaSpirillum volutans, Helicobacter pylori (helical), Rhodospirillum rubrum
    SpirocheteFlexible helix with internal periplasmic flagella (axial filaments); corkscrew motilityTreponema pallidum, Borrelia burgdorferi, Leptospira interrogans (hooked ends)
    Filamentous / mycelialLong branching threads, sometimes with aerial hyphae and sporesStreptomyces coelicolor, Nocardia, Beggiatoa, Sphaerotilus natans
    PleomorphicNo fixed shape; variable in the same cultureMycoplasma pneumoniae (no wall), Corynebacterium diphtheriae (club-shaped, variable), Rickettsia
    Stalked / appendaged (prosthecate)Cytoplasmic extension used for attachment or surface areaCaulobacter (holdfast stalk), Hyphomicrobium (budding hypha)
    Star-shapedFlat six-rayed starStella humosa, Stella vacuolata
    Square / flat platesPostage-stamp squares ~2 μm across, only ~0.2 μm thick, gas-vesicle filledHaloquadratum walsbyi (an archaeon, but the classic example of geometric prokaryotes)
    Trichome / multicellular filamentChains of cells sharing an outer envelope, sometimes differentiatedAnabaena (heterocysts + akinetes), Oscillatoria, Nostoc

    Arrangement follows from the plane of division and whether daughter cells separate. Cocci that divide in one plane and stay attached form pairs (diplococci) or chains (streptococci); division in random planes gives grape-like clusters (staphylococci); two perpendicular planes give tetrads; three perpendicular planes give cubical packets of eight (sarcinae). Rods may form chains (streptobacilli), side-by-side palisades, or the sharp-angled V and L forms characteristic of snapping division.

    Table 3. Cell arrangements and their diagnostic value
    ArrangementHow it formsClassic exampleClinical cue
    DiplococciOne division plane, cells remain pairedNeisseria meningitidis, Streptococcus pneumoniaeGram-negative diplococci in CSF neutrophils → meningococcus
    Streptococci (chains)One plane, repeated, no separationStreptococcus pyogenes, Enterococcus faecalisChains of Gram-positive cocci in blood culture
    Staphylococci (clusters)Multiple random planesStaphylococcus aureusGrape-like clusters, catalase-positive, coagulase-positive
    TetradsTwo perpendicular planesMicrococcus luteus, AerococcusCommon skin/air contaminant
    Sarcinae (packets of 8)Three perpendicular planesSarcina ventriculiGastric contents in delayed emptying
    Streptobacilli (chains of rods)Rods dividing transversely, not separatingBacillus anthracis ("bamboo" chains)Long boxcar chains in blood → anthrax
    Palisades / V and L forms"Snapping division": outer wall layer holds while inner splitsCorynebacterium diphtheriae, Listeria"Chinese-letter" morphology on Loeffler's medium
    Rosettes / starsCells adhering by holdfast or stalkStella, Agrobacterium, Caulobacter rosettesEnvironmental

    3. Cell structure in detail

    3.1 Cytoplasm, nucleoid, plasmids and ribosomes

    The bacterial cytoplasm is a crowded gel — roughly 300–400 mg/mL of macromolecule, so concentrated that diffusion of large proteins is markedly slowed. It contains no membrane-bound organelles in the eukaryotic sense (with intriguing exceptions in Planctomycetota and magnetotactic bacteria), but it is far from unstructured: cytoskeletal filaments of MreB, FtsZ, ParA/ParM and bactofilins organize the cell, and proteinaceous microcompartments compartmentalize chemistry.

    The nucleoid is the irregular region occupying about a quarter of the cell volume where the chromosome sits. The typical bacterial chromosome is a single covalently closed circular double-stranded DNA molecule — E. coli K-12's is 4,641,652 bp, about 1.6 mm of contour length packed into a 2 μm cell, a ~1000-fold compaction. This is achieved by supercoiling (DNA gyrase introduces negative supercoils; topoisomerase I relaxes them) and by nucleoid-associated proteins HU, H-NS, Fis and IHF that bend and bridge DNA into ~10–100 kb topological domains. Not all chromosomes are circular or single: Borrelia burgdorferi has a linear chromosome plus more than 20 linear and circular plasmids; Vibrio cholerae has two circular chromosomes; Streptomyces has a linear chromosome with terminal proteins.

    Plasmids are small, independently replicating extrachromosomal DNA molecules, usually circular, from about 1 kb to over 200 kb (megaplasmids larger still). They are typically dispensable for growth but carry accessory functions: antibiotic resistance (R plasmids), virulence (the Bacillus anthracis pXO1 toxin and pXO2 capsule plasmids; the Shigella and Yersinia virulence plasmids), bacteriocin production (Col plasmids), metabolic capabilities (TOL plasmid for toluene degradation in Pseudomonas putida), and conjugative transfer machinery (F plasmid, Ti plasmid of Agrobacterium tumefaciens). Copy number ranges from 1–2 (large, stringent) to hundreds (small, relaxed, e.g. ColE1 derivatives used as cloning vectors).

    Ribosomes are 70S particles, roughly 20 nm across, each built from a 30S small subunit (16S rRNA of 1,542 nucleotides plus 21 proteins) and a 50S large subunit (23S rRNA of 2,904 nt, 5S rRNA of 120 nt, and ~33 proteins). A rapidly growing E. coli holds 20,000–70,000 ribosomes, up to a third of its dry mass. The 16S rRNA gene is the universal phylogenetic marker of microbiology, used since Carl Woese's work in the 1970s to define the three domains and still the backbone of bacterial taxonomy and microbiome surveys. Ribosomal differences from the eukaryotic 80S ribosome are exploited by whole antibiotic classes: aminoglycosides and tetracyclines bind the 30S; macrolides, chloramphenicol, clindamycin, linezolid and streptogramins bind the 50S.

    Table 4. Cytoplasmic inclusion bodies and microcompartments
    InclusionCompositionFunctionFound in
    PHB / PHA granulesPoly-β-hydroxybutyrate and related polyesters, lipid-monolayer boundedCarbon and energy storage; basis of biodegradable bioplasticsCupriavidus necator (up to 80% dry weight), Azotobacter, Pseudomonas
    Glycogen granulesα-1,4-glucan polymerCarbon storage, rapidly mobilizedE. coli, many enterics
    Volutin / metachromatic granulesLinear polyphosphatePhosphate and energy reserve; stains red with methylene blueCorynebacterium diphtheriae (Babes–Ernst granules), Yersinia, Spirillum volutans
    Sulfur globulesElemental S° in periplasm or cytoplasmElectron reservoir from H2S oxidation, later oxidized to sulfateBeggiatoa, Thiomargarita, purple sulfur bacteria (Chromatium)
    MagnetosomesMembrane-bounded crystals of magnetite (Fe3O4) or greigite, 35–120 nm, in chainsMagnetotaxis: align to geomagnetic field to find preferred oxygen levelsMagnetospirillum magneticum, Magnetococcus marinus
    Gas vesiclesProtein shells (GvpA), gas-permeable, water-impermeable, conical endsBuoyancy control in the water columnMicrocystis, Anabaena, Planktothrix
    CarboxysomesIcosahedral protein shell (~100 nm) enclosing RuBisCO and carbonic anhydraseCO2-concentrating mechanism; raises local CO2 to suppress photorespirationCyanobacteria, many chemoautotrophs (Halothiobacillus, Nitrosomonas)
    Other BMCs (Pdu, Eut)Shell proteins enclosing propanediol- or ethanolamine-utilization enzymesSequester toxic aldehyde intermediatesSalmonella, Listeria
    AnammoxosomeLadderane-lipid membrane-bound organelleAnaerobic ammonium oxidation; contains hydrazine intermediatesBrocadia, Kuenenia (Planctomycetota)
    ChlorosomesLarge lipid-monolayer light-harvesting antennae with bacteriochlorophyll c/d/ePhotosynthesis at extremely low light (down to ~1 photon/chlorophyll/8 h)Green sulfur bacteria (Chlorobium), Chloroflexus

    3.2 The cell envelope and peptidoglycan chemistry

    Almost all bacteria are encased in a peptidoglycan (murein) sacculus — one enormous covalently closed bag-shaped molecule that resists an internal turgor pressure of roughly 3 atmospheres in Gram-negatives and up to 25 atmospheres in Gram-positives. Its backbone is an alternating β-(1→4)-linked chain of N-acetylglucosamine (NAG/GlcNAc) and N-acetylmuramic acid (NAM/MurNAc). Each NAM carries a stem peptide, classically L-alanine – D-glutamate – meso-diaminopimelate (in most Gram-negatives and bacilli) or L-lysine (in most Gram-positive cocci) – D-alanine – D-alanine.

    Crosslinking is performed by transpeptidases (penicillin-binding proteins, PBPs), which cleave the terminal D-Ala–D-Ala bond of one stem and use the released energy to form a peptide bond to the diamino acid of a neighbouring stem — directly in Gram-negatives, or through a pentaglycine bridge in Staphylococcus aureus. β-Lactam antibiotics (penicillins, cephalosporins, carbapenems, monobactams) are structural mimics of D-Ala–D-Ala; they acylate the PBP active-site serine irreversibly. Glycopeptides (vancomycin, teicoplanin) act one step earlier, binding the D-Ala–D-Ala terminus itself and sterically blocking both transglycosylation and transpeptidation. Vancomycin resistance in Enterococcus (VanA/VanB) works by remodelling the terminus to D-Ala–D-lactate, a swap that costs 1000-fold binding affinity. Methicillin resistance in MRSA works differently: acquisition of mecA on the SCCmec cassette encodes PBP2a, a transpeptidase with low affinity for essentially all β-lactams. Lysozyme, abundant in tears, saliva and egg white, hydrolyses the β-1,4 bond between NAM and NAG.

    The Gram stain, devised by Hans Christian Gram in 1884, remains the single most informative rapid test in bacteriology. Cells are stained with crystal violet, fixed with iodine (forming a large crystal violet–iodine complex), decolorized with alcohol or acetone, and counterstained with safranin. Gram-positive cells retain the purple complex because their thick, highly crosslinked, dehydration-shrunken peptidoglycan traps it; Gram-negative cells lose it because alcohol dissolves the outer membrane's lipid and the thin peptidoglycan cannot retain the dye, so they take up the pink counterstain.

    Table 5. Cell envelope architectures compared
    FeatureGram-positiveGram-negativeAcid-fast (mycobacterial)Mycoplasma (wall-less)
    PeptidoglycanThick, 20–80 nm, 20–40 layers, ~90% of wall massThin, 2–7 nm, 1–3 layers, ~10% of wall massThin PG, covalently linked to arabinogalactanAbsent entirely
    Outer membraneAbsentPresent: asymmetric bilayer, LPS outer leaflet, phospholipid inner leafletFunctional "outer membrane" of mycolic acids (mycomembrane)Absent
    PeriplasmMinimal/absent (thin periplasmic space described)Prominent gel-filled compartment with binding proteins, β-lactamases, chaperonesPresentNone
    Teichoic acidsWall teichoic acid (linked to NAM) and lipoteichoic acid (anchored in membrane); ribitol- or glycerol-phosphate polymersAbsentAbsentAbsent
    Distinctive lipids—LPS (endotoxin): lipid A + core oligosaccharide + O-antigenMycolic acids (C60–C90), cord factor (trehalose dimycolate), LAM, sulfolipidsSterols in membrane (acquired from host)
    Porins / transportWall is porous to ~30–50 kDaOmpF/OmpC/PhoE trimeric β-barrels; specific channels (LamB); TonB-dependent receptors for ironVery low permeability porins; wall is a major drug barrierMembrane transporters only
    SensitivityPenicillin G, lysozyme, vancomycinResistant to many large/hydrophobic drugs; endotoxic shock on lysisResistant to most standard antibiotics; requires isoniazid, rifampicin, etc.Intrinsically resistant to all β-lactams and vancomycin
    ExamplesStaphylococcus, Streptococcus, Bacillus, Clostridium, ListeriaE. coli, Pseudomonas, Neisseria, Salmonella, VibrioMycobacterium tuberculosis, M. leprae, Nocardia (partially)Mycoplasma pneumoniae, Ureaplasma

    Lipopolysaccharide deserves separate attention because it is the archetypal endotoxin. Its three parts are: lipid A, a diglucosamine backbone bearing 4–7 fatty acyl chains, anchoring LPS in the membrane and responsible for essentially all toxicity; the core oligosaccharide, containing the unusual sugars KDO (2-keto-3-deoxyoctonate) and heptose; and the O-antigen, a long repeating polysaccharide, highly variable, that defines serotypes (the "O157" in E. coli O157:H7). Lipid A is recognized by the host TLR4/MD-2 receptor complex at picogram concentrations, triggering TNF-α, IL-1 and IL-6 release; in excess this produces fever, hypotension, disseminated intravascular coagulation and septic shock. Unlike protein exotoxins, endotoxin is heat-stable (survives autoclaving), is not convertible into a toxoid, and is released mainly on cell lysis — which is why bacteriolytic antibiotics can transiently worsen Gram-negative sepsis (a Jarisch–Herxheimer-like effect). Braun's lipoprotein, the most abundant protein in E. coli by molecule count (~7×105 copies), covalently tethers the outer membrane to the peptidoglycan.

    Acid-fastness arises from the mycobacterial envelope's extraordinary lipid content (up to 60% of wall dry weight). Peptidoglycan is covalently linked through a phosphodiester to arabinogalactan, whose arabinose termini are esterified with mycolic acids — α-branched, β-hydroxy fatty acids of 60–90 carbons. These pack into a quasi-crystalline pseudo-bilayer of astonishingly low fluidity and permeability. Once carbolfuchsin has been driven in with heat (Ziehl–Neelsen) or a detergent (Kinyoun), acid-alcohol cannot remove it. Trehalose dimycolate ("cord factor") causes the serpentine cord growth of virulent M. tuberculosis and is itself immunostimulatory; lipoarabinomannan modulates macrophage signalling.

    S-layers are paracrystalline two-dimensional arrays of a single protein or glycoprotein species, self-assembling into oblique, square or hexagonal lattices outside the wall. They act as molecular sieves, protective coats, adhesins and virulence factors — the Bacillus anthracis S-layer proteins Sap and EA1, and the Campylobacter fetus S-layer that blocks complement C3b binding, are examples. Capsules are well-organized, firmly attached polysaccharide (occasionally polypeptide) layers; slime layers are loose and easily shed; the general term for all extracellular polymer is the glycocalyx. Capsules resist phagocytosis by preventing opsonin deposition and receptor engagement, which is why the major encapsulated pathogens — S. pneumoniae (>100 capsular serotypes), H. influenzae type b (polyribosylribitol phosphate), N. meningitidis, Klebsiella pneumoniae, group B Streptococcus, Salmonella Typhi (Vi antigen) — are precisely the ones targeted by conjugate vaccines and are the pathogens that devastate asplenic patients. B. anthracis is unusual in having a poly-D-glutamic acid capsule, which is poorly immunogenic because D-amino acid polymers resist proteolysis. Capsules also mediate biofilm formation and attachment: Streptococcus mutans converts dietary sucrose into extracellular glucans that glue it to enamel.

    Biofilms

    Most bacteria in nature live not as planktonic cells but in biofilms — surface-attached communities embedded in a self-produced matrix of exopolysaccharide, extracellular DNA and protein. Biofilm cells can be 100–1000 × less susceptible to antibiotics than the same strain in broth, due to reduced penetration, slow growth, and persister cells. Clinically this drives catheter infections, prosthetic joint infection (Staphylococcus epidermidis), Pseudomonas aeruginosa in cystic fibrosis airways, and dental plaque. Formation is coordinated by quorum sensing: acyl-homoserine lactones in Gram-negatives (LuxI/LuxR, as first described in Aliivibrio fischeri bioluminescence), oligopeptides in Gram-positives (Agr in S. aureus), and AI-2 as an interspecies signal.

    3.3 Flagella, motility and chemotaxis

    The bacterial flagellum is a rotary nanomachine, not a whip. It has three parts: the filament, a hollow helical tube 20 nm wide and 5–20 μm long, self-assembled from ~20,000 copies of flagellin (FliC) that are exported through the 2 nm central channel and add at the distal tip under a cap protein (FliD); the hook, a flexible universal joint of FlgE; and the basal body, the motor embedded in the envelope. In Gram-negatives the basal body has four rings — L (in the LPS outer membrane), P (peptidoglycan), MS (membrane/supramembrane) and C (cytoplasmic, the switch complex FliG/FliM/FliN) — while Gram-positives, lacking an outer membrane, have only the MS and C rings.

    Torque is generated by Mot stator complexes (MotA5MotB2 in E. coli) anchored to peptidoglycan, which conduct protons down the proton motive force and push against FliG in the rotor. Roughly 1,200 protons per revolution drive rotation at 100–300 Hz in E. coli and up to ~1,700 Hz in the sodium-driven polar flagellum of Vibrio alginolyticus. Swimming speeds are 20–60 μm/s — tens of body lengths per second. The flagellum is entirely powered by ion flux, not ATP, and can reverse direction within a millisecond. Flagellin is a potent PAMP recognized by host TLR5 and by the NAIP5/NLRC4 inflammasome, so many pathogens (e.g. Helicobacter, Campylobacter) modify their flagellins to evade detection.

    Table 6. Flagellar arrangements and alternative motility
    TypeArrangementExample
    Monotrichous (polar)Single flagellum at one poleVibrio cholerae, Pseudomonas aeruginosa, Caulobacter crescentus
    LophotrichousTuft of several flagella at one poleHelicobacter pylori (2–6 sheathed), Spirillum, Pseudomonas fluorescens
    AmphitrichousFlagella at both poles (single or tufts)Alcaligenes faecalis, Campylobacter jejuni (bipolar single)
    PeritrichousFlagella distributed over the whole surface (5–10+)Escherichia coli, Salmonella Typhimurium, Proteus mirabilis (swarming), Bacillus subtilis
    AtrichousNo flagellaKlebsiella pneumoniae, Shigella, most cocci
    Axial filaments (endoflagella)Flagella confined within the periplasm, wrapped round the protoplasmic cylinder; rotation flexes the whole cellSpirochetes: Treponema pallidum, Borrelia burgdorferi, Leptospira — efficient corkscrewing through viscous media such as mucus and connective tissue
    Twitching motilityType IV pilus extends, attaches, retracts (PilT ATPase) — a grappling hook generating >100 pNPseudomonas aeruginosa, Neisseria gonorrhoeae, Myxococcus xanthus
    Gliding motilitySurface adhesin complexes moved by a helical track (Agl/Glt) or focal adhesions; no flagellaMyxococcus xanthus, Flavobacterium johnsoniae, Cytophaga
    SwarmingCoordinated multicellular surface migration by hyperflagellated elongated swarmer cellsProteus mirabilis (bull's-eye rings on agar), Serratia, Bacillus subtilis

    Chemotaxis is the best-understood signal transduction system in biology. E. coli alternates runs (counterclockwise flagellar rotation, bundling the peritrichous flagella into a coherent left-handed helix, ~1 s, ~30 μm) with tumbles (clockwise rotation, the bundle flies apart, ~0.1 s, random reorientation). The cell cannot sense a spatial gradient across its own 2 μm length; instead it performs a temporal comparison: if attractant concentration is rising, tumbling is suppressed and runs lengthen, producing a biased random walk up the gradient. Methyl-accepting chemotaxis proteins (MCPs: Tar for aspartate/maltose, Tsr for serine, Trg for ribose/galactose, Tap for dipeptides) cluster in a hexagonal array at the cell pole with the histidine kinase CheA and the coupling protein CheW. Attractant binding inhibits CheA autophosphorylation, so less phospho-CheY reaches FliM, so the motor stays counterclockwise. CheZ dephosphorylates CheY to reset the system; CheR (methyltransferase) and CheB (methylesterase) provide the slow feedback that gives near-perfect adaptation, letting the cell respond to relative rather than absolute changes over five orders of magnitude of concentration. Related behaviours include phototaxis, aerotaxis (via the Aer receptor sensing redox), magnetotaxis, and energy taxis.

    3.4 Pili and fimbriae

    Fimbriae are short (0.5–2 μm), numerous (up to 1,000 per cell), thin protein filaments used for adhesion. The archetype is E. coli type 1 fimbriae, assembled by the chaperone–usher pathway and tipped with the mannose-binding adhesin FimH; these mediate binding to uroplakin on bladder epithelium in uropathogenic E. coli, and the binding is a "catch bond" that strengthens under shear from urine flow. P fimbriae (PapG adhesin) bind globoside glycolipids on kidney cells and are associated with pyelonephritis.

    Type IV pili are functionally different: polar, retractile, built from pilin subunits assembled and disassembled by dedicated ATPases (PilB for extension, PilT for retraction). They mediate twitching motility, natural DNA uptake (competence), microcolony formation, and phage attachment. In Neisseria gonorrhoeae the pilin gene undergoes antigenic variation through recombination with silent pilS cassettes, producing millions of variants and defeating protective immunity — a major reason there is still no gonococcal vaccine.

    The sex pilus (F pilus, conjugative pilus) is a long, hollow, dynamically extending and retracting tube encoded by the tra operon of a conjugative plasmid. It contacts a recipient cell, retracts to draw the cells together, and a mating pair stabilization complex forms; a single strand of the plasmid is then nicked at oriT by relaxase and transferred 5′-first through a type IV secretion system, with rolling-circle replication in both cells. F-specific bacteriophages (M13, filamentous; MS2, an RNA phage) adsorb to the F pilus and are used in the laboratory to detect its presence.

    3.5 Endospores

    A handful of Gram-positive lineages — principally Bacillus, Clostridium, Clostridioides, Geobacillus, Sporosarcina, Paenibacillus and the filamentous Thermoactinomyces — respond to nutrient starvation by making endospores: dormant, cryptobiotic, single-copy survival structures with no measurable metabolism. Sporulation in Bacillus subtilis is a 6–8-hour developmental program governed by the phosphorelay to Spo0A and a cascade of compartment-specific sigma factors (σF and σG in the forespore, σE and σK in the mother cell), traditionally divided into seven stages.

    Table 7. Stages of sporulation and spore anatomy
    Stage / layerEvent or compositionSignificance
    Stage 0–IStarvation sensed; Spo0A~P accumulates; chromosome replicated and stretched into an axial filamentCommitment point; irreversible once passed
    Stage IIAsymmetric septation near one pole; forespore (~1/4 volume) and mother cell formed; DNA translocated by SpoIIIETwo cells with identical genomes but divergent fates
    Stage IIIEngulfment: mother-cell membrane migrates around forespore, pinching it off as a free protoplast within the mother cellForespore now has two surrounding membranes
    Stage IVCortex synthesis between the membranes: modified peptidoglycan with muramic-δ-lactam and low crosslinkingEnables dehydration of the core; essential for heat resistance
    Stage VCoat deposition: ≥70 proteins in layered, cross-linked (dityrosine) basement, inner and outer coatsChemical and enzymatic resistance; lysozyme exclusion
    Stage VI–VIIMaturation, then mother-cell lysis releasing the sporeFree spore released to environment
    CoreCytoplasm with DNA, ribosomes, enzymes; only 10–30% water (vegetative cell ~80%); pH ~1 unit lowerDehydration is the primary basis of wet-heat resistance
    DPA–Ca2+Dipicolinic acid chelated with calcium; 5–15% of spore dry weightDisplaces water, stabilizes DNA, contributes to heat resistance; released within seconds of germination
    SASPsSmall acid-soluble proteins (α/β type) saturating the DNA, changing it to an A-like conformationProtect against UV, desiccation, dry heat and chemicals; degraded on germination as an amino acid source
    Inner membraneExtremely low permeability; lipids largely immobileBlocks entry of damaging chemicals; site of germinant receptors (GerA)
    ExosporiumLoose outermost balloon-like layer of glycoprotein (BclA hairs) — present in B. anthracis and B. cereus, absent in B. subtilisMediates interaction with macrophages and the environment

    Resistance is spectacular: endospores survive boiling for hours (hence autoclaving at 121 °C, 15 psi for 15–20 min, verified with Geobacillus stearothermophilus spore strips), UV doses 10–50 × lethal for vegetative cells, desiccation, ionizing radiation, organic solvents, and disinfectants including alcohol — which is why alcohol hand gel does not kill Clostridioides difficile and soap-and-water washing plus sporicidal bleach is required. Viable spores have been recovered from 25–40-million-year-old amber-entombed bees and, controversially, from 250-million-year-old salt crystals. Germination is triggered by specific germinants (L-alanine, or the AGFK mixture of asparagine/glucose/fructose/K+ for B. subtilis; bile salts such as taurocholate for C. difficile) binding inner-membrane receptors, followed by DPA release, cortex hydrolysis by SleB/CwlJ, rehydration, and outgrowth — the whole process taking minutes.

    Spore position and shape are taxonomically useful: central non-bulging in B. anthracis, subterminal in Clostridium perfringens (rarely seen), terminal and bulging like a drumstick or tennis racket in Clostridium tetani, oval subterminal in C. botulinum. Note that endospores differ fundamentally from the reproductive exospores of Streptomyces or fungal spores: one cell makes exactly one endospore, so it is survival, not multiplication.

    4. Metabolism, growth and genetics

    4.1 Nutritional and energetic strategies

    Bacteria are classified by carbon source (autotroph = CO2; heterotroph = organic), energy source (phototroph = light; chemotroph = chemical), and electron donor (lithotroph = inorganic; organotroph = organic). Combining these gives the four principal lifestyles: photoautotrophy (cyanobacteria), photoheterotrophy (Rhodopseudomonas, purple non-sulfur bacteria), chemolithoautotrophy (Nitrosomonas, Thiobacillus, Acidithiobacillus), and chemoorganoheterotrophy (most bacteria, including nearly all pathogens). Bacteria collectively exploit a far wider redox range than eukaryotes: essentially any pair of half-reactions with a sufficient potential difference has been recruited by some lineage.

    Aerobic respiration oxidizes substrates through glycolysis (Embden–Meyerhof, or the Entner–Doudoroff pathway favoured by Pseudomonas and Zymomonas, or the pentose phosphate pathway), the TCA cycle, and an electron transport chain terminating in O2. Bacterial chains are modular and branched: E. coli has two terminal oxidases (cytochrome bo3 at high oxygen, high-affinity cytochrome bd-I at low oxygen) and multiple dehydrogenases, allowing rapid re-wiring. Oxygen relationships define handling in the laboratory.

    Table 8. Oxygen relationships
    ClassBehaviourEnzymes presentExamples
    Obligate aerobeRequires O2 as terminal acceptorCatalase, superoxide dismutase (SOD)Mycobacterium tuberculosis, Pseudomonas aeruginosa, Bacillus, Nocardia
    Facultative anaerobeRespires O2 if available, otherwise ferments or respires anaerobicallyCatalase, SODE. coli and other Enterobacterales, Staphylococcus, Listeria, Vibrio
    Obligate anaerobeKilled by O2; uses fermentation or anaerobic respirationUsually lacks catalase and SODClostridium, Bacteroides fragilis, Fusobacterium, Actinomyces
    Aerotolerant anaerobeCannot use O2 but tolerates itSOD, peroxidase; no catalaseStreptococcus pyogenes, Lactobacillus, Cutibacterium acnes (aerotolerant)
    MicroaerophileNeeds O2 at reduced tension (2–10%), often elevated CO2Limited detox capacityCampylobacter jejuni, Helicobacter pylori, Borrelia burgdorferi
    CapnophileRequires elevated CO2 (5–10%)—Neisseria gonorrhoeae, Haemophilus influenzae, Brucella abortus

    4.2 Anaerobic respiration and fermentation

    When oxygen is unavailable, many bacteria respire with alternative terminal electron acceptors, still generating a proton motive force and ATP by oxidative phosphorylation. The acceptors are used in a predictable thermodynamic order — the "redox tower" — which explains the vertical zonation of lake sediments and the sequential redox zones of a landfill plume: O2 → NO3− → Mn(IV) → Fe(III) → SO42− → CO2.

    Table 9. Anaerobic respiration: terminal electron acceptors
    AcceptorProduct(s)ProcessRepresentative genera
    NO3−NO2−, NO, N2O, N2Denitrification / nitrate respirationParacoccus denitrificans, Pseudomonas stutzeri, E. coli (nitrate→nitrite only)
    NO3−NH4+DNRA (dissimilatory nitrate reduction to ammonium) — retains N in the systemWolinella succinogenes, Desulfovibrio, Campylobacter
    SO42−H2SSulfate reduction; blackens sediments (FeS), sours oil wells, corrodes steelDesulfovibrio vulgaris, Desulfobacter, Archaeoglobus (archaeal)
    Fe(III)Fe(II) (magnetite, siderite)Iron reduction; used in uranium bioremediation and microbial fuel cellsGeobacter sulfurreducens (conductive nanowires), Shewanella oneidensis

    Fermentation, by contrast, uses an internal organic molecule as electron acceptor, generates ATP only by substrate-level phosphorylation, and exists fundamentally to regenerate NAD+. The characteristic end products of each fermentation pathway are the basis of many classical identification tests and of much of the food industry.

    PropionicPropionate + acetate + CO2Propionibacterium freudenreichii, CutibacteriumSwiss/Emmental cheese: CO2 makes the eyes, propionate the nutty flavour
