    4.3 Bacteria and the elemental cycles

    Nitrogen fixation reduces atmospheric N2 to ammonia and is performed only by prokaryotes. The enzyme, nitrogenase, is a two-component metalloenzyme (Fe protein + MoFe protein) with the overall stoichiometry N2 + 8H+ + 8e− + 16 ATP → 2 NH3 + H2 + 16 ADP + 16 Pi — extravagantly expensive, and irreversibly poisoned by O2. Different fixers solve the oxygen problem differently: Rhizobium, Bradyrhizobium and Sinorhizobium live inside legume root nodules where plant leghemoglobin buffers free O2 to nanomolar levels; Frankia forms nodules on alders and other actinorhizal plants and encloses nitrogenase in thick-walled vesicles; Azotobacter vinelandii is free-living and aerobic, protecting nitrogenase by an extremely high respiratory rate and conformational protection; filamentous cyanobacteria such as Anabaena differentiate specialized non-photosynthetic heterocysts (about 1 in every 10–20 cells) that lack photosystem II, have thickened glycolipid envelopes, and exchange fixed nitrogen for carbon with neighbours. Biological fixation supplies roughly 130 Tg N per year, comparable to the industrial Haber–Bosch process, which by contrast runs at 400–500 °C and 200 atm.

    Fixed nitrogen then cycles through nitrification — a two-step chemolithoautotrophic oxidation, NH3 → NO2− by Nitrosomonas/Nitrosospira (ammonia monooxygenase), then NO2− → NO3− by Nitrobacter/Nitrospira; some Nitrospira perform both steps ("comammox"). Nitrification is the reason ammonium fertilizer leaches as nitrate, and it is deliberately harnessed in the aeration basins of sewage works and in aquarium filters. Denitrification returns N2 to the atmosphere and closes the cycle, while anammox bacteria (Brocadia, Kuenenia) combine ammonium and nitrite directly to N2 using hydrazine as an intermediate, accounting for a large share of oceanic nitrogen loss and now used in low-energy wastewater treatment.

    In the sulfur cycle, sulfate reducers make H2S and sulfur oxidizers reverse it: Beggiatoa and Thiothrix deposit intracellular S° at oxic–anoxic interfaces; Acidithiobacillus ferrooxidans oxidizes both sulfur and Fe(II) at pH 2, generating sulfuric acid — the cause of acid mine drainage and the basis of commercial bioleaching of copper and gold ores. In the carbon cycle, methanotrophs (Methylococcus capsulatus, Methylosinus trichosporium, and the acidophilic verrucomicrobial Methylacidiphilum) oxidize methane using particulate or soluble methane monooxygenase, consuming a large fraction of the methane produced in soils and sediments before it reaches the atmosphere; sMMO's broad specificity also co-oxidizes trichloroethylene, exploited in bioremediation.

    4.4 Growth and binary fission

    Bacteria reproduce by binary fission: chromosome replication initiates bidirectionally at oriC (controlled by DnaA), the two copies are segregated (ParABS systems, or in E. coli largely by entropic and replication-driven mechanisms), FtsZ polymerizes into the Z-ring at midcell — positioned by the oscillating Min system and by nucleoid occlusion — and recruits the divisome to build the septum. In rich medium E. coli divides every 20 minutes even though a full replication round takes ~40 minutes, achieved by multifork replication: new rounds initiate before the previous ones finish. A batch culture passes through lag, exponential (log), stationary and death phases; in stationary phase the alternative sigma factor RpoS (σS) remodels the transcriptome for stress survival. Growth in continuous culture (chemostat) instead holds cells at a fixed sub-maximal rate set by the dilution rate. A single cell dividing every 20 minutes would, unchecked, yield ~1021 cells in 24 hours — roughly 4,000 tonnes; nutrient limitation, waste accumulation and predation ensure it never happens.

    4.5 Gene regulation: the operon

    Bacterial genes are frequently organized into operons — sets of genes transcribed from one promoter into a single polycistronic mRNA. Two classics illustrate the logic.

    The lac operon of E. coli (Jacob and Monod, 1961) is an inducible catabolic operon containing lacZ (β-galactosidase), lacY (lactose permease) and lacA (transacetylase). In the absence of lactose the LacI repressor, encoded by a separate gene, binds the operator and blocks transcription. Lactose taken up is isomerized to allolactose, which binds LacI, changes its conformation and releases it — negative control by induction. Superimposed is positive control: the catabolite activator protein CAP (CRP), bound to cyclic AMP, must bind upstream to recruit RNA polymerase to the weak lac promoter. Glucose lowers cAMP, so CAP–cAMP is scarce and the operon stays largely off even with lactose present — catabolite repression, giving the diauxic growth curve in which E. coli consumes all the glucose before touching the lactose. IPTG, a non-hydrolysable inducer, and the chromogenic substrate X-gal underpin blue/white cloning screens worldwide.

    The trp operon is a repressible anabolic operon of five structural genes for tryptophan biosynthesis. Tryptophan itself acts as a corepressor: TrpR alone cannot bind DNA, but the TrpR–tryptophan complex binds the operator and shuts the operon down when tryptophan is plentiful. A second, finer layer is attenuation: the leader transcript contains two tryptophan codons in tandem and can fold into alternative stem-loops. Because transcription and translation are coupled in bacteria, a ribosome that stalls at those codons (low Trp) shifts the folding to an antiterminator, and transcription proceeds; if tryptophan is abundant the ribosome runs on, a terminator hairpin forms, and transcription aborts after the leader. Related mechanisms — riboswitches binding metabolites directly (TPP, FMN, lysine, glycine, guanine, c-di-GMP), small regulatory RNAs with the Hfq chaperone, two-component systems (sensor histidine kinase + response regulator, e.g. EnvZ/OmpR, PhoP/PhoQ) and alternative sigma factors — give bacteria a regulatory repertoire far richer than the textbook operon alone suggests.

    4.6 Mutation and horizontal gene transfer

    Vertical inheritance is only half the story. Spontaneous mutation rates are roughly 10−9–10−10 per base per generation (about 0.003 mutations per genome per generation), but with 109 cells in a millilitre of culture, every possible single point mutation exists in any dense population. Under stress, error-prone polymerases (Pol IV/DinB, Pol V/UmuDC) induced by the SOS response raise the rate further. Superimposed on this is horizontal gene transfer, which shuffles genes across species and even domain boundaries and is the primary engine of antibiotic-resistance spread and pathogen emergence. Comparative genomics of E. coli shows a "core genome" of only ~2,000 genes shared by all strains against a pangenome of >16,000 — most of the species' gene content is accessory and mobile.

    Table 11. Mechanisms of horizontal gene transfer and mobile elements
    MechanismHow it worksDiscovery / key exampleConsequence
    TransformationUptake of naked DNA from the environment through a competence pilus/DNA translocase (ComEC); one strand degraded, the other recombined by RecAGriffith 1928 (rough/smooth Pneumococcus in mice); Avery, MacLeod & McCarty 1944 identified DNA as the transforming principleNatural competence in Streptococcus pneumoniae, Neisseria, Haemophilus, Bacillus subtilis, Vibrio cholerae (chitin-induced); drives capsule switching and penicillin resistance mosaics
    Transduction — generalizedDuring lytic phage assembly, host DNA is mispackaged into a capsid; any chromosomal fragment can be carriedZinder & Lederberg 1952, Salmonella phage P22; phage P1 in E. coliLaboratory workhorse for strain construction; moves resistance genes between staphylococci
    Transduction — specializedImprecise excision of an integrated prophage takes flanking host genes onlyPhage λ excising with gal or bio from E. coliLysogenic conversion: phage-encoded toxins — diphtheria toxin (β-phage), cholera toxin (CTXΦ), Shiga toxin in O157:H7, botulinum toxin C/D, streptococcal SpeA
    ConjugationCell-to-cell transfer through a type IV secretion system; relaxase nicks oriT, single strand transferred 5′-first, rolling-circle replication restores bothLederberg & Tatum 1946; F plasmid (~100 kb, tra region)F+ × F− converts recipient to F+; Hfr strains (F integrated into chromosome) transfer chromosomal genes in a time-ordered manner — the basis of the classical interrupted-mating genetic map (100 minutes for the whole E. coli chromosome); F′ plasmids carry chromosomal genes
    Conjugative transposons / ICEsIntegrative and conjugative elements excise, transfer, and reintegrateTn916 (tetracycline resistance in enterococci), SXT element in V. choleraeSpread resistance without a plasmid replicon
    Transposons"Jumping genes" moving by cut-and-paste (Tn5, Tn10) or replicative (Tn3) transposition via transposase and inverted repeats; insertion sequences (IS elements) carry only the transposaseBarbara McClintock (in maize); IS1, IS10, Tn21Insertional inactivation of genes; mobilization of resistance cassettes onto plasmids; genome rearrangement
    IntegronsSite-specific recombination platform: intI integrase + attI site + promoter captures promoterless gene cassettes and expresses them in seriesClass 1 integrons in Gram-negative multiresistance; the V. cholerae superintegronAssembles multidrug resistance arrays; explains why one plasmid can confer 6–8 resistances at once
    Membrane vesicles / nanotubesOuter membrane vesicles package DNA; intercellular nanotubes exchange cytoplasmic contentsNeisseria, Acinetobacter, Bacillus subtilisEmerging fourth route of transfer, phage- and nuclease-protected

    CRISPR-Cas: bacterial adaptive immunity

    CRISPR arrays (Clustered Regularly Interspaced Short Palindromic Repeats) with associated cas genes occur in roughly 40% of bacterial and 85% of archaeal genomes. They work in three stages. Adaptation: Cas1–Cas2 excises a ~30 bp protospacer from invading phage or plasmid DNA (selecting sequences next to a PAM motif, e.g. 5′-NGG for Streptococcus pyogenes Cas9) and inserts it as a new spacer at the leader end of the array — a chronological record of past infections. Expression: the array is transcribed and processed into crRNAs. Interference: a crRNA-guided effector (single-protein Cas9 or Cas12 in Class 2; multi-subunit Cascade with Cas3 in Class 1 type I; Cas13 targeting RNA in type VI) base-pairs with the invader and cleaves it. The system explains why phage–host coevolution is so rapid, and its repurposing by Jinek, Charpentier and Doudna in 2012 as a programmable nuclease — Nobel Prize 2020 — gave biology its dominant genome-editing tool. Phages counter with anti-CRISPR proteins (Acr), and bacteria maintain many other defences: restriction–modification systems, abortive infection, retrons, cyclic-oligonucleotide-based CBASS, and Thoeris.

    5. The major bacterial phyla

    Bacterial taxonomy has been in upheaval. In 2021 the International Code of Nomenclature of Prokaryotes formally regularized phylum names, so the familiar Proteobacteria became Pseudomonadota, Firmicutes became Bacillota, Actinobacteria became Actinomycetota, and so on. Both names are given below. Modern classification rests on 16S rRNA and increasingly on genome-wide markers (the Genome Taxonomy Database, GTDB) and average nucleotide identity, with a species boundary conventionally at ~95–96% ANI or 98.7% 16S identity. Depending on the scheme, between 40 and more than 100 phyla are recognized — and the majority have no cultured representative at all.

    5.1 Pseudomonadota (Proteobacteria)

    The largest and most metabolically diverse Gram-negative phylum, named after the shape-shifting god Proteus. It contains most Gram-negative bacteria of medical importance and is conventionally divided into classes.

    Alphaproteobacteria

    Often oligotrophic, symbiotic or intracellular. Rhizobium, Bradyrhizobium, Sinorhizobium (legume nitrogen fixation); Agrobacterium tumefaciens (crown gall, Ti plasmid — nature's genetic engineer and the workhorse of plant transformation); Rickettsia, Ehrlichia, Anaplasma, Wolbachia (obligate intracellular; Wolbachia infects ~half of all insect species and is deployed to block dengue transmission in Aedes); Brucella; Bartonella; Caulobacter crescentus (cell-cycle model); Rhodobacter; Pelagibacter ubique of the SAR11 clade — possibly the most abundant single bacterial clade in the ocean, with a streamlined 1.3 Mb genome. Mitochondria descend from this class.

    Betaproteobacteria

    Neisseria meningitidis and N. gonorrhoeae; Bordetella pertussis; Burkholderia cepacia complex and B. pseudomallei (melioidosis); Nitrosomonas (ammonia oxidation); Thiobacillus; Spirillum. Many are aquatic degraders of aromatic compounds.

    Gammaproteobacteria

    The biggest class and the medical heavyweight: Enterobacterales (Escherichia, Salmonella, Shigella, Klebsiella, Enterobacter, Serratia, Proteus, Yersinia); Vibrionales (Vibrio cholerae, V. parahaemolyticus, V. vulnificus, bioluminescent Aliivibrio fischeri); Pseudomonadales (Pseudomonas aeruginosa, Acinetobacter baumannii, Moraxella); Legionellales (Legionella pneumophila, Coxiella burnetii); Pasteurellales (Haemophilus, Pasteurella); Francisella; Xanthomonas (plant pathogen); Shewanella; Alcanivorax (oil degradation); Buchnera (aphid endosymbiont, 640 kb genome).

    Deltaproteobacteria (now often split as Desulfobacterota / Myxococcota)

    Sulfate reducers Desulfovibrio, Desulfobacter; iron reducer Geobacter; predatory Bdellovibrio bacteriovorus, which invades the periplasm of other Gram-negatives and consumes them from within; the myxobacteria Myxococcus xanthus and Sorangium cellulosum, which swarm, prey cooperatively, build multicellular fruiting bodies, and are prolific sources of secondary metabolites (epothilones).

    Epsilonproteobacteria (now Campylobacterota)

    Campylobacter jejuni, Helicobacter pylori, Wolinella, plus the sulfur-oxidizing symbionts of deep-sea hydrothermal vents (Sulfurimonas, Sulfurovum).

    Zetaproteobacteria & others

    Mariprofundus ferrooxydans, iron-oxidizing, forms the rusty microbial mats at Loihi Seamount; Acidithiobacillia (Acidithiobacillus ferrooxidans) drive bioleaching and acid mine drainage.

    5.2 Other major phyla

    Table 12. A systematic tour of bacterial phyla
    Phylum (current / former name)Defining featuresRepresentative generaWhy it matters
    Bacillota / FirmicutesGram-positive, low G+C DNA, many endospore-formers; classes Bacilli, Clostridia, Negativicutes (Gram-negative-staining), ErysipelotrichiaBacillus, Staphylococcus, Streptococcus, Enterococcus, Listeria, Lactobacillus, Clostridium, Clostridioides, Veillonella, Faecalibacterium prausnitziiDominant Gram-positive pathogens; most food fermentations; a major gut phylum whose ratio to Bacteroidota has been linked (contentiously) to obesity; butyrate production by Faecalibacterium and Roseburia maintains colonic health
    Actinomycetota / ActinobacteriaGram-positive, high G+C (up to 70%+), often filamentous with true branching and aerial myceliumStreptomyces, Mycobacterium, Corynebacterium, Nocardia, Actinomyces, Bifidobacterium, Cutibacterium, Micrococcus, Rhodococcus, Frankia, Gardnerella, Tropheryma whippleiStreptomyces alone yields ~two-thirds of clinically used natural-product antibiotics (streptomycin, tetracycline, erythromycin, chloramphenicol, vancomycin, daptomycin, rifamycin, nystatin, amphotericin B) and produces geosmin, the smell of rain on soil; Mycobacterium tuberculosis kills ~1.2 million people a year; Bifidobacterium dominates the breastfed infant gut

    Mycoplasmatota / TenericutesNo cell wall at all; small genomes (0.58–1.4 Mb); sterol-containing membranes; pleomorphic; "fried-egg" coloniesMycoplasma pneumoniae, M. genitalium, M. hominis, Ureaplasma urealyticum, Spiroplasma, plant-pathogenic phytoplasmasSmallest self-replicating cells known; intrinsically resistant to β-lactams and glycopeptides; a notorious contaminant of cell cultures; M. genitalium was the template for the JCVI synthetic minimal cell JCVI-syn3.0 (473 genes)
    Deinococcota / Deinococcus–ThermusThick multilayered envelope that stains Gram-positive but is structurally Gram-negative-like; extreme stress resistanceDeinococcus radiodurans, D. geothermalis, Thermus aquaticus, T. thermophilusD. radiodurans survives 5,000–15,000 Gy of gamma radiation (10 Gy kills a human) by holding 4–10 genome copies in a ring-like nucleoid, accumulating Mn2+–peptide antioxidant complexes that protect repair proteins, and reassembling shattered chromosomes by extended synthesis-dependent strand annealing plus RecA. T. aquaticus, isolated from Yellowstone in 1969, gave us Taq polymerase and made PCR practical

    Aquificota / AquificaeHyperthermophilic, microaerophilic chemolithoautotrophs oxidizing H2 ("water-maker")Aquifex aeolicus (growth to ~95 °C), Hydrogenobacter, SulfurihydrogenibiumAmong the deepest-branching bacterial lineages; primary producers in hot springs and vents, fixing CO2 by the reductive TCA cycle
    Chloroflexota / ChloroflexiFilamentous, gliding, monoderm-like envelope; anoxygenic photosynthesis with chlorosomes in some members ("green non-sulfur bacteria")Chloroflexus aurantiacus, Roseiflexus, Dehalococcoides mccartyi, AnaerolineaDominant mat-formers in hot springs alongside cyanobacteria; use the 3-hydroxypropionate CO2-fixation bicycle; Dehalococcoides is the only known organism that fully dechlorinates PCE/TCE to harmless ethene and is used commercially for groundwater bioremediation; extremely abundant in activated sludge and marine sediments

Nanosynbacter lyticus (TM7x), the first cultured member, lives attached to the surface of an Actinomyces host cell in the human mouth — a bacterium parasitizing a bacterium

    6. Pathogenic bacteria

    Only a small minority of bacteria — perhaps a few hundred species out of the millions estimated to exist — cause human disease. Pathogenicity is the capacity to cause disease; virulence is its quantitative degree, often expressed as the LD50 or ID50. Infectious dose varies over eight orders of magnitude: as few as 10–100 organisms for Shigella dysenteriae, Campylobacter jejuni and E. coli O157:H7, versus 105–108 for Vibrio cholerae (acid-labile, so antacid use dramatically lowers it) or Salmonella Typhi. Pathogens are further divided into primary pathogens, which infect healthy hosts, and opportunists such as Pseudomonas aeruginosa, Acinetobacter baumannii and Staphylococcus epidermidis, which require breached barriers, indwelling devices or immunosuppression.

    6.1 Virulence factors

    Table 13. Categories of bacterial virulence factor
    CategoryMechanismExamples
    AdhesinsBind specific host receptors to resist mechanical clearanceFimH (type 1 pili, uroplakin); PapG (P pili, globoside); S. pyogenes protein F and lipoteichoic acid binding fibronectin; H. pylori BabA binding Lewisb; B. pertussis filamentous haemagglutinin

    EndotoxinLPS lipid A triggering TLR4 → cytokine stormAll Gram-negatives; central to Gram-negative septic shock; assayed by the Limulus amoebocyte lysate test

    Exotoxins are secreted proteins, often extraordinarily potent, and unlike endotoxin they are heat-labile, highly antigenic, and can be formaldehyde-treated into toxoids for vaccines (diphtheria and tetanus toxoids being the classic examples). Many are AB toxins: a catalytic A subunit plus a B subunit that binds the target cell receptor and delivers A to the cytosol. Superantigens take a different route, cross-linking MHC class II on antigen-presenting cells to the Vβ region of T-cell receptors outside the normal peptide groove, so that 5–30% of all T cells are activated at once (versus ~0.01% in a conventional response), producing massive IL-2, IFN-γ and TNF release — the mechanism of toxic shock syndrome.

    Antimicrobial resistance

    Resistance arises by four broad mechanisms: enzymatic inactivation (β-lactamases — from simple TEM-1 through extended-spectrum ESBLs to carbapenemases KPC, NDM-1, OXA-48, VIM; aminoglycoside-modifying enzymes; chloramphenicol acetyltransferase), target alteration (PBP2a in MRSA, altered PBPs in penicillin-resistant pneumococci, gyrA mutations for fluoroquinolones, 23S rRNA methylation by erm for macrolides, D-Ala–D-Lac in VRE, rpoB mutation for rifampicin), reduced uptake (porin loss in Klebsiella and Pseudomonas; the mycobacterial wall), and active efflux (RND pumps such as AcrAB-TolC and MexAB-OprM, tetracycline TetA). Resistance genes move on plasmids, transposons and integrons, so a single conjugation event can transfer multidrug resistance. Roughly 1.3 million deaths per year are directly attributable to bacterial AMR, with several million more associated. Countermeasures include stewardship, infection control, rapid diagnostics, β-lactamase inhibitor combinations (ceftazidime–avibactam, meropenem–vaborbactam), revived older drugs (colistin, fosfomycin), and non-traditional approaches such as phage therapy, anti-virulence compounds and monoclonal antibodies.

    7. Beneficial, commensal and industrial bacteria

    For every pathogen there are thousands of bacteria on which human life depends. The overwhelming majority of bacterial activity on Earth is either neutral or actively beneficial to us: they mineralize organic matter, fix nitrogen, treat our sewage, make our food, and manufacture our medicines.

    7.1 The human microbiota

    An adult carries roughly 3.8 × 1013 bacterial cells, mostly in the colon, where densities reach 1011–1012 per gram of contents. The dominant phyla are Bacillota and Bacteroidota, with Actinomycetota, Pseudomonadota and Verrucomicrobiota present in smaller proportions. Different body sites host sharply distinct communities: Cutibacterium acnes in sebaceous follicles, Staphylococcus epidermidis on moist skin, Streptococcus mutans and Streptococcus sanguinis in dental biofilms, Lactobacillus crispatus and L. iners dominating the healthy vagina where their lactic acid holds the pH below 4.5.

    The gut microbiota performs functions the human genome cannot encode. It ferments the 20–60 g of dietary fibre reaching the colon each day into short-chain fatty acids — acetate, propionate and butyrate — supplying perhaps 5–10% of daily calories, with butyrate the preferred fuel of colonocytes and an inducer of regulatory T cells (Faecalibacterium prausnitzii, Roseburia, Eubacterium rectale are key producers). It synthesizes vitamin K2 (menaquinone), biotin, folate and B12; deconjugates and recycles bile acids; educates the immune system (Bacteroides fragilis polysaccharide A, segmented filamentous bacteria driving Th17 development); provides colonization resistance against pathogens such as C. difficile and Salmonella; and metabolizes drugs (digoxin inactivation by Eggerthella lenta, activation of sulfasalazine by azoreductases). Dysbiosis is associated with inflammatory bowel disease, obesity, metabolic syndrome, colorectal cancer, allergy and possibly neuropsychiatric conditions through the gut–brain axis. Faecal microbiota transplantation cures more than 85% of recurrent C. difficile infection — the most decisive demonstration that the microbiota is a functioning organ.

    Ecosystem services you never see

    Bacteria mineralize dead organic matter and return carbon, nitrogen, phosphorus and sulfur to circulation — without decomposition the biosphere would exhaust its nutrients within decades. Rumen bacteria (Fibrobacter succinogenes, Ruminococcus flavefaciens, Prevotella ruminicola) hydrolyse cellulose so that cattle, sheep and antelope can live on grass, converting it into volatile fatty acids that the animal absorbs; termites, shipworms and aphids all depend on comparable symbioses. Deep-sea hydrothermal vent ecosystems rest entirely on chemolithoautotrophic sulfur oxidizers, including the endosymbionts packed into the trophosome of the tubeworm Riftia pachyptila, which has no mouth or gut at all. Bacterial ice-nucleation proteins from Pseudomonas syringae seed cloud droplets and are used in commercial snowmaking. And in the ocean, marine bacteria and their phages turn over an estimated 20–40% of bacterial biomass every day — the "viral shunt" that keeps carbon in the microbial loop rather than sinking.

    Summary: bacteria are the oldest, most numerous, most metabolically versatile and most ecologically indispensable cellular life on Earth. A few dozen species are why we invented antibiotics; the remaining millions are why the planet still works.

    Archaea

    For most of the twentieth century, biology recognised two kinds of cell: the prokaryote and the eukaryote. Anything small, wall-bounded and lacking a nucleus was a bacterium. In 1977 a quiet, obsessive molecular evolutionist at the University of Illinois discovered that this tidy dichotomy concealed a schism as deep as any in the living world — that hidden among the “bacteria” was a third domain of life, as distinct from bacteria as bacteria are from us. The Archaea are not primitive bacteria, not evolutionary also-rans, and not confined to boiling mud. They build their membranes with a different chemistry, copy their genomes with machinery that looks startlingly like our own, run metabolisms no other organism can perform, produce nearly a billion tonnes of methane a year, dominate the deep ocean’s nitrogen cycle, and — almost uniquely among cellular life — include not a single confirmed pathogen of humans. They also, in all probability, contain our own ancestry.

    1. Discovery: Woese, Fox, and the Third Domain

    The story begins with a methodological conviction. Carl Woese (1928–2012) believed that the true tree of life could not be drawn from morphology — microbes have too little of it — but had to be read from molecules that every organism possesses and that change slowly enough to record billions of years of divergence. His chosen chronometer was the small-subunit ribosomal RNA: the 16S rRNA of prokaryotes and its 18S counterpart in eukaryotes. The ribosome is universal, functionally constrained, and central; its RNA is under strong purifying selection, so substitutions accumulate slowly and can be aligned across enormous evolutionary distances.

    In the pre-sequencing era of the early 1970s, reading an RNA molecule directly was impractical. Woese used oligonucleotide cataloguing: cells were grown with 32P-labelled phosphate, the 16S rRNA purified, digested with ribonuclease T1 (which cuts after every guanosine), and the resulting fragments separated by two-dimensional electrophoresis into a characteristic fingerprint. Each spot was then sequenced by hand. The result was a “catalogue” of oligonucleotides — typically a few dozen fragments of six residues or more — and two organisms could be compared by an association coefficient (SAB) measuring how many catalogue entries they shared. It was punishing, radioactive, year-consuming work.

    In 1976 Woese’s student Ralph Wolfe supplied cultures of methane-producing organisms, then classified as odd bacteria. When Woese and George Fox catalogued the 16S rRNA of Methanobacterium, the fingerprint failed to match anything. The SAB values against true bacteria were no higher than the values against eukaryotic 18S rRNA — the methanogens were as far from Escherichia coli as E. coli was from yeast. In November 1977 the pair published “Phylogenetic structure of the prokaryotic domain: the primary kingdoms” in PNAS, proposing three primary lines of descent: eubacteria, archaebacteria, and urkaryotes. A companion paper with Magrum and Wolfe extended the finding to extreme halophiles and thermoacidophiles.

    Resistance

    The reception was, by the standards of scientific discourse, brutal. Salvador Luria and Ernst Mayr both rejected the proposal outright; Mayr argued from an organismal-biology standpoint that the prokaryote–eukaryote divide was the fundamental one and that a single molecule could not overturn it. Critics objected that Woese had never looked at most of the organisms he classified, that ribosomal RNA might evolve at different rates in different lineages, and that the methanogens’ strangeness was a metabolic curiosity, not a phylogenetic one. Newspaper coverage before peer commentary had settled inflamed matters further. Woese, temperamentally reclusive, largely declined to fight in public.

    Vindication accumulated through the 1980s from independent evidence: the ether-linked isoprenoid membrane lipids described by Thomas Langworthy, De Rosa and others; the absence of peptidoglycan and the discovery of pseudomurein by Otto Kandler and Hans König; the eukaryote-like multi-subunit RNA polymerases characterised by Wolfram Zillig; and the anomalous antibiotic-sensitivity profile. The decisive structural argument came in 1989, when two groups (Iwabe and colleagues; Gogarten and colleagues) used ancient paralogous gene pairs — the elongation factors EF-Tu/EF-G and the ATPase catalytic and non-catalytic subunits, which duplicated before the last universal common ancestor — to root the universal tree without an outgroup. The root fell on the bacterial branch, leaving Archaea and Eukarya as sisters. In 1990 Woese, Kandler and Mark Wheelis formally proposed the term domain and renamed the groups Bacteria, Archaea and Eucarya, deliberately dropping “archaebacteria” because the name perpetuated the very error it described.

    Why archaea were mistaken for bacteria for a century

    Under the microscope they are indistinguishable: 0.5–5 μm cells, no nucleus, no membrane-bound organelles, a single circular chromosome, operons, polycistronic mRNA, 70S ribosomes, binary fission, often flagellated. Gram staining works on them (variably) and gives no warning. Every diagnostic character that separates them from bacteria is molecular — lipid chemistry, wall polymer, transcription and translation machinery — and none of it was visible to classical microbiology. The three-domain proposal is therefore also a lesson in the limits of phenotype: the deepest divisions in the tree of life left no morphological trace at all.

    The conceptual consequences reached far beyond taxonomy. “Prokaryote” was demoted from a taxon to a description of cellular grade — a negative statement (“lacking a nucleus”) that unites two unrelated domains, much as “invertebrate” does. Molecular phylogenetics became the arbiter of microbial classification. And the 16S rRNA gene, having built the tree, became the tool by which the uncultured majority of the microbial world was later discovered: Norman Pace’s culture-independent surveys in the 1980s, and the metagenomics that followed, are direct descendants of Woese’s catalogues.

    2. What Makes an Archaeon: The Molecular Signature

    2.1 Membrane lipids

    The archaeal membrane is the domain’s most chemically absolute distinction — no known exception exists. Four differences compound:

    • Ether, not ester, linkages. Bacterial and eukaryotic phospholipids attach their hydrocarbon tails to glycerol through ester bonds (–CO–O–). Archaea use ether bonds (–O–), which lack a carbonyl and are far more resistant to hydrolysis, to oxidation, and to high temperature, low pH and high salt.
    • Isoprenoid, not fatty acyl, chains. The tails are polymers of the five-carbon isoprene unit, built by the mevalonate pathway — typically C20 phytanyl chains, methyl-branched at every fourth carbon. That branching is functionally important: the methyl groups prevent tight crystalline packing and keep membranes fluid over an enormous temperature range, while also reducing proton and ion permeability.
    • Opposite glycerol stereochemistry. Archaeal lipids are built on sn-glycerol-1-phosphate (G1P); bacteria and eukaryotes use the enantiomer sn-glycerol-3-phosphate (G3P). The enzymes that make them — G1P dehydrogenase versus G3P dehydrogenase — are non-homologous, i.e. independently invented. This is the strongest form of the “lipid divide,” and it underpins one influential hypothesis that the last universal common ancestor had a heterochiral or non-lipid boundary, with true membranes crystallising separately in each lineage.
    • Tetraethers and monolayers. Beyond the standard bilayer-forming archaeol (diphytanyl glycerol diether), many archaea — especially hyperthermophiles and acidophiles — make caldarchaeol or GDGTs (glycerol dibiphytanyl glycerol tetraethers), in which two C40 biphytanyl chains span the entire membrane and are capped by glycerol head groups at both ends. The result is a covalently continuous monolayer, not a bilayer: it cannot be split, cannot suffer leaflet separation, and is spectacularly impermeable to protons. Picrophilus and Sulfolobus maintain a near-neutral cytoplasm against an external pH of 0–2 largely because of it. Tetraethers may additionally contain up to eight cyclopentane rings per molecule; the number of rings rises with growth temperature, tightening lipid packing — a tunable thermostat, and the basis of the TEX86 palaeothermometer (§8).
    • Crenarchaeol. A distinctive GDGT bearing four cyclopentane rings plus a cyclohexane ring, discovered in the 1990s and initially attributed to marine Crenarchaeota. It is now recognised as the signature lipid of the Thaumarchaeota / Nitrososphaerota, the ammonia-oxidising archaea, and its ubiquity in marine sediments was one of the first clues that non-thermophilic archaea were abundant in ordinary seawater.
    Membrane lipid comparison
    FeatureBacteria & EukaryaArchaea
    Glycerol–chain linkageEster (–COO–)Ether (–O–)
    Hydrocarbon chainStraight-chain fatty acids (C14–C20), unbranchedIsoprenoid, methyl-branched (phytanyl C20, biphytanyl C40)
    Glycerol stereoisomersn-glycerol-3-phosphatesn-glycerol-1-phosphate
    Biosynthetic enzymeG3P dehydrogenase (GpsA)G1P dehydrogenase (EgsA) — non-homologous
    Chain synthesisFatty acid synthase, acetate unitsMevalonate / isoprenoid pathway
    ArchitectureBilayer onlyBilayer (archaeol) or covalent monolayer (GDGT tetraethers)
    Thermal tuningChain length, unsaturation, branchingCyclopentane/cyclohexane ring number, tetraether fraction
    Proton permeabilityModerateVery low, especially tetraethers
    SterolsPresent in eukaryotes; hopanoids in many bacteriaGenerally absent (some archaea make related polyterpenes)

    2.2 Cell walls — and the absence of peptidoglycan

    No archaeon makes peptidoglycan (murein). The defining bacterial polymer — alternating N-acetylglucosamine and N-acetylmuramic acid cross-linked by short peptides containing D-amino acids — is entirely absent. What archaea build instead is remarkably varied:

    • Pseudomurein (pseudopeptidoglycan) in Methanobacterium, Methanothermus and relatives: superficially similar, but built from N-acetyltalosaminuronic acid instead of muramic acid, joined by β(1→3) rather than β(1→4) bonds, and cross-linked by peptides of exclusively L-amino acids. A striking case of convergent engineering.
    • S-layers — the commonest archaeal wall. A paracrystalline two-dimensional lattice of a single (glyco)protein species, self-assembled directly on the membrane surface, often anchored by a transmembrane stalk. In Sulfolobus and Haloferax the S-layer is the sole wall, and its geometry determines cell shape.
    • Methanochondroitin in Methanosarcina: a heteropolysaccharide chemically resembling animal chondroitin, allowing the cells to form large multicellular packets.
    • Sulfated heteropolysaccharides in Halococcus, whose dense negative charge helps balance extreme external ionic strength.
    • No wall at all in Thermoplasma and Ferroplasma, which survive naked at 55 °C and pH 2 by stabilising the membrane with a lipoglycan and tetraether lipids; they are pleomorphic and osmotically fragile in dilute media.
    Clinical/practical consequence. Because they have no peptidoglycan, archaea are intrinsically resistant to all β-lactams (penicillins, cephalosporins, carbapenems), to glycopeptides such as vancomycin, to D-cycloserine, bacitracin and fosfomycin, and to lysozyme, which cleaves the β(1→4) muramic acid bond that archaea do not possess. This is not acquired resistance and carries no plasmid; it is a consequence of chemistry. Selective media for methanogens exploit it directly, using penicillin and vancomycin to suppress bacterial competitors.

    2.3 Information processing: a eukaryotic core in a prokaryotic cell

    If the membrane is what makes archaea unlike bacteria, the transcription and replication machinery is what makes them like us. The archaeal cell runs bacterial-style genome organisation — compact circular chromosome, operons, polycistronic messages, coupled transcription and translation, Shine–Dalgarno ribosome binding on some genes — using enzymes that are simplified versions of the eukaryotic set.

    • RNA polymerase. Bacteria use a single core enzyme of ~5 subunits (α2ββ′ω) plus an interchangeable σ factor. Archaea use a single RNAP of 11–13 subunits, subunit-for-subunit homologous to eukaryotic RNA polymerase II, including the Rpb4/7 stalk. It is insensitive to rifampicin and streptolydigin, which inhibit bacterial RNAP.
    • Basal transcription factors. Archaeal promoters carry a TATA box and an upstream BRE element, bound by a genuine TBP (TATA-binding protein) and TFB (the orthologue of eukaryotic TFIIB), with TFE (TFIIEα) assisting open-complex formation. This minimal three-component system is the ancestral core of the eukaryotic pre-initiation complex, and it is the reason archaeal transcription is a favourite model for structural biologists. Sequence-specific regulation, by contrast, is usually carried out by bacterial-type regulators — a genuine mosaic.
    • Histones and chromatin. Many archaea (most Euryarchaeota, Asgards, some others) encode histones with the classic histone fold, which dimerise and wrap DNA into a hypernucleosome — a continuous, variable-length superhelical ramp rather than a fixed octamer, and lacking the N-terminal tails that carry eukaryotic epigenetic marks. Other archaea (Sulfolobales) instead use small basic proteins such as Sul7d, Cren7 and Alba. Thermoplasma uses HTa.
    • Replication. The archaeal replisome is eukaryotic in origin: ORC/Cdc6 initiator proteins mark origins (one in most Euryarchaeota, up to three in Sulfolobus), the MCM hexamer is the replicative helicase, GINS and Cdc45/RecJ homologues form the CMG-like complex, priming is by a heterodimeric primase of the PriS/PriL (eukaryotic Polα-primase) family rather than bacterial DnaG, the sliding clamp is a true trimeric PCNA loaded by an RFC-like clamp loader, and the replicative polymerases are B-family (and, in Euryarchaeota, the archaea-specific D-family) — not bacterial C-family Pol III. Okazaki fragment maturation uses Fen1 and DNA ligase I homologues.
    • Translation. Ribosomes are 70S with 16S/23S/5S rRNA — bacterial in size — but carry additional eukaryote-like ribosomal proteins and a distinct set of rRNA modifications, many guided by C/D-box and H/ACA-box sRNAs homologous to eukaryotic snoRNAs. The initiator tRNA carries unmodified methionine, not the N-formyl-methionine of bacteria and of mitochondria/chloroplasts; there is no formyltransferase and no peptide deformylase requirement. Initiation factors are the eukaryotic aIF2 (a heterotrimeric GTPase) rather than bacterial IF2. Elongation factors are aEF1A and aEF2, and aEF2 carries the modified histidine residue diphthamide — the target of diphtheria toxin. tRNAs bear archaea-specific modified bases such as archaeosine (G+) at position 15 and, in hyperthermophiles, extensive methylation and thiolation that raise melting temperature.
    • Introns and RNA processing. Archaea lack spliceosomal introns, but many have bulge-helix-bulge introns in tRNA and rRNA genes, excised by a splicing endonuclease that is the direct ancestor of the eukaryotic tRNA-splicing endonuclease.
    Antibiotic response as a domain diagnostic
    AgentTargetBacteriaArchaeaEukarya
    Penicillin / vancomycinPeptidoglycan synthesisSensitiveResistant (no murein)Resistant
    Lysozymeβ(1→4) MurNAc–GlcNAc bondSensitiveResistantResistant
    Chloramphenicol50S peptidyl transferaseSensitiveResistantResistant (cytosol)
    Streptomycin / kanamycin30S decoding siteSensitiveResistantResistant (cytosol)
    RifampicinRNA polymerase βSensitiveResistantResistant
    Diphtheria toxinADP-ribosylates diphthamide on EF-2Resistant (no diphthamide)SensitiveSensitive
    AnisomycinLarge-subunit A siteResistantSensitiveSensitive
    Pseudomonic acid (mupirocin)Ile-tRNA synthetaseSensitiveResistantResistant
    NovobiocinDNA gyrase BSensitiveVariable (many lack gyrase)Resistant

    2.4 The archaellum

    Archaea swim, but not with a bacterial flagellum. The organelle once called the archaeal flagellum is now named the archaellum, because it is not homologous to the bacterial structure at any level. Its filament is built from small glycosylated archaellins assembled from the base (bacterial flagellin is added at the distal tip through a hollow channel); the filament is thinner (10–14 nm versus ~20 nm) and solid, not hollow. Its assembly apparatus — FlaI ATPase, FlaJ membrane platform, FlaH, and a class III signal peptidase (FlaK/PibD) that cleaves the archaellin pre-protein — is unmistakably derived from the type IV pilus secretion system. Most fundamentally, rotation is powered by direct ATP hydrolysis at the FlaI motor, not by proton-motive force through a Mot stator. It is a genuine case of convergent evolution: two domains independently invented a rotary swimming machine, from unrelated parts, for the same job. Archaeal chemotaxis, however, uses bacterial-type Che proteins acquired by lateral transfer, coupled to the archaellum by the adaptor protein CheF.

    Read the table by column pairs and a pattern emerges: archaea share cell architecture with bacteria and information processing with eukaryotes. That split is not a coincidence — it is the central evidence in the eukaryogenesis debate of §4.6.

    4. The Major Archaeal Lineages

    Archaeal taxonomy is in active flux. The classical two-phylum picture (Euryarchaeota, Crenarchaeota) has been replaced by a sprawl of phyla, most known only from metagenome-assembled genomes, and formal names have been revised under the phylum-rank rules adopted in 2021 (Crenarchaeota → Thermoproteota; Thaumarchaeota → Nitrososphaerota; Euryarchaeota split into Methanobacteriota, Halobacteriota and others). Both name sets remain in use.

    4.1 Euryarchaeota — the metabolic generalists

    Methanogens
    Methanobrevibacter smithii — dominant archaeon of the human colon, present in most adults. Methanocaldococcus jannaschii — deep-sea vent hyperthermophile, and in 1996 the first archaeal genome ever sequenced, which revealed that ~56% of its genes had no known counterpart in any bacterium. Methanosarcina — the metabolic virtuoso, the only genus able to use all three methanogenic pathways; M. acetivorans has one of the largest archaeal genomes (5.7 Mb). Methanopyrus kandleri — the temperature record holder.
    Haloarchaea
    Halobacterium salinarum — the bacteriorhodopsin organism, colouring salterns purple-red with its carotenoids. Haloferax volcanii — from the Dead Sea, now the workhorse genetic model for archaea, capable of cell fusion and genome exchange. Haloquadratum walsbyi — the “square archaeon” of Walsby: flat, gas-vacuolated postage-stamp squares 2–5 μm across and only ~0.1–0.2 μm thick, seen in 1980 and resisting culture until 2004.
    Thermoplasmatales
    Thermoplasma acidophilum — wall-less, from self-heating coal refuse piles, 59 °C and pH 2. Picrophilus torridus — grows optimally at pH 0.7 and still divides at pH −0.06, the most acidophilic organism known; it requires acid simply to hold its membrane together. Ferroplasma acidarmanus — from Iron Mountain acid mine drainage.
    Thermococci & Archaeoglobi
    Pyrococcus furiosus (“rushing fireball”) — 100 °C optimum, doubling in 37 minutes, source of Pfu polymerase. Thermococcus litoralis — source of Vent polymerase. Archaeoglobus fulgidus — a hyperthermophilic sulfate reducer that retains methanogen coenzymes without producing methane, and a notorious agent of oil-reservoir souring.

    4.2 Crenarchaeota / Thermoproteota — heat and sulfur

    Overwhelmingly thermophilic and sulfur-dependent, with S-layer walls and ESCRT-based (Cdv) cell division. Saccharolobus solfataricus and Sulfolobus acidocaldarius (the genus was split in 2018) are aerobic thermoacidophiles from solfataras, growing near 80 °C and pH 2–3 and oxidising sulfur to sulfuric acid — the founding models for archaeal chromatin, CRISPR and virology. Pyrolobus fumarii, from a black smoker chimney wall, grows from 90 to 113 °C and cannot grow below 90 °C; it survived an hour in an autoclave at 121 °C. Pyrodictium occultum forms mats of disc-shaped cells joined by a network of hollow protein tubules (cannulae) unique in biology. Thermoproteus tenax and Thermofilum are anaerobic rod-shaped sulfur respirers; Ignicoccus hospitalis is remarkable for a double membrane enclosing a large periplasm in which its ATP synthase resides — energising the outer, not the inner, membrane.

    4.3 Thaumarchaeota / Nitrososphaerota — the abundant, invisible majority

    The discovery that changed archaeal ecology. Culture-independent surveys in the early 1990s (Fuhrman; DeLong) found archaeal 16S sequences in ordinary cold seawater, where no one expected them. It is now clear that these ammonia-oxidising archaea (AOA) are among the most abundant organisms on Earth: they can constitute 20–40% of all picoplankton cells in the mesopelagic ocean, with a global population estimated near 1028 cells. Nitrosopumilus maritimus SCM1, isolated from a Seattle aquarium in 2005, oxidises ammonia to nitrite using an ammonia monooxygenase (AMO) and a copper-based, cytochrome-free electron chain, and does so at nanomolar substrate concentrations — an affinity roughly two orders of magnitude better than ammonia-oxidising bacteria, which is why they dominate the oligotrophic ocean and many soils. They fix CO2 by the 3-hydroxypropionate/4-hydroxybutyrate cycle, are the principal source of crenarchaeol, and are a significant biological source of the greenhouse gas nitrous oxide. Soil AOA (Nitrososphaera) matter greatly to agricultural nitrogen loss.

    4.4 Nanoarchaeota and DPANN — life at the minimum

    Nanoarchaeum equitans (“the riding dwarf”), described by Huber and Stetter in 2002 from an Icelandic submarine vent, is a 400 nm coccus that grows only while attached to the surface of its crenarchaeal host Ignicoccus hospitalis. Its genome is 490,885 bp with ~540 genes — among the smallest of any cellular organism — and it lacks essentially all biosynthetic capacity for lipids, amino acids, nucleotides and cofactors, drawing them across the cell–cell junction. Curiously, it retains a near-complete DNA repair and information-processing set, and its genome is extremely dense with split and fragmented genes, including a split tRNA gene whose halves are transcribed separately and joined. Its 16S rRNA is so divergent that standard “universal” PCR primers miss it entirely — a warning about how much of the biosphere molecular surveys may silently exclude.

    Nanoarchaeum is now placed in the DPANN superradiation (Diapherotrites, Parvarchaeota, Aenigmarchaeota, Nanoarchaeota, Nanohaloarchaeota, plus Micrarchaeota, Woesearchaeota, Pacearchaeota, Altiarchaeota and more). DPANN organisms share tiny cells, reduced genomes, absent core pathways, and — where studied — an ectosymbiotic or parasitic dependence on other archaea. Whether their reduction is ancient or a repeated recent consequence of symbiosis is contested, and long-branch attraction complicates their placement in the tree.

    4.5 Korarchaeota and Bathyarchaeota

    Korarchaeota were identified in 1996 as deeply branching 16S sequences from Obsidian Pool in Yellowstone; the first genome, of “Candidatus Korarchaeum cryptofilum,” came from an enrichment in 2008 and revealed a thin filamentous peptide-fermenting anaerobe with a curious mixture of crenarchaeal and euryarchaeal traits. Bathyarchaeota (formerly the Miscellaneous Crenarchaeotal Group) are among the most abundant organisms in marine subsurface sediments — plausibly 1028 cells globally — and metagenomics indicates a versatile heterotrophic and acetogenic lifestyle, with some lineages carrying mcrA genes, implying methane metabolism outside the Euryarchaeota. Neither group has a robust pure culture, and both illustrate how much archaeal diversity is known only as genome sequence.

    4.6 Asgard archaea and the origin of eukaryotes

    In 2015 Ettema’s group assembled a genome from sediment near the “Loki’s Castle” hydrothermal field in the Arctic mid-ocean ridge, and named it Lokiarchaeum. It contained a startling number of eukaryotic signature proteins (ESPs): multiple small Ras-superfamily GTPases, a complete ESCRT-I/II/III system, ubiquitin-conjugating machinery, an expanded actin/profilin/gelsolin cytoskeletal repertoire, and roadblock/longin domain proteins. Sister lineages followed — Thorarchaeota, Odinarchaeota, Heimdallarchaeota, and later Hodarchaeales, Helarchaeota, Wukongarchaeota — collectively the Asgard superphylum. Phylogenomic analyses of conserved marker proteins consistently place eukaryotes within the Asgards, most often as sister to or nested inside the Heimdallarchaeia (specifically the Hodarchaeales in recent analyses).

    In 2020 Imachi, Nobu and colleagues reported, after twelve years of continuous bioreactor cultivation of deep-sea methane-seep sediment, the first isolate: “Candidatus Prometheoarchaeum syntrophicum” strain MK-D1. It is a small (~550 nm) slow-growing anaerobe — doubling times of 14 to 25 days — that degrades amino acids and depends syntrophically on partners (a Methanogenium and a Halodesulfovibrio) to consume the hydrogen it produces. Strikingly, it has no internal membrane compartments but extends long, branching, sometimes vesicle-forming protrusions. Its discoverers proposed the entangle–engulf–endogenize (E3) model: an archaeal host wrapped its protrusions around a hydrogen-consuming alphaproteobacterial partner, and the eventual internalisation of that partner produced both the mitochondrion and, from the enclosing membranes, the beginnings of the eukaryotic endomembrane system.

    Two domains or three? The eocyte hypothesis, argued by James Lake since 1984 and revived by modern phylogenomics, holds that eukaryotes are not a third primary domain but a branch inside the Archaea. On this view the tree of life has two domains, Bacteria and Archaea, with Eukarya a derived archaeal lineage that acquired a bacterial endosymbiont. The evidence — ESPs, the archaeal character of eukaryotic transcription, replication and ribosomal proteins, and the placement of eukaryotes within Asgard in marker-gene trees — is strong but not unanimous; critics point to compositional bias, gene selection, and the chimeric ancestry of eukaryotes (informational genes archaeal, operational genes largely bacterial). Woese’s three-domain scheme remains the standard teaching framework, but the two-domain tree is now the majority position among specialists.

    5. Extremophily — and How It Is Achieved

    Archaea are not exclusively extremophiles — the AOA of the open ocean and the methanogens of a cow’s rumen live in perfectly ordinary conditions — but they hold nearly every record for physicochemical tolerance.

    Record-holding archaea and their adaptations
    ExtremeOrganism / limitPrincipal adaptations
    Highest temperatureMethanopyrus kandleri strain 116 — growth at 122 °C (under 20 MPa); Pyrolobus fumarii 113 °C; Geogemma barossii “strain 121”Tetraether monolayer with cyclopentane rings; molar concentrations of cyclic 2,3-diphosphoglycerate and di-myo-inositol phosphate as protein stabilisers; reverse gyrase introducing positive DNA supercoils; compact proteins with more ion pairs, salt bridge networks and shorter loops; hyper-modified tRNA; chaperonins (thermosome)
    Lowest pHPicrophilus torridus — optimum pH 0.7, growth to pH −0.06; Ferroplasma, SulfolobusExtremely proton-impermeable tetraether membrane; highly positive cytoplasmic membrane potential (inside-positive Donnan potential) opposing proton influx; active proton export; acid-stable, isoelectrically acidic exported proteins; the cytoplasm is held near pH 4.6
    Highest pHNatronomonas pharaonis, Natronococcus — soda lakes at pH 9.5–11Acidic cell-surface glycoproteins; Na+/H+ antiporters and a sodium-based rather than proton-based bioenergetics; often simultaneously haloalkaliphilic
    Highest salinityHalobacterium, Haloquadratum, Halococcus — to saturated NaCl (5.2 M); many lyse below 2–3 MThe salt-in strategy (below)
    Highest pressurePyrococcus yayanosii (obligate piezophile, optimum 52 MPa); Thermococcus barophilus; methanogens active above 100 MPaPressure-adjusted membrane fluidity (raised unsaturation/branching); piezo-stabilised protein cores with reduced cavity volume; upregulated hydrogenases; osmolyte shifts (mannosylglycerate)
    Radiation / desiccationHalobacterium NRC-1 survives kilogray doses and years of desiccation in salt crystals; Thermococcus gammatolerans to 30 kGyMultiple genome copies (polyploidy) enabling homologous recombination repair; efficient RadA/Mre11-Rad50 double-strand break repair; high intracellular Mn(II)/Fe ratio protecting repair proteins from oxidation; carotenoid (bacterioruberin) quenching; DNA protection within trapped brine inclusions
    Lowest temperatureMarine Thaumarchaeota and psychrophilic methanogens active near −2 °C in polar water and permafrostIncreased unsaturated/anteiso lipid content, cold-shock and RNA helicase expression, flexible high-turnover enzymes

    Two solutions to salt

    Extreme halophiles face an osmotic problem: at 4–5 M external NaCl, water is drawn out of the cell. Two strategies exist, and archaea use both, in different lineages.

    • Compatible solutes (“salt-out”). The cytoplasm is kept comparatively low in ions and osmotic balance is achieved by accumulating uncharged or zwitterionic organic solutes — glycine betaine, ectoine, trehalose, and the archaeal specialities 2-sulfotrehalose, mannosylglycerate and di-myo-inositol phosphate. These do not perturb protein hydration shells, so the proteome need not be specially adapted, and the organism can move between salinities. Most methanogenic and halophilic bacteria, and haloalkaliphilic methanogens, use this route.
    • Salt-in. The Halobacteria instead pump K+ inward to molar concentrations (often 4–5 M KCl intracellularly) while excluding Na+, balancing the external brine ion-for-ion. This is energetically cheap but demands a wholesale rebuilt proteome: haloarchaeal proteins are conspicuously enriched in aspartate and glutamate on their surfaces and depleted in lysine, giving them strongly acidic isoelectric points. The excess negative charge binds an ordered hydration shell and keeps the protein soluble at high ionic strength — but the same proteins denature in dilute buffer, which is why haloarchaea lyse in fresh water and why their enzymes are useful in non-aqueous industrial settings.

    6. Archaeal Metabolism

    6.1 Methanogenesis — a metabolism unique to one domain

    Biological methane production is performed by no bacterium and no eukaryote. It is a strictly anaerobic form of respiration, poisoned by traces of oxygen, and it terminates the anaerobic food chain in wetlands, rice paddies, landfills, sediments, digesters and animal guts. Three pathways exist:

    The three methanogenic pathways
    PathwayReactionΔG°′ (kJ/mol CH4)Typical organisms & habitats
    Hydrogenotrophic (CO2-reducing)4 H2 + CO2 → CH4 + 2 H2O−131Methanobrevibacter, Methanocaldococcus, Methanopyrus; gut, vents, deep subsurface. Depends on syntrophic H2 supply from fermenters
    AcetoclasticCH3COO− + H+ → CH4 + CO2−36Methanosarcina, Methanosaeta (Methanothrix); freshwater sediment, digesters. Accounts for roughly two-thirds of global biogenic methane
    Methylotrophic4 CH3OH → 3 CH4 + CO2 + 2 H2O (also methylamines, dimethyl sulfide)−106Methanosarcina, Methanosphaera, Methanomassiliicoccus; marine sediment, hypersaline brines, human gut. Uses substrates sulfate reducers cannot, avoiding competition

    The chemistry runs on cofactors found almost nowhere else: methanofuran accepts the first CO2; tetrahydromethanopterin (H4MPT) carries the C1 unit through formyl, methenyl, methylene and methyl states; coenzyme F420 is the deazaflavin electron carrier whose blue-green 420 nm autofluorescence lets a microscopist identify methanogens on sight; coenzyme M (2-mercaptoethanesulfonate, the smallest known coenzyme) carries the terminal methyl group; and coenzyme B supplies the electrons. The final step is catalysed by methyl-coenzyme M reductase (MCR), which contains the nickel tetrapyrrole cofactor F430 and releases methane plus the CoM-S-S-CoB heterodisulfide. Energy is conserved indirectly: the methyl transfer from H4MPT to CoM by the MtrA-H complex pumps Na+, and heterodisulfide reductase drives flavin-based electron bifurcation. The mcrA gene encoding MCR’s alpha subunit is the standard molecular marker for methanogens in environmental surveys.

    Methanogens generate on the order of 1 billion tonnes (109 t) of methane per year — the majority of atmospheric methane input. Methane has roughly 28–30 times the 100-year global-warming potential of CO2, making archaeal metabolism a first-order term in the climate system.

    6.2 Anaerobic oxidation of methane

    Much of that methane never reaches the atmosphere. In marine sediments, ANME archaea (ANME-1, -2, -3; relatives of Methanosarcinales) run methanogenesis in reverse, using a modified MCR to activate methane, and pass electrons to sulfate-reducing bacterial partners (Desulfosarcina, Desulfobulbus) in dense structured consortia. The net reaction — CH4 + SO42− → HCO3− + HS− + H2O — yields barely −20 to −40 kJ/mol, close to the thermodynamic minimum for life, which is why doubling times are measured in months. Electron transfer occurs by direct interspecies electron transfer through multihaem cytochromes and conductive nanowires, not by diffusible intermediates. This microbial filter consumes an estimated 80–90% of methane produced in marine sediments, and it precipitates the enormous authigenic carbonate structures found at cold seeps. Other ANME couple methane oxidation to nitrate (Ca. Methanoperedens) or to metal oxides.

    6.3 Ammonia, sulfur, and light

    Beyond methane, archaea perform: ammonia oxidation (§4.3), the first and rate-limiting step of nitrification, dominating in oligotrophic ocean and acidic soils; sulfur oxidation, as in Sulfolobus and Acidianus, generating sulfuric acid and driving the acidity of solfataras and acid mine drainage; sulfate reduction in Archaeoglobus; sulfur and elemental-sulfur respiration in Thermoproteus, Pyrodictium and Pyrococcus; iron oxidation in Ferroplasma; and a full range of aerobic and anaerobic heterotrophy in haloarchaea, which also carry out denitrification.

    No archaeon performs chlorophyll-based photosynthesis. Instead, haloarchaea use bacteriorhodopsin: a seven-transmembrane-helix protein with a retinal chromophore covalently bound through a Schiff base to a lysine. Absorption of a photon isomerises retinal from all-trans to 13-cis, and the ensuing cycle of proton transfers translocates one H+ across the membrane per photon. The resulting proton-motive force drives ATP synthase — light-driven ATP synthesis with no chlorophyll, no electron transport chain, and no carbon fixation. It is a photoheterotrophic supplement, deployed when oxygen tension falls (oxygen dissolves poorly in brine) and organics are scarce. Related pigments in the same purple membrane include halorhodopsin (an inward light-driven chloride pump, maintaining KCl balance) and the sensory rhodopsins I and II, which mediate phototaxis. Bacteriorhodopsin’s two-dimensional crystalline purple membrane made it the first membrane protein whose structure was solved (Henderson and Unwin, 1975, by electron cryo-microscopy), and its relatives became the founding tools of optogenetics.

    7. Archaea and Humans

    Humans carry archaea. The human archaeome is small in diversity but not in abundance:

    • Gut. Methanobrevibacter smithii is the dominant archaeon, detectable in the large majority of adults and reaching up to 10% of anaerobes in some individuals. It consumes H2 and formate produced by bacterial fermentation, and by keeping hydrogen partial pressure low it thermodynamically favours the fermentations of Bacteroides and others — classic syntrophy, and the reason methanogens increase the efficiency of polysaccharide extraction. Methanosphaera stadtmanae, a methylotroph reducing methanol with H2, is the second commonest. Members of the Methanomassiliicoccales occur in gut and oral cavity. Roughly 30–60% of adults are breath-methane positive, a phenotype tracking methanogen load.
    • Mouth. Methanobrevibacter oralis is enriched in subgingival plaque of patients with periodontitis, and its abundance correlates with pocket depth; the proposed mechanism is again syntrophic, hydrogen removal boosting the fermentative activity of established periodontal pathogens. Causation is unproven.
    • Skin, nose and lung. Amplicon studies using archaea-tuned primers find Thaumarchaeota (Nitrososphaera-like) on human skin, especially at low-sebum, ammonia-rich sites, and more abundant in the very young and the elderly. Their metabolic role — possibly ammonia oxidation from sweat urea — is speculative.
    • Vagina and other sites. Sparse, occasional methanogen detection.
    The pathogen puzzle. Among tens of thousands of bacterial species, hundreds are human pathogens. Among archaea, not one is a confirmed pathogen — there is no archaeal Koch’s-postulate disease. Leading explanations, none decisive: (1) archaea generally lack the recognised bacterial virulence toolkit — no lipopolysaccharide, no known exotoxins, few secretion systems of types III/IV used for host-cell injection; (2) most human-associated archaea are strict anaerobes with narrow metabolic and redox requirements, poorly suited to invading oxygenated tissue; (3) slow growth relative to bacterial competitors; (4) they may simply be under-detected — universal 16S primers miss archaea, standard clinical culture conditions do not support them, and archaeal infection would be invisible to routine diagnostics; (5) possible immune tolerance, though M. stadtmanae does provoke strong TLR-dependent cytokine responses from human dendritic cells, whereas M. smithii does not — suggesting immunological differences that are real but not yet linked to disease.

    Correlational research continues on M. smithii and human physiology: methanogen load associates with constipation and with methane-positive small intestinal bacterial overgrowth (IMO), where methane slows intestinal transit; studies of associations with obesity and with anorexia nervosa have produced conflicting results. Archaea have also been reported in abscesses and in gut inflammation, but as commensal bystanders. Treatments targeting methanogens (e.g. combined neomycin plus rifaximin for IMO) exist clinically, though their microbiological rationale is imperfect given archaeal antibiotic resistance profiles.

    8. Biotechnology, Ecology and Climate

    Archaeal products and applications

    Pfu DNA polymerasePyrococcus furiosusThermostable B-family polymerase with 3′→5′ proofreading exonuclease — error rate roughly 10-fold lower than Taq (which has none).     TEX86 palaeothermometerMarine Thaumarchaeota (GDGTs, crenarchaeol)The ratio of tetraether lipids with different cyclopentane ring numbers preserved in sediment calibrates to sea-surface temperature, giving records reaching back >100 million years, independent of carbonate chemistry.     Biogas / anaerobic digestionMethanosarcina, Methanothrix, MethanoculleusMethanogens are the terminal step converting sewage sludge, manure and food waste into 55–70% methane biogas; process stability depends on keeping acetoclastic methanogens from being outpaced by acidogens
    Halophilic enzymesHaloferax, HalobacteriumAcidic, salt-requiring proteases, amylases, lipases and nucleases active in brines and in low-water/organic-solvent systems; used in salt-fermented foods and speciality biocatalysis

    BioleachingSulfolobus metallicus, Acidianus brierleyi, Metallosphaera sedula, FerroplasmaThermoacidophilic oxidation of sulfide minerals liberates copper, gold and uranium from low-grade ore; high-temperature archaeal heaps and stirred tanks outperform mesophilic bacteria on refractory chalcopyrite
    ArchaeosomesArchaeal tetraether lipidsLiposomes of exceptional chemical and thermal stability and resistance to bile salts and low pH — investigated as oral vaccine adjuvants and drug carriers
    Optogenetics precursorsHalobacterium, NatronomonasBacteriorhodopsin and halorhodopsin established the microbial rhodopsin family that yielded the neuroscience toolkit

    Archaea and the climate system

    Ruminants emit roughly 90–100 million tonnes of methane per year, produced not by the animal but by rumen archaea — principally Methanobrevibacter ruminantium and Methanobrevibacter gottschalkii — consuming the hydrogen released by fibre-fermenting bacteria, fungi and protozoa. This represents both a climate burden (about 5% of all anthropogenic greenhouse forcing) and a 2–12% loss of the animal’s gross energy intake. Mitigation strategies target the archaea directly: the MCR inhibitor 3-nitrooxypropanol (Bovaer) suppresses methanogenesis by around 30%; bromoform-containing red seaweed (Asparagopsis) can achieve far larger reductions in feedlot trials; and vaccines against rumen methanogens, alternative hydrogen sinks (nitrate, propionate-directed fermentation), and selective breeding for low-methane rumen microbiomes are under development.

    On geological timescales, archaea have been decisive. Methanogens may have supplied the methane greenhouse that kept the Archean Earth habitable under a faint young Sun. Their descendants now sit at both ends of the methane budget — the largest biological source and, through the ANME consortia, the largest biological sink. Ammonia-oxidising archaea control the entry point of nitrification in the ocean and hence nitrogen availability, nitrous oxide production, and the marine nitrogen cycle’s coupling to carbon export. For a domain unknown before 1977, and unrecognised outside boiling springs before 1992, archaea turn out to be running some of the planet’s largest chemical fluxes — and, if the Asgard evidence holds, to be the lineage from which we ourselves emerged.

    Viruses & Acellular Agents

    Between chemistry and biology sits a class of entities that own genomes but not metabolisms, that reproduce but cannot grow, that evolve relentlessly yet manufacture nothing on their own. Viruses and their stripped-down relatives — viroids, satellites, prions — are the most abundant biological objects on Earth, the deepest reservoir of genetic novelty in the biosphere, the drivers of some of the largest mass mortality events in human history, and, increasingly, precision tools of medicine and molecular biology. This section works from the definition of a virus through structure, classification, replication, ecology, pathogenesis, and countermeasures, and closes with the acellular oddities that push the definition of "infectious agent" to its limits.

    1. What a Virus Is

    A virus is an obligate intracellular parasite consisting, at minimum, of a nucleic acid genome (DNA or RNA, single- or double-stranded, linear or circular, segmented or monopartite) enclosed in a protein shell. Viruses possess no ribosomes, no ATP-generating machinery, no capacity for protein synthesis, and — with rare exceptions among the giant viruses — no metabolic enzymes of their own. They replicate only by commandeering the biosynthetic apparatus of a living cell, redirecting host ribosomes, nucleotide pools, membranes, and energy currency toward the production of progeny particles.

    Two terms are frequently conflated but should be kept apart. The virion is the extracellular, metabolically inert infectious particle — the transmission vehicle, essentially a delivery device for a genome. The virus is better understood as the intracellular replicating entity: once the genome enters the cell, the virion no longer exists, and what proceeds is a reprogrammed cell (some virologists call it the "virocell") producing viral gene products. Under this framing, the virion is to the virus roughly what a spore or seed is to an organism, and the "is it alive?" question changes character depending on which stage you interrogate.

    The "are viruses alive?" debate

    Arguments on both sides of the living/non-living question
    CriterionArgument that viruses are NOT aliveArgument that viruses ARE alive
    MetabolismNo ATP generation, no catabolism or anabolism; virions are chemically inert and can be crystallised like a saltThe virocell — the infected cell running viral programs — is fully metabolically active; giant viruses encode components of translation and even TCA-adjacent enzymes
    ReproductionCannot self-replicate; assembled by host machinery rather than dividingObligate intracellular bacteria (Chlamydia, Rickettsia) are equally dependent and are unhesitatingly called alive
    Cellular organisationNo membrane-bounded cell, no cytoplasm, no ribosomes — the universal ribosome criterion excludes themCell theory is a description of one successful strategy, not a definition of life; the ribosome criterion is arbitrary
    EvolutionEvolution alone is not life; prions and even computer viruses "evolve"Viruses have heritable genomes, undergo mutation, recombination and selection, form lineages, and possess deep phylogenies — the hallmark of living systems
    Homeostasis / responseNo irritability, no internal regulation, no growth in sizeComplex phage decision circuits (lysis vs lysogeny) sense and integrate environmental information
    OriginsEscaped genetic elements — derived from cells, therefore secondaryPossible relics of a pre-cellular RNA world, or reduced descendants of a fourth domain; either way, coeval with cellular life

    A pragmatic resolution. Most working virologists treat the question as semantic rather than empirical. Viruses are unambiguously biological: they carry genes, they evolve, they participate in ecosystems, and they share ancestry with cellular genes. Whether the word "alive" applies depends entirely on the definition chosen, and no definition has ever commanded consensus. The interesting biology lies elsewhere.

    Discovery: a timeline

    • 1886 — Adolf Mayer demonstrated that tobacco mosaic disease could be transmitted by rubbing sap from a diseased plant onto a healthy one, establishing an infectious agent but failing to culture or see any bacterium.
    • 1892 — Dmitri Ivanovsky passed tobacco mosaic sap through a Chamberland porcelain filter fine enough to retain all known bacteria; the filtrate remained infectious. Ivanovsky, conservatively, proposed a bacterial toxin or an unusually small bacterium.
    • 1898 — Martinus Beijerinck repeated the filtration independently and went further: the agent multiplied only in living, dividing plant tissue, diffused through agar, and could not be grown on any medium. He named it a contagium vivum fluidum — a "contagious living fluid" — and coined the modern use of "virus." The same year, Loeffler and Frosch showed foot-and-mouth disease of cattle was filterable, extending the concept to animals.
    • 1901 — Walter Reed and the U.S. Army Yellow Fever Commission identified yellow fever as the first filterable agent of human disease, transmitted by Aedes aegypti.
    • 1911 — Peyton Rous transmitted a chicken sarcoma with a cell-free filtrate, the first demonstration of an oncogenic virus (Rous sarcoma virus); his Nobel came 55 years later, in 1966.
    • 1915–1917 — Frederick Twort and Félix d'Herelle independently described agents that lysed bacterial cultures and produced clear plaques on lawns. D'Herelle named them bacteriophages ("bacteria eaters"), recognised their particulate nature from plaque counting, and immediately proposed therapeutic use.
    • 1935 — Wendell Stanley crystallised tobacco mosaic virus, showing that an infectious agent could be handled as a pure chemical substance; Bawden and Pirie showed shortly after that the crystals were nucleoprotein, not pure protein. Stanley shared the 1946 Nobel Prize in Chemistry.
    • 1939 onward — the electron microscope made virions visible (Kausche, Pfankuch and Ruska imaged TMV in 1939), and the Delbrück–Luria–Hershey "phage group" turned bacteriophages into the organism of choice for molecular genetics, delivering the Luria–Delbrück fluctuation test (1943) and the Hershey–Chase experiment (1952) that established DNA as the genetic material.
    A typical virion is 20–300 nm across — a poliovirus at ~30 nm is about a thousandth the diameter of a human hair, and roughly 50 poliovirions would fit end-to-end across a single E. coli cell.

    2. Virion Architecture

    The virion's job is to protect a genome in a hostile extracellular environment, recognise the correct host cell, and deliver the genome across a membrane or wall. Every structural feature follows from these three imperatives, and the economy of viral genomes forces an elegant solution: build the shell from many copies of a small number of protein subunits, each encoded by a short gene. Crick and Watson predicted this in 1956 on purely genetic-economy grounds, before any virus structure was solved.

    Capsids, capsomeres and symmetry

    The protein shell is the capsid, assembled from repeating subunits called protomers that cluster into morphological units, the capsomeres (pentamers at vertices, hexamers on faces). Capsid plus enclosed genome is the nucleocapsid. Because identical subunits must pack with equivalent or quasi-equivalent bonding, only two symmetry classes are geometrically economical.

    Helical symmetry. Protomers stack in a helix around the genome, which threads along a groove in the protein. Tobacco mosaic virus is the canonical example: 2130 identical coat protein subunits arranged in a rigid rod 300 nm long and 18 nm wide, 16⅓ subunits per turn, its 6.4 kb (+)ssRNA genome wound between them with three nucleotides per subunit. Helical capsid length is dictated by genome length, not by the protein. In animal viruses helical nucleocapsids are typically flexible and coiled inside an envelope — influenza, measles, rabies, Ebola. Rabies lyssavirus and other rhabdoviruses give this a distinctive twist: the helical nucleocapsid is condensed into a bullet-shaped, envelope-wrapped particle roughly 180 × 75 nm, rounded at one end and flat at the other. Filoviruses (Ebola, Marburg) take the opposite route, producing filaments up to 14 μm long that loop into the "shepherd's crook" shape seen in electron micrographs.

    Icosahedral symmetry. The icosahedron — 20 triangular faces, 12 vertices, 30 edges, with 2-, 3- and 5-fold rotational axes — is the closed shell that encloses the most volume for the least protein while allowing all subunits equivalent contacts. The minimal icosahedral capsid uses 60 identical subunits (three per face). Larger shells require quasi-equivalence, formalised by Caspar and Klug (1962) in the triangulation number T, where the number of subunits is 60T and T = h² + hk + k² for non-negative integers h, k. Permitted values are therefore 1, 3, 4, 7, 9, 12, 13, 16, 19, 21, 25…. Every icosahedral capsid has exactly 12 pentamers regardless of size; the rest are hexamers, of which there are 10(T − 1).

    Triangulation numbers of representative icosahedral viruses
    VirusT numberSubunits (60T)CapsomeresApprox. diameter
    Satellite tobacco necrosis virus16012 pentamers17 nm
    Poliovirus, rhinovirus (picornaviruses)pseudo-3 (P=3)60 protomers of VP1–VP4—28–30 nm
    Parvovirus B191601218–26 nm
    Hepatitis B core4 (and 3)240 (180)—32–36 nm
    Polyomavirus (JC, SV40)pseudo-7d360 VP1 in 72 pentamers72 (all pentameric)45 nm
    Papillomavirus (HPV)pseudo-7d360 L17255 nm
