    Bacteriophage HK97 head7420—65 nm
    Mimivirus~1141 (pseudo)——500 nm capsid, 750 nm with fibrils

    Complex and head–tail morphology. Many tailed bacteriophages (class Caudoviricetes, formerly the morphological groups Myoviridae, Siphoviridae, Podoviridae) combine an icosahedral or prolate head with a helical tail — a genuinely chimeric symmetry. Phage T4 is the archetype: a prolate T=13 head holding 169 kb of dsDNA, a contractile tail sheath, a baseplate with six short tail fibres and six long tail fibres, and tail pins. Poxviruses are "complex" in a different sense — brick-shaped particles ~300 × 250 × 200 nm with a biconcave core, lateral bodies and an outer membrane, with no recognisable symmetry at all.

    Enveloped versus naked, and why it matters clinically

    An envelope is a host-derived lipid bilayer acquired by budding through the plasma membrane (influenza, HIV, retroviruses), the ER or Golgi (coronaviruses, flaviviruses, bunyaviruses), or the inner nuclear membrane (herpesviruses). Embedded in it are virally encoded spike glycoproteins mediating receptor binding and membrane fusion — influenza haemagglutinin and neuraminidase, SARS-CoV-2 spike (S), HIV gp120/gp41, rabies G protein. Beneath the envelope, matrix proteins (influenza M1, HIV p17 MA, rhabdovirus M, paramyxovirus M) bridge the nucleocapsid to the membrane, drive budding, and give the particle mechanical coherence. Herpesviruses insert an additional protein-rich compartment, the tegument, between capsid and envelope, delivering pre-formed transactivators into the newly infected cell.

    Practical consequence. The envelope is a lipid bilayer and is destroyed by soaps, detergents, alcohols, drying and moderate heat. Enveloped viruses (influenza, SARS-CoV-2, HIV, RSV, herpesviruses) are therefore labile: they transmit best by respiratory droplet, direct mucosal contact, blood or sexual contact, and are readily inactivated by 60–80% ethanol hand rub. Naked (non-enveloped) viruses — norovirus, rotavirus, poliovirus and other enteroviruses, hepatitis A, adenovirus, parvovirus B19, HPV — have only a protein shell. They survive for days to months on fomites, tolerate stomach acid and bile (hence faecal–oral transmission), resist many detergents, and are poorly inactivated by alcohol gels. Norovirus and Clostridioides difficile spores are the classic reasons hospitals mandate soap-and-water handwashing and hypochlorite (bleach) surface disinfection during outbreaks.

    Genome packaging and the size extremes

    Genomes must be compressed to near-crystalline density — dsDNA inside a phage head reaches ~500 mg/mL and internal pressures of tens of atmospheres, achieved by ATP-driven terminase motors among the most powerful known molecular machines (phage φ29 generates ~60 pN). Other strategies include condensation by basic core proteins or polyamines, coating by nucleoprotein (all negative-strand RNA viruses keep their genome permanently sheathed in N protein), and segmentation, which distributes a large coding capacity across several small molecules (influenza A: 8 segments; rotavirus: 11; bunyaviruses: 3 — L, M, S).

    The size range of the virosphere
    VirusParticle sizeGenomeApprox. genesNote
    Circoviruses (e.g. porcine circovirus, TTV-like agents)17–22 nmssDNA circular, ~1.7–2.0 kb2–3Among the smallest autonomous animal viruses
    Hepatitis delta virus (satellite)36 nmssRNA circular, 1.7 kb1 (HDAg)Requires HBV surface antigen
    Poliovirus30 nm(+)ssRNA, 7.5 kb~11 (one polyprotein)Naked icosahedral
    Influenza A80–120 nm(−)ssRNA, 8 segments, 13.5 kb~11–14Enveloped, pleomorphic
    SARS-CoV-2~100 nm(+)ssRNA, ~30 kb~29 proteinsLargest RNA genomes known; proofreading nsp14

    Megavirus chilensis~440 nmdsDNA, 1.26 Mb~1120Amoeba host

    Pithovirus sibericum1.5 μm × 0.5 μmdsDNA, 610 kb~467Largest particle known; revived from 30,000-year-old Siberian permafrost (2014)
    Pandoravirus salinus1.0 μm × 0.5 μmdsDNA, 2.5 Mb~2500Largest viral genome; >90% of genes have no known homologue

    The giant viruses overlap in both particle size and coding capacity with small parasitic bacteria such as Mycoplasma genitalium (580 kb, ~470 genes) and Carsonella ruddii (160 kb), dissolving the old textbook claim that viruses are always smaller and simpler than cells.

    3. The Baltimore Classification

    Formal taxonomy (ICTV: realm → kingdom → phylum → class → order → family → genus → species) reflects evolutionary relationships. The scheme devised by David Baltimore in 1971 is orthogonal and arguably more useful for understanding replication: it groups viruses by the nature of the genome and the route taken to make mRNA. Every virus must produce mRNA readable by host ribosomes — that is, (+)-sense, capped or otherwise ribosome-competent RNA. Baltimore's insight was that the number of ways to get there is small.

    The seven Baltimore groups
    GroupGenomeRoute to mRNAReplication site & enzymesExample families and viruses
    IdsDNAHost DNA-dependent RNA polymerase II transcribes directly from one strand; no special conversion neededNucleus for most (herpes, adeno, papilloma, polyoma); poxviruses uniquely replicate in cytoplasm and carry their own RNA polymerase in the virionHerpesviridae (HSV-1/2, VZV, EBV, CMV, HHV-6/8), Adenoviridae, Papillomaviridae (HPV), Polyomaviridae (JC, BK, SV40), Poxviridae (variola, vaccinia, mpox), Caudoviricetes phages (T4, λ, T7), Mimiviridae
    IIssDNA (usually (+)-sense, circular or linear)Host polymerases convert to a dsDNA replicative form; transcription proceeds from thatNucleus; strictly dependent on host DNA polymerase, so requires S-phase cells (parvoviruses infect erythroid precursors and fetal tissue)Parvoviridae (parvovirus B19, adeno-associated virus — the AAV gene-therapy vector), Circoviridae, Anelloviridae (torque teno virus), Microviridae (phage φX174), Inoviridae (filamentous phage M13/fd)
    IIIdsRNA, usually segmentedVirion-packaged RNA-dependent RNA polymerase (RdRp) transcribes the (−) strand inside an intact core particle, extruding mRNACytoplasm, inside the never-fully-uncoated core — a strategy that hides dsRNA (a potent PAMP) from host RIG-I/MDA5 sensorsReoviridae/Sedoreoviridae (rotavirus, Colorado tick fever virus, bluetongue virus), Birnaviridae, Cystoviridae (phage φ6), Totiviridae (fungal killer viruses)
    IV(+)ssRNAGenome is mRNA — naked RNA is infectious.     V(−)ssRNA, segmented or non-segmentedGenome is complementary to mRNA and cannot be translated. Genome always encapsidated by N protein as a ribonucleoproteinNon-segmented (Mononegavirales): Rhabdoviridae (rabies, VSV), Filoviridae (Ebola, Marburg), Paramyxoviridae (measles, mumps, parainfluenza, Nipah, Hendra), Pneumoviridae (RSV, hMPV). Segmented: Orthomyxoviridae (influenza A/B/C, 8 segments), Arenaviridae (Lassa, ambisense), Hantaviridae, Nairoviridae (CCHF), Phenuiviridae (Rift Valley fever)
    VIssRNA with DNA intermediate (retroviruses)Reverse transcriptase copies (+)ssRNA → dsDNA; integrase inserts the provirus into host chromatin; host RNA Pol II then transcribes genomic RNA and mRNAsReverse transcription in cytoplasmic core; integration and transcription in nucleus. Two copies of the genome per virion (diploid), enabling high recombinationRetroviridae: HIV-1, HIV-2 (lentiviruses), HTLV-1/2 (deltaretroviruses), Rous sarcoma virus, murine leukaemia virus; endogenous retroviruses (HERVs)
    VIIdsDNA (gapped/partial) with RNA intermediate (pararetroviruses)Genome repaired to covalently closed circular DNA (cccDNA) and transcribed by host Pol II; the pregenomic RNA is then reverse-transcribed back into DNA inside assembling capsidscccDNA persists as an episomal minichromosome in the nucleus — the reason chronic hepatitis B is not cured by current antiviralsHepadnaviridae (hepatitis B virus), Caulimoviridae (cauliflower mosaic virus)

    Why the (−)-strand rule matters. Purified genomic RNA from a Group IV virus, transfected into a cell, initiates a full infection — it is functionally mRNA. Purified genomic RNA from a Group V virus does nothing, because no cell on Earth encodes an RNA-dependent RNA polymerase capable of reading it, and the naked RNA is not even a competent template until coated with nucleoprotein. This single distinction dictates virion composition (Group V virions are packed with polymerase complexes), the design of reverse-genetics systems, and the tractability of each group in the laboratory.

    4. The Replication Cycle

    All viral replication follows the same six-step logic — attachment, penetration, uncoating, synthesis, assembly, release — but each step is executed differently across the Baltimore groups, and the differences are exactly where antiviral drugs act.

    Attachment and receptor specificity

    Attachment is a lock-and-key interaction between a virion surface protein and a host cell surface molecule that the virus has co-opted as a receptor. This interaction is the primary determinant of tropism — which species, tissues and cell types a virus can infect — and therefore of pathogenesis. Many viruses also require a secondary co-receptor or an attachment factor (heparan sulphate, DC-SIGN) that concentrates particles before engagement of the true receptor.

    Receptor usage and the tropism it dictates
    VirusViral attachment proteinHost receptor (and co-receptor)Consequence for disease

    SARS-CoV-2Spike (S1 receptor-binding domain); primed by TMPRSS2 or cathepsin LACE2ACE2 on nasal, alveolar type II, intestinal and endothelial cells → respiratory plus multisystem disease; anosmia via sustentacular cells

    Penetration and uncoating

    • Receptor-mediated endocytosis — the commonest route for animal viruses. Clathrin-coated pits (influenza, VSV), caveolae (SV40), macropinocytosis (vaccinia, Ebola). Endosomal acidification then triggers a conformational change: influenza HA at pH ~5 springs its fusion peptide into the endosomal membrane, while the M2 ion channel acidifies the virion interior to release M1 from the ribonucleoproteins — the step blocked by amantadine.
    • Direct fusion at the plasma membrane — pH-independent fusion driven by class I fusion proteins (HIV gp41, paramyxovirus F, some coronaviruses where TMPRSS2 is abundant). Because F protein can fuse neighbouring uninfected cells, paramyxoviruses and HIV form multinucleate syncytia, a diagnostic cytopathic effect (Warthin–Finkeldey cells in measles).
    • Genome injection — tailed phages never enter the cell. T4 binds irreversibly via short tail fibres, the baseplate changes conformation, the sheath contracts, the rigid tail tube punctures the envelope with the help of a lysozyme-like domain, and only the DNA is delivered; the empty capsid ("ghost") remains outside. This was the physical basis of the Hershey–Chase experiment.
    • Uncoating — disassembly releasing the genome. Picornaviruses form a pore and extrude RNA through the capsid; HIV's cone-shaped core traffics to the nuclear pore and uncoats there or within the nucleus; reoviruses and poxviruses never fully uncoat, transcribing from within a subviral particle.

    Synthesis, assembly, maturation, release

    Genome replication and gene expression follow the Baltimore logic set out above. Two general regulatory patterns recur. DNA viruses and retroviruses use temporal cascades — herpesviruses express immediate-early (α) regulatory proteins, then early (β) replication enzymes such as the viral thymidine kinase and DNA polymerase, then, after genome replication, late (γ) structural proteins. RNA viruses instead regulate by polyprotein processing (picornaviruses, flaviviruses), by a transcription gradient (in Mononegavirales, the single 3′ entry site for the polymerase and stochastic detachment at each gene junction produce a steep 3′→5′ expression gradient: N > P > M > G > L), or by nested subgenomic mRNAs (coronaviruses).

    Assembly may be spontaneous self-assembly (TMV coat protein reconstitutes infectious rods in vitro, as Fraenkel-Conrat and Williams showed in 1955), scaffold-dependent (phage and herpesvirus procapsids assemble around a scaffolding protein that is then proteolysed and expelled before DNA packaging), or membrane-coupled. Maturation is often a discrete proteolytic step that converts a non-infectious immature particle into an infectious one — HIV protease cleaves Gag into MA, CA, NC and p6 after budding, condensing the conical core; picornavirus VP0 cleaves to VP4+VP2; flavivirus prM is cleaved by furin in the trans-Golgi. Protease inhibitors and the HIV maturation inhibitor lenacapavir-adjacent chemistry exploit precisely this.

    Release takes two forms. Lysis: naked viruses and most phages destroy the cell. Phages use a two-component holin/endolysin system — holin punches a hole in the inner membrane at a genetically timed moment, admitting endolysin to the peptidoglycan, followed by spanins that disrupt the outer membrane. Budding: enveloped viruses wrap themselves in membrane and exit without immediate cell death, permitting persistent, chronic infection. HIV recruits the host ESCRT machinery via late domains in p6; influenza neuraminidase cleaves sialic acid to prevent progeny from re-binding the cell surface and aggregating — the target of oseltamivir and zanamivir. Some viruses exit by non-lytic egress in vesicles ("quasi-enveloped" hepatitis A and E) or by direct cell-to-cell spread through virological synapses and actin tails.

    One-step growth: characteristic parameters
    VirusEclipse periodLatent/burst timingBurst size (progeny per cell)
    Phage T4 (E. coli, 37 °C)~12 minLysis at ~25–30 min100–200
    Poliovirus (HeLa)2–3 hLysis at 6–8 h104–105
    Influenza A~4 hContinuous budding from 6 h103–104
    Herpes simplex virus~6 hRelease 18–24 h~103–104
    HIV-1 (activated CD4+ T cell)~24 h (incl. integration)Continuous budding, cell dies in ~2 days~103–5×104 over lifetime

    Lytic and lysogenic cycles

    Phages fall into two behavioural classes. Virulent phages (T4, T7) can only replicate lytically. Temperate phages (λ, P22, Mu, CTXφ) choose between lysis and lysogeny, in which the genome is maintained quiescently — integrated into the host chromosome as a prophage (λ at the attB site between gal and bio, via Int-mediated site-specific recombination) or, in some phages such as P1, as a stable plasmid. The lysogenic bacterium replicates the prophage passively at every division and is immune to superinfection by the same phage.

    The lambda decision circuit is the textbook genetic switch. Two repressors compete for the operators OR1, OR2 and OR3 in the divergent PRM/PR region. The cI repressor, a dimer, binds OR1 and OR2 cooperatively: this shuts off PR (blocking Cro and the lytic genes) while simultaneously activating its own promoter PRM — positive autoregulation that locks in lysogeny. At high concentration cI also occupies OR3 and represses itself, damping the loop. Cro binds the same operators in the opposite order of affinity, preferring OR3 first, thereby switching off PRM and committing the cell to lysis. The initial tilt is set by CII (a transcriptional activator of the cI-establishment promoter PRE and of int), whose stability depends on the host protease HflB/FtsH and hence on nutritional state: starved cells with high cAMP and low protease activity favour lysogeny; well-fed cells favour lysis. A high multiplicity of infection also favours lysogeny.

    Induction occurs when DNA damage (UV, mitomycin C, fluoroquinolones) activates the SOS response; RecA* stimulates autocleavage of cI (and of LexA), PR fires, Xis and Int excise the prophage, and the lytic programme begins — the phage abandoning a doomed host. Certain Bacillus phages add a quorum-like layer: the arbitrium system, in which infected cells secrete a small peptide (AimP) whose accumulation signals to later infections that hosts are scarce, biasing them toward lysogeny.

    Transduction and phage conversion

    • Generalised transduction — a packaging error in a lytic phage (e.g. P22 in Salmonella, P1 in E. coli) encapsidates a random fragment of host DNA; the resulting particle injects bacterial rather than phage DNA into a new cell, where it may recombine. Any chromosomal marker can be moved; this underpinned classic bacterial genetic mapping.
    • Specialised transduction — imprecise excision of an integrated prophage carries adjacent host genes (λ picking up gal or bio), producing defective transducing particles that transfer only those loci.
    • Lysogenic (phage) conversion — the prophage supplies genes that change the host phenotype, frequently converting a benign organism into a pathogen. Named examples: cholera toxin is encoded by ctxAB on the filamentous prophage CTXφ of Vibrio cholerae (which uses the toxin-coregulated pilus as its receptor, so toxin production and phage uptake are coupled); diphtheria toxin is encoded by tox on corynephage β of Corynebacterium diphtheriae, with expression repressed by iron-bound DtxR; Shiga toxins 1 and 2 of E. coli O157:H7 are carried by lambdoid prophages whose induction by fluoroquinolones is the reason antibiotics are contraindicated in EHEC infection; botulinum toxin type C/D, streptococcal pyrogenic exotoxins (scarlet fever), staphylococcal Panton–Valentine leukocidin and enterotoxin A, and Salmonella SopE are all prophage-encoded.

    Persistence in animal viruses

    Patterns of animal virus infection
    PatternDefinitionExamples

    Chronic / persistentContinuous low-level production for yearsHBV, HCV, HIV (ongoing replication), LCMV in mice

    SlowVery long incubation then progressive fatal diseaseSSPE after measles (5–10 y), PML from JC virus, HIV pre-ART
    Transforming / oncogenicImmortalisation via viral oncoproteins or insertional activationHPV-16/18 E6 (degrades p53) and E7 (inactivates Rb) → cervical cancer; HBV/HCV → hepatocellular carcinoma; EBV → Burkitt, nasopharyngeal, Hodgkin; HHV-8 → Kaposi sarcoma; HTLV-1 Tax → adult T-cell leukaemia; Merkel cell polyomavirus. Roughly 12–15% of human cancers are attributable to infection

    5. Bacteriophages and the Virosphere

    Viruses are the most numerous biological entities on the planet by an enormous margin. Current estimates put the global phage population at roughly 1031 particles — about ten times the number of bacterial and archaeal cells, and, laid end to end, a column extending some 200 million light years. Seawater typically contains 106–108 virus-like particles per millilitre, soil rather more per gram. Phages initiate on the order of 1023 infections every second worldwide and turn over roughly 20–40% of the ocean's bacterial biomass each day.

    The viral shunt and biogeochemistry

    Lysis by marine phages diverts carbon and nutrients away from the classical grazing food chain and back into the dissolved organic matter pool, where it is reassimilated by surviving bacteria — the viral shunt. This releases an estimated 3 gigatonnes of carbon per year from lysed cells, retains iron, nitrogen and phosphorus in the euphotic zone rather than exporting them to the deep sea, and simultaneously supplies the "viral shuttle" of aggregated cell debris that sinks as marine snow. Phages also structure microbial communities through "kill-the-winner" dynamics: any bloom-forming clone becomes a dense target and is culled, sustaining diversity. Cyanophages infecting Prochlorococcus and Synechococcus carry auxiliary metabolic genes — including psbA, encoding the photosystem II D1 protein — so that infected cells keep photosynthesising to fuel phage production; a measurable fraction of oceanic oxygen production runs on phage-encoded proteins.

    Phage therapy

    D'Herelle used phages therapeutically from 1919, treating dysentery in children in Paris and cholera in India. Western interest collapsed after antibiotics arrived in the 1940s, partly because early preparations were poorly characterised and trials uncontrolled. The tradition persisted in the Soviet sphere: the Eliava Institute in Tbilisi, Georgia (founded 1923 with d'Herelle's participation) and the Hirszfeld Institute in Wrocław, Poland have administered phage preparations continuously for a century, including commercial fixed cocktails (Pyophage, Intestiphage). The antibiotic-resistance crisis has revived interest, and modern practice is dominated by personalised compassionate use: the 2016 rescue of Tom Patterson at UC San Diego from a pan-resistant Acinetobacter baumannii infection using intravenous phage cocktails (leading to the founding of IPATH, the first US phage therapy centre); the 2018 treatment of a cystic fibrosis patient in London with engineered mycobacteriophages against Mycobacterium abscessus; and a growing set of registered trials in diabetic foot ulcer, prosthetic joint infection and Pseudomonas lung disease. The attractions are exquisite specificity (sparing the microbiome), self-amplification at the site of infection, and activity on biofilms; the difficulties are narrow host range demanding matching, rapid bacterial resistance, neutralising antibodies on repeat dosing, endotoxin in lysates, and regulatory frameworks built for fixed-composition drugs. Phage-derived enzymes (endolysins such as exebacase) are advancing as a more conventional alternative.

    Phages as tools, and the origin of CRISPR

    • Phage display (George Smith, 1985; Greg Winter's antibody application; Nobel Prize in Chemistry 2018) fuses peptide or antibody libraries to the pIII coat protein of filamentous phage M13, linking phenotype to the genotype inside the particle and permitting affinity selection ("panning") from libraries of 109–1011 variants. Adalimumab, the best-selling drug in history, was isolated this way.
    • Molecular biology's toolkit is largely phage-derived: T4 DNA ligase, T7 and SP6 RNA polymerases (used to transcribe mRNA vaccines), T4 polynucleotide kinase, λ Red recombineering, Cre–lox from phage P1, integrases from φC31 and Bxb1 used in genome writing.
    • CRISPR–Cas is a prokaryotic adaptive immune system against phages and plasmids: spacers acquired from invader DNA are transcribed and guide Cas nucleases to cleave matching sequences. Its phage origin was recognised in 2005 when three groups noticed spacer sequences matched phage genomes, confirmed experimentally by Barrangou and Horvath in Streptococcus thermophilus (2007) in the yoghurt industry. Phages counter with anti-CRISPR (Acr) proteins, and the arms race continues. Doudna and Charpentier's 2012 repurposing of Cas9 won the 2020 Nobel Prize in Chemistry.
    • Restriction–modification systems, likewise anti-phage defences, supplied the restriction enzymes that made recombinant DNA possible. Newer defence systems — retrons, Thoeris, Gabija, CBASS, cyclic-oligonucleotide signalling, and prokaryotic homologues of the cGAS–STING and viperin pathways — reveal that innate immunity in animals is partly inherited from bacterial anti-phage machinery.

    The virome

    Metagenomic sequencing has revealed that the human body carries a persistent virome: gut bacteriophages (dominated by Caudoviricetes and the ubiquitous crAss-like phages of Bacteroides, discovered computationally in 2014 and now known to be the most abundant human gut virus), anelloviruses in blood at levels tracking immunosuppression, papillomaviruses on skin, and endogenous retroviral transcripts. Most of these are commensal or asymptomatic. Global virome surveys suggest that of an estimated 108–109 viral species, fewer than 12,000 have been formally described — the least-catalogued major component of the biosphere.

    7. Antigenic Variation, Evolution and Emergence

    Drift versus shift

    Antigenic drift is the gradual accumulation of point mutations in the antibody-binding epitopes of haemagglutinin and neuraminidase. Influenza's RdRp lacks proofreading, giving an error rate near 10−4–10−5 substitutions per nucleotide per replication — roughly one mutation per genome per copy. Under immune selection, variants that escape existing antibodies sweep through the population. Drift is why the seasonal vaccine is reformulated annually by WHO consultation (February for the Northern Hemisphere, September for the Southern) and why immunity from last year's infection is incomplete.

    Antigenic shift is abrupt and discontinuous: the appearance of a virus bearing an HA (and often NA) subtype to which the human population is essentially naive. It arises by reassortment — when two influenza A viruses co-infect one cell, their eight segments are shuffled into progeny in any of 28 = 256 combinations. Pigs, expressing both α2,3- and α2,6-linked sialic acids, serve as classic "mixing vessels," though reassortment also occurs in birds and humans. Shift can also occur by direct adaptation of a wholly avian virus. Only influenza A undergoes shift, because only influenza A has a broad animal reservoir — wild aquatic birds harbour essentially all HA (H1–H16) and NA (N1–N9) subtypes.

    Influenza A pandemics of the modern era
    YearNameSubtypeOrigin of segmentsEstimated deathsNotable feature

    1957–58"Asian" fluH2N2Reassortment: avian HA, NA and PB1 into the human H1N1 backbone1–4 millionH2N2 later disappeared entirely from humans; anyone born after 1968 is naive
    1968–69"Hong Kong" fluH3N2Reassortment: avian HA and PB1 into H2N21–4 millionRetained N2, so partial immunity limited severity; H3N2 still circulates

    2009–10"Swine" fluH1N1pdm09Quadruple reassortant: North American swine triple-reassortant (itself avian + human + swine) plus Eurasian avian-like swine NA and M150,000–575,000Older adults partly protected by pre-1957 H1N1 exposure; now a seasonal strain
    Ongoing threatAvian influenzaH5N1 clade 2.3.4.4b, H7N9, H5N6Wholly avian; panzootic in wild birds, poultry, and since 2024 in dairy cattle (H5N1 in milk)—High CFR in sporadic human cases but no sustained human-to-human transmission; α2,3 binding preference is the barrier

    Mutation rates and quasispecies

    RNA-dependent RNA polymerases and reverse transcriptases lack 3′→5′ exonuclease proofreading, so RNA viruses mutate 103–106 times faster than their hosts. A consequence is that an RNA virus population within a single host is not a clone but a quasispecies — a mutant swarm clustered around a consensus sequence, on which selection acts collectively. This explains the speed of drug-resistance emergence (HIV generates every possible single point mutant many times over each day, which is precisely why combination ART is required), immune escape, and host-range jumps. It also sets a limit: genomes sit near the error threshold, above which information is lost to "error catastrophe" — the rationale for lethal mutagenesis by favipiravir and molnupiravir. Coronaviruses are the exception among RNA viruses, encoding an nsp14 exoribonuclease proofreader that lowers their error rate enough to permit their uniquely large ~30 kb genomes (and which confers partial resistance to nucleoside analogues, so remdesivir needed careful design to evade excision).

    Approximate mutation rates by genome type
    Genome classSubstitutions per site per replicationSubstitutions per site per year (evolutionary rate)Example
    dsDNA virus (host polymerase, proofreading)10−8–10−7~10−8–10−6Herpesviruses, papillomaviruses (co-diverge with hosts)
    ssDNA virus~10−610−4–10−3Parvovirus, circovirus
    Coronavirus (with nsp14 proofreading)~10−6~10−3SARS-CoV-2 (~2 mutations/month in the pandemic)
    Other RNA viruses10−5–10−410−3–10−2Influenza, poliovirus, HCV, foot-and-mouth disease virus
    Retrovirus (reverse transcriptase)~3 × 10−5~10−3HIV-1 — ~1010 virions produced daily in untreated infection

    Spillover, reservoirs and One Health

    Roughly 60–75% of emerging human infectious diseases are zoonotic, and the majority of those are RNA viruses — a bias explained by high mutation rates, rapid adaptation, and the lack of a required nuclear stage. Spillover requires a chain of conditions: reservoir infection prevalence, contact opportunity, sufficient dose, receptor compatibility, and the ability to evade the new host's innate immunity. Drivers include land-use change and deforestation, bushmeat hunting and the wildlife trade, intensive livestock production, encroachment on bat roosts, and climate-driven shifts in vector range.

    Bats are disproportionate reservoirs — hosts of rabies and other lyssaviruses, Marburg, likely Ebola, Nipah, Hendra, SARS-CoV and SARS-CoV-2 progenitors, and MERS-CoV ancestors. Explanations advanced include their exceptional species diversity (~20% of all mammals), dense colonial roosting, long lifespans relative to body size, and flight-associated physiology: the metabolic demands of flight are thought to have selected for dampened inflammatory responses (loss or attenuation of the PYHIN/AIM2 inflammasome locus, dampened STING signalling, constitutively expressed interferon-α in some species). The net effect is immunological tolerance — bats carry high viral loads with minimal immunopathology, but that same tolerance may select for viruses that replicate fast and therefore hit naive hosts hard. Other important reservoirs include rodents (hantaviruses, arenaviruses), birds (influenza, West Nile), primates (HIV from SIVcpz and SIVsmm), and camels (MERS).

    One Health is the operational response: an approach recognising that human, animal and environmental health are inseparable, and coordinating surveillance, veterinary vaccination, wildlife monitoring and antimicrobial stewardship across those domains. Its successes are concrete — canine rabies vaccination has eliminated dog-mediated human rabies from most of the Americas; Hendra virus vaccination of horses in Australia broke the transmission chain to humans; culling and surveillance of pigs contained the 1998–99 Nipah outbreak in Malaysia.

    8. Antivirals and Vaccines

    Antiviral chemotherapy is intrinsically harder than antibacterial chemotherapy: viruses use host machinery, so there are far fewer uniquely viral targets to exploit. The successful drug classes all attack a step that is virus-encoded.

    R0 and herd immunity thresholds

    The basic reproduction number R0 is the average number of secondary cases generated by one infectious individual in a wholly susceptible population. The herd immunity threshold — the proportion that must be immune to drive the effective reproduction number below 1 — is approximately 1 − 1/R0, adjusted upward for imperfect vaccine efficacy (required coverage = (1 − 1/R0) / vaccine effectiveness) and for heterogeneous mixing.

    Transmissibility and herd immunity thresholds

    Diphtheria6–7~85%~95%~85%

    Smallpox eradication and the polio endgame

    Smallpox remains the only human disease ever eradicated. The WHO Intensified Eradication Programme, launched in 1967 under D.A. Henderson, succeeded not by mass vaccination alone but by surveillance and containment — ring vaccination around each detected case. The biology cooperated: no animal reservoir, no latency or chronic carriage, a distinctive rash making case-finding reliable, a stable virus with no antigenic variation, and a robust, heat-tolerant freeze-dried vaccine deliverable by bifurcated needle. The last natural case was Ali Maow Maalin in Somalia (October 1977); the last case overall was Janet Parker, a medical photographer infected in a 1978 laboratory accident in Birmingham. The World Health Assembly declared eradication on 8 May 1980. Known stocks remain at the CDC in Atlanta and VECTOR in Koltsovo, Russia; the debate over their destruction is unresolved, and mpox's spread in a population no longer vaccinated against orthopoxviruses is a live reminder of waning population immunity.

    Polio has proved harder. Wild poliovirus type 2 was declared eradicated in 2015 and type 3 in 2019; wild type 1 persists only in Afghanistan and Pakistan. The complications are instructive: most infections are asymptomatic (so surveillance requires environmental sewage sampling and acute flaccid paralysis investigation rather than clinical case-finding), and the oral vaccine's live attenuated strains can revert during prolonged circulation in under-immunised populations, producing circulating vaccine-derived polioviruses (cVDPV) that now cause more paralysis worldwide than wild virus. The endgame strategy is a coordinated switch from OPV to IPV, deployment of the genetically stabilised novel OPV2 (nOPV2), containment of laboratory stocks, and eventual cessation of all OPV use — the last steps of which are genuinely difficult, because IPV protects the individual against paralysis but induces weaker intestinal immunity than OPV and so does less to stop transmission.

    9. Viroids, Satellites and Prions

    Below viruses lie agents stripped even further — naked RNA with no protein at all, RNAs parasitic on other viruses, and, most radically, an infectious agent with no nucleic acid whatsoever.

    Viroids

    Viroids are the smallest known infectious agents: naked, covalently closed circular single-stranded RNA molecules of only 246–401 nucleotides, encoding no protein at all. They have no capsid, no envelope and no genes in the conventional sense; their entire pathogenic effect derives from the RNA's structure and its interactions with host machinery. All known viroids infect plants. They were discovered by Theodor Diener in 1971, who showed that the agent of potato spindle tuber disease was a low-molecular-weight RNA insensitive to protein-degrading treatments — work that first established sub-viral pathogens as a category.

    Viroid RNA folds into a rod-like or branched secondary structure of alternating base-paired and loop regions, stabilised extensively enough to resist cellular ribonucleases. Two families are recognised:

    • Pospiviroidae — rod-shaped, containing a central conserved region, replicating in the nucleus via an asymmetric rolling-circle mechanism: host DNA-dependent RNA polymerase II is redirected to accept an RNA template, producing a longer-than-unit-length (−) multimer, which templates (+) multimers that are cleaved by host RNase III-type enzymes and circularised by DNA ligase 1 acting on RNA. Members: Potato spindle tuber viroid (PSTVd, the type species, 359 nt), Citrus exocortis viroid (CEVd, causing bark scaling and stunting in citrus), Coconut cadang-cadang viroid (CCCVd, at 246 nt the smallest, which has killed tens of millions of coconut palms in the Philippines and is invariably fatal over 8–15 years), Chrysanthemum stunt viroid, Hop stunt viroid, Tomato apical stunt viroid.
    • Avsunviroidae — branched, lacking a central conserved region, replicating in the chloroplast via a symmetric rolling circle using the nuclear-encoded chloroplast RNA polymerase. Crucially, their RNA contains hammerhead ribozyme motifs in both polarities, so the multimeric intermediates self-cleave without any protein enzyme — among the clearest surviving demonstrations of RNA catalysis and a key piece of evidence cited for viroids as "living fossils" of a pre-cellular RNA world. Members: Avocado sunblotch viroid (ASBVd), Peach latent mosaic viroid, Chrysanthemum chlorotic mottle viroid.

    Pathogenesis appears to work largely through RNA silencing: viroid-derived small RNAs generated by the host Dicer machinery misdirect RISC to cleave host mRNAs, producing the stunting, epinasty, chlorosis and fruit distortion characteristic of infection. Transmission is mechanical — contaminated pruning tools, grafting, handling — and via seed and pollen in some species, which makes certified pathogen-free planting stock the mainstay of control. There is no treatment; eradication of infected stock is the standard response, and PSTVd is a quarantine pathogen in most jurisdictions.

    Virusoids, satellites and hepatitis delta

    Satellite nucleic acids depend on a co-infecting helper virus for replication or encapsidation. Virusoids (satellite RNAs) are small circular ssRNAs resembling viroids but packaged inside the capsid of a helper plant virus — for example the satellite RNA of tobacco ringspot virus, and rice yellow mottle virusoid. Some carry hammerhead ribozymes. Satellite viruses go one step further and encode their own coat protein while still requiring a helper for replication: satellite tobacco necrosis virus, and adeno-associated virus (AAV), which requires adenovirus or herpesvirus co-infection for productive replication — a dependency that makes recombinant AAV such a safe and now widely licensed gene-therapy vector (Luxturna, Zolgensma, Hemgenix).

    Hepatitis delta virus is the only known human satellite. Its genome is a 1,679-nucleotide circular ssRNA of negative polarity that folds into an unbranched rod — structurally viroid-like — but it encodes one protein, the hepatitis delta antigen, in two forms produced by ADAR1-mediated RNA editing of the amber stop codon: small HDAg (195 aa) promotes replication, large HDAg (214 aa, prenylated) shuts replication down and drives packaging. HDV replicates by rolling circle using host RNA polymerase II and self-cleaving delta ribozymes, but it cannot make an envelope of its own: it borrows the HBsAg surface protein of hepatitis B. Consequently HDV infects only HBV-positive people, either as co-infection (usually acute, sometimes fulminant) or superinfection of a chronic HBV carrier (frequently progressing to rapid cirrhosis). Around 12–20 million people are affected; HDV causes the most aggressive form of chronic viral hepatitis. It is fully prevented by hepatitis B vaccination, and the entry inhibitor bulevirtide, which blocks NTCP, is the first specifically licensed therapy.

    Prions

    A prion (proteinaceous infectious particle) is an infectious agent composed solely of protein, propagating by templating a conformational change in a normal host protein. The protein-only hypothesis was proposed in outline by Tikvah Alper and J.S. Griffith in the 1960s, after Alper showed that the scrapie agent resisted UV and ionising radiation doses far beyond those that destroy any nucleic acid of comparable target size. Stanley Prusiner purified the agent, coined the term "prion" in Science in 1982, identified PrP as its sole component, and received the 1997 Nobel Prize in Physiology or Medicine. Definitive confirmation came from the demonstration that Prnp-knockout mice are entirely resistant to scrapie inoculation, and later from the generation of infectious prions de novo from recombinant protein.

    The normal cellular protein PrPC is a GPI-anchored cell-surface glycoprotein of 209 amino acids, expressed most highly in neurons, rich in α-helix (~42%) with little β-sheet (~3%), protease-sensitive and detergent-soluble. Its physiological role remains incompletely defined but includes copper binding, myelin maintenance in peripheral nerves, and synaptic function. The disease-associated isoform PrPSc has an identical amino acid sequence but a radically different fold — predominantly β-sheet (~43%) with reduced α-helix (~30%), arranged in parallel in-register β-solenoid stacks. PrPSc is protease-resistant, insoluble in detergents, and aggregates into amyloid fibrils and plaques. Conversion is autocatalytic: PrPSc acts as a template that recruits PrPC and refolds it, fibrils fragment to create new templating ends, and the process propagates exponentially through the nervous system, producing spongiform vacuolation, neuronal loss and astrogliosis with no inflammatory infiltrate and no antibody response — the immune system does not recognise the agent as foreign, since the sequence is self.

    Decontamination. Prions are extraordinarily resistant. Standard autoclaving (121 °C, 15 min), dry heat, formalin fixation, ethanol, ionising radiation and UV all fail — indeed formalin fixation stabilises infectivity, and formalin-fixed brain tissue has remained infectious for decades. Recommended inactivation is 1 M sodium hydroxide or 20,000 ppm sodium hypochlorite for 1 hour, followed by porous-load autoclaving at 134 °C for 18 minutes, or incineration. Single-use instruments are mandated for high-risk neurosurgical and ophthalmic procedures on suspected cases; there is no way to sterilise a standard endoscope of prion contamination with confidence.

    Transmissible spongiform encephalopathies
