    Transmissible mink encephalopathy, feline SE, exotic ungulate SEMink, cats, zoo ungulatesFeed-borne, mostly BSE-derivedHistorical; largely resolved by feed controls

    Strains and the species barrier. A protein-only agent nonetheless exhibits distinct, heritable "strains" — differing in incubation period, brain lesion profile and PrPSc glycoform/protease-cleavage pattern. The information is encoded conformationally: different stable folds of the same polypeptide propagate faithfully. Transmission between species is inefficient when the donor and recipient PrP sequences differ (the species barrier), and human susceptibility is modulated by the methionine/valine polymorphism at PRNP codon 129.

    Non-pathogenic prions. Yeast prions demonstrate that conformational inheritance is a general biological phenomenon rather than a pathology. [PSI+] is the prion form of the translation termination factor Sup35, whose aggregation causes stop-codon readthrough and unmasks cryptic genetic variation, potentially acting as an evolutionary capacitor; [URE3] is the prion form of Ure2, altering nitrogen catabolite repression; [Het-s] in the fungus Podospora anserina functions in heterokaryon incompatibility — a prion with an evident physiological role. In mammals, the cytoplasmic polyadenylation element binding protein CPEB has a functional prion-like domain implicated in the maintenance of long-term synaptic facilitation, and self-templating prion-like propagation is now recognised in the spread of tau, α-synuclein, Aβ and TDP-43 through neural networks in Alzheimer's, Parkinson's and ALS — "prion-like" but not, on current evidence, transmissible between individuals under natural conditions.

    Diagnosis. Definitive diagnosis historically required brain biopsy or autopsy with immunohistochemistry and Western blot for protease-resistant PrP. Surrogate CSF markers (14-3-3, total tau, neuron-specific enolase) lack specificity. The transformative advance is RT-QuIC (real-time quaking-induced conversion), an amplification assay analogous in principle to PCR but operating on protein: recombinant PrP substrate is seeded with CSF, nasal brushing or skin biopsy material and subjected to cycles of shaking and incubation, with fibril formation read out in real time by thioflavin T fluorescence. RT-QuIC on CSF achieves greater than 92% sensitivity and near 99% specificity for sporadic CJD and is now incorporated into diagnostic criteria; olfactory mucosa brushings perform even better. Comparable seed-amplification assays for α-synuclein are entering routine use in Parkinson's disease.

    Endogenous retroviruses and domesticated viral genes

    Roughly 8% of the human genome consists of endogenous retroviruses (HERVs) — the fossilised remains of retroviral infections of germline cells in our ancestors, fixed by drift or selection and inherited ever since. That is around four times the ~2% occupied by protein-coding exons. Together with LINEs, SINEs and DNA transposons, mobile and virus-derived elements make up nearly half of human DNA. Most HERVs are inactivated by mutation and epigenetic silencing; HERV-K (HML-2) is the youngest group, with some proviruses polymorphic in the population and a few retaining intact open reading frames.

    Far from being inert junk, these sequences have been repeatedly domesticated (exaptation). The most striking example is syncytin: syncytin-1 (from HERV-W) and syncytin-2 (from HERV-FRD) are retroviral env genes captured by primate ancestors and now essential for placental development, where their fusogenic activity drives trophoblast cells to fuse into the multinucleate syncytiotrophoblast that forms the maternal–fetal interface. Their immunosuppressive domains may also contribute to maternal tolerance of the fetus. Remarkably, functionally analogous but non-orthologous syncytins have been captured independently in mice, rabbits, carnivores, ruminants and tenrecs — convergent domestication of retroviral fusion machinery on at least ten separate occasions, implying that placental mammals as a group owe a defining organ to viral genes. Other domestications include the Arc gene, derived from a Ty3/Gypsy retrotransposon Gag and forming virus-like capsids that transfer RNA between neurons; RAG1/RAG2, the V(D)J recombinases underpinning adaptive immunity, derived from a Transib DNA transposon; and thousands of HERV LTRs repurposed as promoters, enhancers and interferon-inducible regulatory elements.

    Giant viruses, virophages and the fourth-domain debate

    The 2003 recognition that a "coccus" isolated from a cooling tower in Bradford in 1992 was in fact a 750-nm virus — Acanthamoeba polyphaga mimivirus, Gram-stainable and visible by light microscopy — opened an unexpected chapter. Mimivirus and its relatives (Megavirus, Pandoravirus, Pithovirus, Mollivirus, Tupanvirus, Medusavirus, the marine Cafeteria roenbergensis virus) belong to the phylum Nucleocytoviricota, the nucleocytoplasmic large DNA viruses. They encode functions previously thought unique to cells: multiple aminoacyl-tRNA synthetases, translation initiation and elongation factors, tRNAs, chaperones, DNA repair enzymes, and in Tupanvirus a nearly complete translation apparatus lacking only ribosomes. They build a cytoplasmic "viral factory" that behaves rather like a transient nucleus.

    Virophages are viruses that parasitise these factories. Sputnik (2008), a 50-nm icosahedral dsDNA virus, replicates only in cells co-infected with mamavirus, hijacking the giant virus's factory, reducing its yield and producing deformed, poorly infectious mimivirus particles — a genuine parasite of a virus. Others include Mavirus (which integrates into the host protist genome and functions as an inducible antiviral defence, an arrangement strikingly parallel to prophage-mediated immunity and possibly a precursor of eukaryotic DNA transposons) and Zamilon. Mimivirus in turn defends itself with MIMIVIRE, a nucleic-acid-based defence system with CRISPR-like logic.

    The fourth-domain debate. Some researchers — notably Didier Raoult and Jean-Michel Claverie — argued that the giant viruses' translation-related gene complement and their deep, self-consistent phylogenies indicate descent from an ancient cellular lineage that underwent reductive evolution, warranting a fourth domain of life alongside Bacteria, Archaea and Eukarya (the "TRUC" proposal). The mainstream counter-argument holds that the shared genes are better explained by extensive horizontal transfer from amoebal hosts and their bacterial endosymbionts, that phylogenies rooted in fast-evolving viral proteins are prone to long-branch attraction, and that giant viruses cluster robustly within the Nucleocytoviricota as elaborations of ordinary large DNA viruses rather than as reduced cells. The debate has not been settled, but it has permanently changed the framing: the boundary between virus and cell is a gradient, and the virosphere is now understood as a reservoir of genetic innovation that has repeatedly written itself into the genomes of every domain of life — including our own.

    Smallest genome

    Coconut cadang-cadang viroid: 246 nucleotides of naked circular RNA, no protein, yet lethal to coconut palms over a decade.

    Largest genome

    Pandoravirus salinus: 2.5 Mb and ~2,500 predicted genes — more than many free-living bacteria, and over 90% without recognisable homologues.

    No genome at all

    Prions: infectivity carried entirely in protein conformation, resisting autoclaving, formalin and radiation, with incubation periods exceeding fifty years.

    Most abundant

    Bacteriophages: ~1031 particles globally, driving ~1023 infections per second and recycling gigatonnes of carbon each year through the viral shunt.

    Fungi & Microfungi

    Fungi are eukaryotes that eat by digesting the world outside themselves and then absorbing the pieces. They build their walls from chitin rather than cellulose, stiffen their membranes with ergosterol rather than cholesterol, store carbohydrate as glycogen, and — despite centuries of being filed alongside plants — sit on the animal branch of the tree of life. Most fungi are microscopic: single-celled yeasts a few micrometres across, moulds whose entire body is a mesh of threads thinner than a spider's silk, obligate intracellular parasites that have shed their mitochondria, and anaerobic gut fungi that never see oxygen. Somewhere between 2.2 and 3.8 million species are thought to exist; roughly 155,000 have been formally described. They rot the world's dead wood, feed most of the world's land plants, brew its beer, make its antibiotics and its statins, and kill an estimated 3.8 million people a year through infections that medicine has only recently begun to take seriously.

    1. What a fungus is

    A fungus is a eukaryote — nucleus, mitochondria (usually), endomembrane system, linear chromosomes, 80S ribosomes — that is heterotrophic by absorptive nutrition. This is the single defining behaviour. Animals ingest food and digest it internally; fungi do the reverse. They secrete a battery of exoenzymes (cellulases, ligninases, proteases, lipases, amylases, chitinases, phosphatases) into the surrounding substrate, break polymers down into monomers and small oligomers outside the cell, and then import those small molecules across the plasma membrane using an unusually rich set of transporters. A fungus is, functionally, a digestive tract turned inside out and spread through its food.

    Several consequences follow directly. Because digestion is external, fungi must maximise contact with the substrate and minimise the diffusion distance — hence the filamentous body plan, a network of tubes with an enormous surface-to-volume ratio. Because the enzymes are shared with whatever else lives nearby, fungi are prolific producers of antibiotics and other secondary metabolites that defend their externalised meal from bacteria and rival fungi; penicillin was discovered precisely because a mould was defending its lunch. And because they cannot chase food, fungi disperse instead as spores, often in astronomical numbers.

    The molecular signatures

    Table 1. Fungal cell chemistry compared with plants and animals
    FeatureFungiPlantsAnimals
    Cell wall polymerChitin (β-1,4-N-acetylglucosamine) + β-1,3/β-1,6-glucan + mannoproteinsCellulose + hemicellulose + pectinNone (extracellular matrix only)
    Dominant membrane sterolErgosterolSitosterol, stigmasterolCholesterol
    Carbohydrate storeGlycogen; also trehalose, mannitolStarchGlycogen
    NutritionHeterotrophic, absorptive (osmotrophy)Autotrophic (photosynthesis)Heterotrophic, ingestive (phagotrophy)
    Lysine biosynthesisα-aminoadipate (AAA) pathwayDiaminopimelate pathwayCannot synthesise (essential amino acid)
    Flagellum, when presentSingle posterior whiplash (chytrids only); lost in higher fungiAnterior, multiple (some gametes)Single posterior whiplash (sperm)
    Higher groupingOpisthokontaArchaeplastidaOpisthokonta — fungi and animals share this supergroup with choanoflagellates and nucleariids

    The posterior single flagellum is the origin of the name Opisthokonta ("posterior pole"). Fungi and animals share it, along with the AAA lysine pathway, chitin synthesis, glycogen storage, and a raft of molecular characters; the split between the fungal and animal lineages is dated to roughly 1.0–1.5 billion years ago. Fungi are more closely related to you than to a mushroom's substrate.

    Why ergosterol matters more than any other fungal molecule

    Ergosterol occupies the same structural niche in the fungal membrane that cholesterol does in ours: it modulates fluidity, permeability and the function of membrane proteins. It is chemically distinct enough to be druggable, and it is the target of the two largest antifungal classes. Polyenes (amphotericin B, nystatin) bind ergosterol directly, extracting it from the bilayer and forming pores. Azoles inhibit lanosterol 14α-demethylase (Erg11/Cyp51), the cytochrome P450 that makes ergosterol, causing depletion plus accumulation of toxic 14-methylated sterols. Allylamines (terbinafine) hit squalene epoxidase (Erg1) further upstream. The reason antifungal pharmacology is so much thinner than antibacterial pharmacology is that ergosterol, the cell wall and a couple of nucleic-acid quirks are almost the whole list of things fungi have that we do not.

    How many fungi are there?

    Formal description lags catastrophically behind reality. Around 155,000 species have been described. The classic Hawksworth estimate of 1.5 million (derived from a ~6:1 fungus-to-plant species ratio in well-surveyed Britain) has been revised upward by high-throughput environmental sequencing; current consensus estimates run 2.2 to 3.8 million species, with some molecular extrapolations reaching 6 million or higher. At current rates of description (roughly 2,000–2,500 new species per year) it would take well over a thousand years to finish the job. The bulk of the unknown is microfungal: soil and litter ascomycetes, endophytes, insect-associated fungi, marine and deep-subsurface lineages, and whole clades such as the Cryptomycota known almost entirely from environmental DNA.

    Scale anchors: a Saccharomyces cerevisiae cell is 5–10 μm — roughly the size of a human red blood cell. A Candida albicans blastoconidium is 4–6 μm; its germ tube is ~2 μm wide. An Aspergillus fumigatus conidium is 2–3 μm, small enough to reach the alveoli. Typical hyphae are 2–10 μm in diameter but can extend metres. A microsporidian spore is 1–4 μm — bacterial size, and long mistaken for one. A Cryptococcus neoformans cell is 5–10 μm plus a polysaccharide capsule that can double the diameter; its "titan cell" form reaches 50–100 μm.

    2. Structure and growth

    The hypha

    The fundamental unit of filamentous fungal growth is the hypha (plural hyphae): a tubular, apically extending cell bounded by a rigid wall and containing a flowing cytoplasm. A mass of interconnected hyphae is a mycelium; a visible mycelial mat is a colony; a dense aggregate of hyphae forming a tissue-like structure (a mushroom, a sclerotium, a rhizomorph) is a plectenchyma.

    Hyphae come in two architectures. Septate hyphae are divided by cross-walls (septa) at intervals, but the septa are perforated, so cytoplasm, mitochondria and even nuclei stream between compartments — the fungus is functionally a single continuous cell with internal bulkheads. Coenocytic (aseptate) hyphae, characteristic of the Mucoromycota and Chytridiomycota, have no regular cross-walls at all and are effectively one enormous multinucleate cell; septa appear only to wall off reproductive structures or damaged regions.

    The septal pore is a piece of remarkable engineering. In Ascomycota it is a simple perforation guarded by Woronin bodies — peroxisome-derived, dense, hexagonal crystalline organelles built of the protein HEX1 that sit beside the pore and, if the neighbouring compartment is punctured, plug the hole within seconds. This is a damage-control system: it lets a mould lose a hypha to a grazing nematode without bleeding out its entire mycelium. In Basidiomycota the equivalent structure is the dolipore septum, a barrel-shaped flared pore capped on each side by a perforated membranous parenthesome (septal pore cap) derived from endoplasmic reticulum, which regulates what passes through and can be occluded by septal pore-associated proteins.

    Apical growth and the Spitzenkörper

    Hyphae grow only at the tip. New wall material and membrane arrive as secretory vesicles carried on microtubules and actin cables to a phase-dark, vesicle-packed body just behind the apex: the Spitzenkörper ("apex body"). The Spitzenkörper acts as a vesicle supply centre and a steering wheel — its position within the apical dome determines the direction of growth, and displacing it makes the hypha turn. Vesicles fuse with the apical plasma membrane, delivering chitin synthases and glucan synthases that build wall in the extending zone, where the nascent wall is still plastic; behind the tip the wall cross-links and rigidifies. Turgor pressure (typically 0.3–1.0 MPa, sometimes far higher) provides the driving force. Extension rates of 1–40 μm per minute are usual; a single colony can add kilometres of hyphal length per day, and the largest known organism — an Armillaria ostoyae mycelium in Oregon — covers roughly 9 km² and may be thousands of years old.

    Behind the apex, hyphae branch, and the branches grow apart from one another (negative autotropism) until the colony reaches its margin, whereupon interior hyphae fuse (anastomosis) into a network that redistributes nutrients from resource-rich to resource-poor regions. A mycelium is thus a foraging network, not merely a mass.

    Yeasts

    A yeast is not a taxon but a growth form: a fungus that grows as single cells reproducing by budding or fission. Yeast forms have evolved repeatedly in both Ascomycota and Basidiomycota.

    • Budding (Saccharomyces cerevisiae, Candida, Cryptococcus): a daughter bud emerges from the mother, the nucleus divides through the bud neck, and the daughter separates leaving a chitin-rich bud scar. Budding is asymmetric — the mother is older and larger, and can produce only a limited number of daughters (the replicative lifespan, ~20–30 in S. cerevisiae), which made yeast the workhorse of ageing research.
    • Fission (Schizosaccharomyces pombe): the rod-shaped cell elongates at its tips and divides by a medial septum into two equal daughters, much like an animal cell. S. pombe and S. cerevisiae are separated by ~400 million years and differ profoundly in cell cycle control, making the pair a natural experiment in eukaryotic cell biology.
    • Pseudohyphae: buds that elongate and fail to detach, forming a chain with constrictions at the septa. Distinct from true hyphae, which are parallel-sided with no constriction. Candida albicans produces yeast, pseudohyphae and true hyphae, and the transitions are central to its virulence.
    • Fragmentation and arthroconidia: hyphae break at septa into individual propagules (Coccidioides, Geotrichum, Trichosporon).

    Dimorphism

    Thermal dimorphism is the ability to switch between a filamentous form in the environment and a yeast (or spherule) form in the host, cued principally by temperature. The classic rule of thumb is mould at 25 °C, yeast at 37 °C. Additional cues include CO₂ tension, cysteine and redox state, and host nutrient limitation; the switch is governed by histidine kinase thermosensors (Drk1 in Blastomyces and Histoplasma) and downstream transcriptional programmes (Ryp1–4). The mould phase makes the infectious propagule; the yeast phase is the invasive, immune-evading tissue form.

    Table 2. Dimorphic and polymorphic fungi
    OrganismEnvironmental (25 °C) formHost (37 °C) formNote
    Histoplasma capsulatumMould with tuberculate macroconidia + microconidiaSmall (2–4 μm) intracellular budding yeast in macrophagesMicroconidia are the infectious particle; bird/bat guano-enriched soil
    Blastomyces dermatitidisMould with pear-shaped conidia on lollipop conidiophoresLarge (8–15 μm) yeast with broad-based single bud, thick "double-contoured" wallBroad-based budding is the diagnostic clue
    Coccidioides immitis / C. posadasiiMould producing barrel-shaped arthroconidia alternating with empty cellsSpherule (20–80 μm) packed with endospores — not a yeastHighly infectious; BSL-3; the exception to the yeast rule
    Paracoccidioides brasiliensisMould, sparse conidiaYeast with multiple narrow-necked buds — "mariner's wheel" / "ship's pilot wheel"Latin American endemic mycosis
    Talaromyces (Penicillium) marneffeiMould with red diffusible pigment, penicillus conidiophoresYeast dividing by fission, with a central septumOnly dimorphic Penicillium-like fungus; SE Asia, HIV-associated
    Sporothrix schenckiiMould with "rosette"/"daisy" conidia on delicate hyphaeCigar-shaped budding yeastSubcutaneous inoculation from plant material — "rose gardener's disease"
    Candida albicansPolymorphic: yeast ↔ pseudohyphae ↔ true hyphae; also white/opaque and GUT switchingReverse-ish: hyphae are the invasive form and are induced by serum and 37 °C
    Malassezia furfurYeast and short hyphal forms together — "spaghetti and meatballs" on skin scrapingsLipid-dependent; requires olive oil overlay to culture

    The cell wall

    The fungal wall is a layered, dynamic composite typically 100–200 nm thick and accounting for 15–30% of cell dry weight. From the inside out:

    • Chitin — β-1,4-linked N-acetylglucosamine chains that hydrogen-bond into microfibrils of great tensile strength, laid down at the membrane by chitin synthases. Usually 1–15% of wall mass (more in filamentous fungi, less in yeasts), concentrated at bud necks, septa and hyphal tips.
    • β-1,3-glucan with β-1,6-glucan branches — the main load-bearing matrix, 30–60% of the wall, synthesised by the membrane complex Fks1/Fks2 with the GTPase Rho1 as regulatory subunit. This is the echinocandin target, and circulating (1→3)-β-D-glucan is the basis of the serum "BDG" diagnostic assay.
    • Mannoproteins — heavily N- and O-glycosylated glycoproteins, many GPI-anchored and cross-linked to glucan, forming the outer layer. They determine surface properties, adhesion, and most of what the immune system sees; galactomannan shed from Aspergillus walls underlies the galactomannan antigen test.
    • Outer coats — a hydrophobin rodlet layer and DHN-melanin on Aspergillus conidia (masking β-glucan from dectin-1); the glucuronoxylomannan (GXM) capsule of Cryptococcus, a genuine polysaccharide capsule outside the wall.

    Wall components as immune ligands

    Innate immunity reads the fungal wall through pattern recognition receptors: dectin-1 binds β-1,3-glucan, dectin-2 and the mannose receptor bind mannans, Mincle binds glycolipids, TLR2/TLR4 bind phospholipomannan and other ligands. Signalling converges on CARD9 and drives Th17 responses. This is why humans with inherited defects in CARD9, STAT3 (hyper-IgE syndrome), STAT1 gain-of-function, or the IL-17 axis suffer chronic mucocutaneous candidiasis and invasive fungal disease — and why fungi that mask their wall ligands (melanised conidia, capsule) are such successful pathogens.

    3. Reproduction

    Asexual reproduction

    Most microfungi propagate overwhelmingly by mitotic spores, which are cheap, numerous and genetically identical to the parent. A single Aspergillus colony can release 109 conidia; ambient outdoor air typically carries 102–104 fungal spores per cubic metre, and a human inhales hundreds of A. fumigatus conidia a day without noticing.

    Table 3. Asexual propagules of fungi
    TypeHow it formsExamples
    SporangiosporesCleaved internally within a sac (sporangium) borne on a sporangiophore; endogenousRhizopus, Mucor, Lichtheimia — the Mucoromycota
    Conidia (general)Formed externally on a conidiogenous cell; never enclosed in a sacAscomycota and many Basidiomycota
    — BlastoconidiaBy budding from a parent cell or hyphaCandida, Cladosporium, black yeasts
    — PhialoconidiaExtruded in succession from a fixed-length flask-shaped phialide, forming chains or slimy headsAspergillus (phialides on a vesicle), Penicillium (brush-like penicillus), Fusarium, Trichoderma
    — ArthroconidiaBy fragmentation of an existing hypha at the septaCoccidioides, Geotrichum, Trichosporon, some dermatophytes in hair
    — Chlamydoconidia (chlamydospores)Thick-walled swollen survival cells formed within or terminal on a hyphaCandida albicans (terminal, on cornmeal-Tween agar — a classic identification test), Fusarium
    — Annelloconidia / poroconidia / aleurioconidiaFrom annellides with ring scars; through pores in the conidiogenous cell; by lysis of a supporting cellScopulariopsis; Alternaria, Bipolaris; Histoplasma macroconidia, dermatophyte macro/microconidia
    Macroconidia / microconidiaTwo size classes in the same species; shapes are diagnostic in dermatophytesMicrosporum (spindle, rough-walled macroconidia), Trichophyton (pencil-shaped, smooth; abundant microconidia), Epidermophyton (club-shaped macroconidia, no microconidia)
    ZoosporesNaked, wall-less, flagellated motile cells released from a zoosporangiumChytridiomycota, Blastocladiomycota, Neocallimastigomycota
    Budding / fission / fragmentationSimple vegetative multiplication without differentiated sporesYeasts generally; mycelial fragments in culture and in tissue

    Sexual reproduction

    The fungal sexual cycle is unusual in that the two steps of fertilisation are widely separated in time. Plasmogamy (fusion of cytoplasm) brings two compatible nuclei into one cell; karyogamy (fusion of nuclei) may follow immediately, or — in the Dikarya — may be postponed through an extended dikaryotic phase in which paired but unfused nuclei divide in step, sometimes for years and over metres of mycelium. Karyogamy produces the only diploid cell in many fungal life cycles, and meiosis follows promptly to restore haploidy and generate the sexual spores.

    Table 4. Sexual spores by phylum
    SporePhylumFormationTypical number
    ZygosporeMucoromycota, ZoopagomycotaThick-walled, ornamented resting spore formed where two compatible gametangia fuse; meiosis at germination1 per zygosporangium
    AscosporeAscomycotaFormed inside a sac (ascus) after karyogamy and meiosis, often with one post-meiotic mitosis8 (sometimes 4, or many)
    BasidiosporeBasidiomycotaFormed outside, on sterigmata of a club-shaped basidium, and forcibly discharged by the Buller's drop mechanism4 (2 in some rusts/smuts)
    Oospore—Belongs to oomycetes (Phytophthora, Pythium) — not fungi, but Stramenopiles with cellulose wallsListed to flag a classic confusion
    Resting sporangiumChytridiomycota, BlastocladiomycotaThick-walled diploid structure; meiosis at germination releases zoosporesVariable

    Ascomycete asci are usually packaged in a fruiting body (ascoma): a closed cleistothecium, a flask-shaped perithecium with an ostiole, an open cup-shaped apothecium, or a pseudothecium with bitunicate asci. Basidiomycete basidia line the hymenium of a basidioma — the mushroom, bracket or puffball. Microfungi frequently produce no fruiting body at all.

    Mating types

    Fungi do not have sexes; they have mating types determined by MAT loci, which are not alleles in the ordinary sense but "idiomorphs" — wholly dissimilar DNA sequences occupying the same chromosomal position and encoding different transcription factors (an α-box protein, an HMG-box protein, or a homeodomain protein). Saccharomyces cerevisiae has two idiomorphs, MATa and MATα, and famously switches between them by gene conversion from silent HML/HMR cassettes cut by the HO endonuclease. Basidiomycetes often use a tetrapolar system with two unlinked loci: A (homeodomain proteins) and B (pheromones and receptors), which in Schizophyllum commune generates something like 23,000 mating types. Some species are homothallic (self-fertile), others heterothallic (requiring a partner). In Cryptococcus neoformans the great majority of clinical isolates are MATα, and the α mating type is associated with enhanced virulence.

    Heterokaryosis and the parasexual cycle

    Because hyphae fuse, a single mycelium may contain genetically distinct nuclei — a heterokaryon. Compatibility is policed by vegetative incompatibility (het/vic) loci, which trigger programmed cell death at the fusion point between incompatible strains, a fungal analogue of allorecognition that limits the spread of mycoviruses and selfish elements.

    Where heterokaryons persist, Pontecorvo's parasexual cycle can generate recombinants without meiosis: two haploid nuclei fuse to give a rare diploid, mitotic crossing-over shuffles chromosome arms, and the unstable diploid then loses chromosomes progressively (haploidisation) back to a recombinant haploid. This matters practically — it is a recombination route in Aspergillus, in the long-"asexual" Candida albicans (which uses an obligate diploid parasex with concerted chromosome loss), and in agricultural and clinical strain evolution.

    One fungus, one name

    The teleomorph / anamorph problem

    Because sexual and asexual states of the same fungus often look completely different, occur at different times, and were frequently discovered separately, mycology developed dual nomenclature: the sexual state (teleomorph) and the asexual state (anamorph) could each carry their own valid Latin binomial, with the whole organism called the holomorph. So Aspergillus fumigatus was the anamorph of the teleomorph Neosartorya fumigata; Candida albicans and Histoplasma capsulatum had teleomorph names too (Ajellomyces capsulatus for the latter). Asexual fungi with no known sexual state were bundled into an artificial dumping ground, the "Deuteromycota" or Fungi Imperfecti.

    DNA sequencing made the two-name system untenable — you can now link anamorph to teleomorph directly, and a single organism carrying two names is simply confusing in a database. The Amsterdam Declaration on Fungal Nomenclature (2011) and the Melbourne Code (International Code of Nomenclature for algae, fungi and plants, 2011, effective 1 January 2013) abolished separate names for morphs: one fungus, one name. Article 59 was rewritten so priority governs, though widely used names can be protected. Consequences visible in the clinical literature include Penicillium marneffei → Talaromyces marneffei, the retirement of Neosartorya and Ajellomyces, and the ongoing dismantling of "Zygomycota" into Mucoromycota and Zoopagomycota.

    4. Classification: a tour of the phyla

    Kingdom Fungi is now usually divided into roughly 8–12 phyla, with the deepest branches consisting entirely of microscopic, often parasitic organisms that were not recognised as fungi at all until molecular phylogenetics arrived. The traditional "Zygomycota" and "Deuteromycota" are both defunct.

    Table 5. Fungal phyla with microbial emphasis
    PhylumKey featuresDescribed spp. (approx.)Representative genera
    Cryptomycota / RozellomycotaMostly wall-less or wall-poor intracellular parasites of other fungi and algae; flagellated; known largely from environmental DNA<100 described; enormous unseen diversityRozella, Paramicrosporidium, "LKM11" clades
    MicrosporidiaObligate intracellular parasites of animals; no true mitochondria (mitosomes); polar tube injection apparatus; genome reduction~1,700Enterocytozoon, Encephalitozoon, Nosema, Vittaforma
    ChytridiomycotaZoospores with a single posterior whiplash flagellum; coenocytic rhizoids; mostly aquatic/soil saprotrophs and parasites~1,000Batrachochytrium, Synchytrium, Rhizophydium
    BlastocladiomycotaFlagellated; alternation of haploid and diploid generations (sporic meiosis) — unique among fungi~200Allomyces, Blastocladiella, Coelomomyces (mosquito parasite)
    NeocallimastigomycotaStrictly anaerobic rumen and hindgut fungi; hydrogenosomes instead of mitochondria; polyflagellate zoospores; potent cellulase/hemicellulase systems~40Neocallimastix, Piromyces, Anaeromyces, Orpinomyces
    ZoopagomycotaParasites and predators of animals, amoebae and other fungi; forcibly discharged conidia~900Entomophthora, Basidiobolus, Conidiobolus, Piptocephalis
    MucoromycotaFast-growing coenocytic saprotrophs; sporangiospores; zygospores; also fine root endophytes~1,000Rhizopus, Mucor, Lichtheimia, Cunninghamella, Pilobolus
    GlomeromycotaObligate arbuscular mycorrhizal symbionts; coenocytic; huge multinucleate spores; no known sexual stage~350Rhizophagus, Glomus, Gigaspora, Funneliformis
    AscomycotaSeptate hyphae with Woronin bodies; ascospores in asci; most yeasts and most moulds; ~75% of described fungi~95,000Saccharomyces, Aspergillus, Penicillium, Candida, Fusarium, Neurospora
    BasidiomycotaDolipore septa with parenthesomes; clamp connections; basidiospores on basidia; mushrooms, rusts, smuts, and a distinct set of yeasts~50,000Cryptococcus, Malassezia, Ustilago, Puccinia, Agaricus, Trichosporon

    Cryptomycota and Microsporidia

    The Cryptomycota (Rozellomycota) were revealed by environmental rRNA surveys of pond water and soil in 2011 as an abundant, ubiquitous, and almost entirely uncultured lineage branching at or near the base of Fungi. They are tiny (2–5 μm) flagellated cells that parasitise other fungi, oomycetes and algae, and they lack a chitinous wall for most of their life cycle — which is why they were missed by every classical criterion of what a fungus is.

    Microsporidia are their close relatives and among the most extreme parasites known. They are obligate intracellular, produce a resistant spore 1–4 μm across (bacterial size, and long classified as protozoa), and infect by an astonishing mechanism: the coiled polar tube everts explosively in under two seconds, punctures the host cell membrane, and injects the sporoplasm directly into the host cytoplasm. They have shed the peroxisome, the flagellum, the Golgi in recognisable form, and the mitochondrion — retaining only a mitosome that makes iron-sulfur clusters and no ATP. They steal host ATP through nucleotide transporters. Encephalitozoon cuniculi has one of the smallest known eukaryotic genomes at 2.9 Mb, smaller than many bacteria.

    Medically, Enterocytozoon bieneusi is the commonest cause of human microsporidiosis, producing chronic diarrhoea and wasting in advanced HIV and in transplant recipients; Encephalitozoon intestinalis, E. hellem and E. cuniculi disseminate to the eye, sinuses, kidney and brain. Albendazole works against Encephalitozoon (which has a susceptible β-tubulin) but not against E. bieneusi, for which fumagillin is used. Nosema (now partly Vairimorpha) apis and N. ceranae are significant honeybee pathogens, and Nosema bombycis caused the 19th-century silkworm epidemic Pasteur was hired to solve.

    Chytridiomycota

    Chytrids are the flagellated fungi — the retained ancestral condition. Zoospores swim with a single posterior whiplash flagellum, encyst on a substrate, and produce rhizoids that penetrate and digest it, while the cell body becomes a sporangium. Most are innocuous decomposers of chitin, keratin, cellulose and pollen in soil and freshwater, and parasitic chytrids of phytoplankton are increasingly recognised as a major link in aquatic food webs (the "mycoloop", converting inedible large algae into edible zoospores for zooplankton).

    Batrachochytrium and the amphibian crisis

    Batrachochytrium dendrobatidis (Bd), described in 1999, infects amphibian skin, where keratinised tissue supports zoosporangia. Because amphibians breathe and osmoregulate through their skin, chytridiomycosis disrupts electrolyte transport, causing hyponatraemia, hypokalaemia and cardiac arrest. Bd has been implicated in declines in more than 500 amphibian species and the presumed extinction of around 90 — described as the most destructive documented pathogen-driven loss of biodiversity in a vertebrate class. The hypervirulent global panzootic lineage (BdGPL) appears to have radiated from an East Asian origin, spread by the trade in amphibians. A second species, B. salamandrivorans (Bsal), described in 2013 from collapsing European fire salamander populations, is even more lethal to salamanders and has driven import restrictions in North America, where salamander diversity is highest.

    Blastocladiomycota and Neocallimastigomycota

    Blastocladiomycota, split from the chytrids on molecular grounds, uniquely among fungi show a true alternation of generations with sporic meiosis. Allomyces is a classic teaching organism; Coelomomyces is an obligate mosquito parasite with an alternating copepod host; Physoderma causes brown spot of maize.

    Neocallimastigomycota are the anaerobic gut fungi of ruminants, horses, elephants and other herbivores. They are strict anaerobes — killed by oxygen — possess hydrogenosomes in place of mitochondria (producing H₂, CO₂ and acetate, with ATP from substrate-level phosphorylation), and have zoospores with 1 to 16 flagella. They matter because their cellulosomes and free enzymes are among the most efficient plant-cell-wall-degrading systems known: they physically penetrate lignified tissue with invasive rhizoids that other rumen microbes cannot breach, and their genomes are exceptionally rich in carbohydrate-active enzymes, making them a major target for biofuel and feed enzyme discovery.

    Zoopagomycota and Mucoromycota

    The old phylum "Zygomycota" was polyphyletic and has been dissolved into two: Zoopagomycota (parasites of animals, amoebae and fungi) and Mucoromycota (saprotrophs, mycorrhizal fine root endophytes, and plant-associated groups).

    Rhizopus and Mucor are the archetypal fast-growing "pin moulds": coenocytic hyphae, sporangia on tall sporangiophores (Rhizopus with rhizoids and stolons at the nodes, Mucor without), and a colony that fills a Petri dish overnight. Pilobolus, the "hat-thrower", grows on herbivore dung and solves the dispersal problem by building a subsporangial vesicle that acts as a lens, orienting the sporangiophore towards light and then firing the whole sporangium up to 2 m at accelerations exceeding 20,000 g — one of the fastest movements in biology — landing it on grass beyond the dung's repugnance zone, where it will be eaten again.

    Mucormycosis — the "black fungus"

    Members of the Mucorales (Rhizopus arrhizus above all, plus Mucor, Lichtheimia, Cunninghamella, Rhizomucor) cause mucormycosis, an angioinvasive infection with a marked predilection for uncontrolled diabetes with ketoacidosis, iron overload and deferoxamine therapy, haematological malignancy, and corticosteroid use. The fungus invades blood vessels, thromboses them and infarcts tissue, producing the characteristic black eschar — hence the popular name. Rhino-orbital-cerebral, pulmonary, cutaneous (often after trauma or contaminated dressings) and gastrointestinal forms occur. Histology shows broad (6–25 μm), thin-walled, ribbon-like, irregularly branching hyphae with few or no septa, branching at wide (~90°) angles — distinguishing them from the narrow, regularly septate, acute-angle (~45°) hyphae of Aspergillus. They are intrinsically resistant to voriconazole and to echinocandins; treatment is high-dose liposomal amphotericin B plus aggressive surgical debridement and correction of the underlying condition, with isavuconazole or posaconazole as alternatives. Mortality is 40–80% and higher in disseminated disease. India recorded a large epidemic of COVID-19-associated mucormycosis in 2021, driven by the collision of diabetes, corticosteroids and severe COVID-19.

    In the Zoopagomycota, Entomophthora muscae infects houseflies, manipulates them into climbing to a high point and adopting a wing-raised "death pose" that maximises spore dispersal, and then fires forcibly discharged conidia from the corpse. Basidiobolus and Conidiobolus cause entomophthoramycosis — chronic subcutaneous infections of the limbs or the nasofacial region, mostly in immunocompetent people in the tropics, and clinically quite unlike mucormycosis.

    Glomeromycota

    The Glomeromycota are obligate biotrophs that form arbuscular mycorrhizae (AM), the most widespread symbiosis on Earth: roughly 80% of land plant species and around 90% of plant families host them, and the association is at least 400 million years old, visible in the Rhynie chert and plausibly instrumental in the colonisation of land by plants. Hyphae penetrate root cortical cells and elaborate finely branched arbuscules inside the plant cell wall but outside the plant plasma membrane, creating an enormous interface for exchange; many species also form storage vesicles. The plant supplies hexoses and, crucially, lipids (AM fungi are fatty-acid auxotrophs, which explains why they cannot be grown without a host); the fungus supplies phosphate, zinc, copper and water from soil volumes that root hairs cannot reach.

    Rhizophagus irregularis (long known as Glomus intraradices) is the model organism, the basis of most commercial AM inoculants, and the source of the first Glomeromycota genome. Its biology is peculiar: coenocytic hyphae carrying hundreds to thousands of nuclei, no observed sexual reproduction (though MAT-like loci and evidence of genetic exchange exist), and a debate over whether isolates are homokaryotic or dikaryotic that is still not fully settled.

    Ascomycota

    The Ascomycota are the largest fungal phylum — about 95,000 described species, some three-quarters of all named fungi — and they dominate microfungal life. They are defined by the ascus, but most microbial ascomycetes are encountered as asexual moulds or yeasts. Three subphyla matter here: Taphrinomycotina (a basal grab-bag including Schizosaccharomyces and Pneumocystis), Saccharomycotina (the "true yeasts": Saccharomyces, Candida, Pichia, Kluyveromyces) and Pezizomycotina (the filamentous ascomycetes: Aspergillus, Penicillium, Fusarium, dermatophytes, dimorphic pathogens, most lichen fungi).

    • Saccharomyces cerevisiae — baker's, brewer's and the model eukaryote. Haploid or diploid, buds, ferments glucose to ethanol and CO₂ even in oxygen (the Crabtree effect).
    • Schizosaccharomyces pombe — fission yeast, isolated from East African millet beer; the organism in which Paul Nurse identified cdc2/CDK1 and the cell-cycle control that won the 2001 Nobel Prize.
    • Candida — a polyphyletic assemblage of yeasts. C. albicans is a normal commensal of gut, mouth and vagina in 30–70% of people and the leading cause of mucosal and invasive candidiasis; C. glabrata (now Nakaseomyces glabratus) is haploid, closer to Saccharomyces, and intrinsically less azole-susceptible; C. parapsilosis loves catheters and parenteral nutrition; C. tropicalis, C. krusei (Pichia kudriavzevii, intrinsically fluconazole-resistant) and C. auris complete the clinical roster.
    • Aspergillus — several hundred species with the diagnostic conidiophore ending in a swollen vesicle covered by phialides. A. fumigatus (thermotolerant to 55 °C, 2–3 μm conidia, uniseriate, columnar) is the great invasive pathogen; A. flavus makes aflatoxin; A. niger makes citric acid; A. terreus makes lovastatin and is amphotericin-resistant; A. oryzae makes koji.
    • Penicillium — the brush-like penicillus; P. rubens (Fleming's mould) gave penicillin; P. roqueforti and P. camemberti make cheese; P. expansum makes patulin in rotting apples.
    • Fusarium — banana-shaped multiseptate macroconidia; devastating plant pathogens (F. oxysporum f. sp. cubense TR4 and the banana; F. graminearum and Fusarium head blight), mycotoxin producers (fumonisins, trichothecenes, zearalenone), keratitis after contact lens use, and refractory disseminated infection in neutropenia.
    • Trichoderma — fast green moulds used as biocontrol agents against plant-pathogenic fungi and as industrial cellulase factories (T. reesei, from rotting canvas in the Pacific in WWII, is the source of most commercial cellulase).
    • Neurospora crassa — the orange bread mould behind Beadle and Tatum's one gene–one enzyme hypothesis (1941), and a model for circadian rhythms, DNA methylation, and repeat-induced point mutation.
    • Dimorphic endemic pathogens — Histoplasma, Blastomyces, Coccidioides, Paracoccidioides, Talaromyces marneffei, all in the Onygenales or Eurotiales (see Table 2).
    • Pneumocystis jirovecii — a Taphrinomycotina yeast-like fungus, host-species-specific, unculturable, with cholesterol scavenged from the host instead of ergosterol (which is why it does not respond to azoles or amphotericin). Classified as a protozoan until rRNA work in 1988 moved it to the fungi.
    • Dermatophytes — keratinophilic Onygenales in three genera: Trichophyton (skin, hair, nails), Microsporum (skin, hair), Epidermophyton (skin, nails; never hair). Classified ecologically as anthropophilic (human-adapted, chronic, mild inflammation — T. rubrum), zoophilic (animal, brisk inflammation — M. canis) or geophilic (soil — M. gypseum).
    • Claviceps purpurea — infects rye and other grasses, replacing the grain with a hard purple-black sclerotium (the ergot) packed with alkaloids. Consumption causes ergotism: the convulsive form (seizures, hallucinations, psychosis) and the gangrenous form ("St Anthony's Fire", vasoconstriction leading to loss of limbs). Medieval European epidemics killed thousands and have been argued — controversially — to lie behind episodes of mass hysteria including the Salem witch trials. The same alkaloids gave medicine ergotamine for migraine, ergometrine for postpartum haemorrhage, bromocriptine and cabergoline — and, from Hofmann's semisynthesis at Sandoz in 1938, LSD.

    Basidiomycota

    The Basidiomycota include the mushrooms, but from a microbiological standpoint the interesting members are the yeasts and the plant-pathogenic rusts and smuts.

    • Cryptococcus neoformans and C. gattii — encapsulated basidiomycete yeasts. C. neoformans is associated with pigeon guano and rotting wood and causes disease overwhelmingly in advanced HIV; C. gattii is associated with eucalypts and other trees, notoriously caused an outbreak on Vancouver Island from 1999 spreading into the Pacific Northwest, and affects apparently immunocompetent people. Virulence rests on the GXM capsule (antiphagocytic, immunomodulatory, the basis of the cryptococcal antigen lateral flow assay), melanin made by laccase from host catecholamines, growth at 37 °C, urease and phospholipase, and titan cells.
    • Malassezia — lipophilic yeasts that dominate the human skin mycobiome. They lack fatty acid synthase and must scavenge lipids, hence their preference for sebaceous areas. They cause pityriasis (tinea) versicolor with its hypo- or hyperpigmented scaly macules, seborrhoeic dermatitis and dandruff (M. globosa, M. restricta), Malassezia folliculitis, and catheter-associated fungaemia in neonates on intravenous lipids.
    • Rhodotorula — carotenoid-pigmented pink/coral yeasts, ubiquitous environmental colonisers of showers and air conditioning; occasional catheter-related fungaemia; intrinsically resistant to echinocandins and poorly susceptible to fluconazole.
    • Trichosporon asahii and relatives — arthroconidium-forming yeasts causing white piedra of hair shafts and, in neutropenia, breakthrough disseminated trichosporonosis notable for occurring on echinocandin therapy. They cross-react with the cryptococcal antigen test.
    • Smuts — Ustilago maydis (corn smut, eaten as huitlacoche) is a superb model for dimorphism, mating-type control and biotrophic plant infection; Ustilago switches from saprophytic budding yeast to infectious dikaryotic hyphae only after mating.
    • Rusts — obligate biotrophs with the most complex life cycles in the fungi: up to five spore stages and often two unrelated hosts. Puccinia graminis f. sp. tritici (stem rust of wheat, alternating on barberry) is a historic and continuing threat to food security, with the Ug99 lineage from Uganda (1999) overcoming widely deployed resistance genes; Hemileia vastatrix (coffee leaf rust) destroyed Ceylon's coffee industry and turned it to tea.

    Lichens

    A lichen is not an organism but an ecosystem in miniature: a fungus (the mycobiont, ~98% of the time an ascomycete, mostly Lecanoromycetes) housing a photosynthetic partner (the photobiont — a green alga such as Trebouxia or Trentepohlia, a cyanobacterium such as Nostoc, or both). The fungus builds the thallus, provides mineral nutrients, water retention and UV protection, and receives fixed carbon (as ribitol from green algae, glucose from cyanobacteria); cyanobacterial partners also fix nitrogen. About 20% of all fungal species are lichenised — roughly 20,000 species. Lichens colonise bare rock, produce over 800 unique secondary metabolites (usnic acid, atranorin, depsides and depsidones), weather rock into soil, dominate arctic and alpine ground cover, and serve as extremely sensitive bioindicators of SO₂ and nitrogen pollution. They grow slowly — often under a millimetre a year — and some arctic thalli are thousands of years old, a fact exploited by lichenometry for dating rock surfaces.

    The third partner, 2016

    The two-partner account of lichens stood for 150 years. In 2016 Toby Spribille and colleagues, trying to explain why two lichens (Bryoria fremontii and B. tortuosa) with identical ascomycete and algal partners differ in appearance and in vulpinic acid content, found abundant transcripts from a basidiomycete yeast (Cyphobasidiales) embedded in the cortex. These yeasts turn out to be widespread across macrolichens on six continents. Later work added further partners, including Tremella yeasts and consistently associated bacterial communities. The lichen is best understood as a multi-species consortium, and the classic "one fungus plus one alga" formulation is a useful simplification rather than the truth.

    5. Fungal ecology

    Decomposition and the lignin monopoly

    Fungi are the principal decomposers of terrestrial plant material, and they hold a near-monopoly on one step no one else does well. Cellulose is degraded by many bacteria; lignin — the irregular, racemic, aromatic polymer that stiffens wood and makes up 15–35% of it — is not. Lignin has no hydrolysable stereoregular bonds, so it cannot be attacked by ordinary hydrolases. White rot fungi (Basidiomycota: Phanerochaete chrysosporium, Trametes versicolor, Pleurotus ostreatus) solve this with a non-specific oxidative arsenal — lignin peroxidase, manganese peroxidase, versatile peroxidase and laccases, working through diffusible radical mediators — that burns the polymer apart by free-radical chemistry, leaving pale, fibrous, cellulose-rich residue. Brown rot fungi (Serpula lacrymans, dry rot) instead use Fenton chemistry to bypass lignin, removing the cellulose and leaving a brown, cubically cracked lignin skeleton. Soft rot ascomycetes (Chaetomium, Xylaria) erode cavities within the secondary wall in wet conditions.

    This matters at planetary scale. The evolution of ligninolytic Agaricomycetes has been dated by molecular clock to around the end of the Carboniferous, and one influential (though contested) hypothesis holds that the delayed arrival of lignin-degrading fungi is why so much Carboniferous plant matter accumulated undecayed as coal. Today, white rot enzymes are exploited for pulp biobleaching, dye decolourisation, and the degradation of recalcitrant pollutants including PAHs, PCBs, TNT and some plastics — the field of mycoremediation.

    Mycorrhizae and the "wood wide web"

    Table 6. Major mycorrhizal types
    TypeFungiPlantsStructure
    Arbuscular (AM)Glomeromycota (Rhizophagus, Gigaspora)~80% of land plant species; most herbs, grasses, crops, tropical treesIntracellular arbuscules and vesicles; no sheath; hyphae penetrate cortical cell walls
    Ectomycorrhizal (ECM)Basidiomycota + some Ascomycota (Amanita, Boletus, Laccaria, Tuber); ~20,000–25,000 spp., evolved >80 times~2% of plant species but dominant temperate/boreal trees: pine, oak, beech, birch, eucalypt, dipterocarpHyphal mantle around the root tip + intercellular Hartig net; no intracellular penetration
    EricoidAscomycota (Rhizoscyphus ericae, Oidiodendron)Ericaceae (heather, blueberry, rhododendron) on acid, N-poor peatCoils inside hair-root epidermal cells; strong saprotrophic/proteolytic capacity
    OrchidBasidiomycota (Rhizoctonia complex: Tulasnella, Ceratobasidium, Serendipita)All orchids, obligately at germinationIntracellular pelotons; carbon flows fungus → plant in mycoheterotrophic seedlings
    Monotropoid / mycoheterotrophicECM basidiomycetesMonotropa, Sarcodes, ghost orchids — achlorophyllous plantsPlant parasitises a tree via a shared fungus; net carbon flow tree → fungus → plant

    Because a single mycelium can colonise several plants, roots in a forest are frequently interconnected by common mycorrhizal networks (CMNs). Isotope-labelling experiments — most famously Suzanne Simard's 1997 Nature paper using ¹³C and ¹⁴C between birch and Douglas fir — demonstrated bidirectional carbon transfer between trees sharing ECM fungi, and other work has shown movement of nitrogen, phosphorus, water and defence-related signals.

    Honest caveats on the "wood wide web"

    The popular framing — mother trees deliberately nursing their offspring through a cooperative underground internet — runs well ahead of the evidence, and a 2023 critical review by Karst, Jones and Hoeksema in Nature Ecology & Evolution found that the three most-repeated claims (that CMNs are widespread in forests, that they meaningfully transfer resources to benefit seedlings, and that mature trees preferentially direct resources to kin) are supported by far less field data than the popular literature implies, with citation bias amplifying positive results. Real uncertainties include: how much labelled carbon actually crosses into a receiver plant rather than remaining in fungal tissue in and around its roots; whether transfers are net-positive for the receiver or simply flux along a source–sink gradient; and whether the fungus — which is not a passive pipe but an organism with its own interests, sometimes closer to a parasite than a partner — is best modelled as a cooperative conduit at all. Networks demonstrably exist and matter; the altruistic narrative around them should be held loosely.

    Endophytes, entomopathogens and predators

    Endophytes live inside healthy plant tissue without causing disease, and essentially every plant examined harbours them. Clavicipitaceous grass endophytes (Epichloë/Neotyphodium in fescue and ryegrass) are transmitted vertically through seed and produce ergot and lolitrem alkaloids that deter insect and mammalian herbivores — the cause of "fescue toxicosis" and "ryegrass staggers" in livestock, and simultaneously the reason endophyte-infected turf is so hardy. Non-clavicipitaceous endophytes are hugely diverse, horizontally transmitted, and can confer drought, heat and salt tolerance — Curvularia protuberata allows a panic grass to grow in Yellowstone geothermal soils above 50 °C, but only when the fungus itself carries a particular mycovirus: a three-way symbiosis.

    Entomopathogenic fungi infect arthropods directly through the cuticle rather than the gut, using proteases, chitinases and lipases plus turgor-driven penetration from an appressorium — which makes them the main biocontrol option against sap-sucking insects that never ingest a bacterial toxin. Beauveria bassiana (white muscardine, the fungus Bassi used in 1835 to give the first demonstration that a microorganism can cause animal disease) and Metarhizium anisopliae (green muscardine) are commercial biopesticides, and transgenic Metarhizium expressing scorpion toxin has been trialled against malaria mosquitoes. Ophiocordyceps unilateralis is the "zombie ant" fungus: it invades a carpenter ant, fills the body with fungal cells (largely sparing the brain), induces the ant to leave the nest, climb vegetation, and clamp its mandibles onto a leaf vein at a stereotyped height and orientation — roughly 25 cm above the forest floor on the north side of a leaf, in the humid microclimate the fungus needs — whereupon the ant dies and a stalk erupts from its head to rain spores on the foraging trails below. Fossil leaves from the Messel Pit show the same death-grip scars 48 million years ago.

    Nematode-trapping fungi are carnivorous. Arthrobotrys oligospora and relatives are ordinary soil saprotrophs until nitrogen becomes scarce and nematode-derived cues (ascarosides) are detected, whereupon they build traps: adhesive networks, adhesive knobs, non-constricting rings, and the spectacular constricting ring, whose three cells inflate to three times their volume in about a tenth of a second when a nematode enters, throttling it before penetrating hyphae digest it from within. Other fungi take different routes — Pleurotus secretes a paralytic toxin droplet, Drechmeria uses adhesive spores.

    Finally, yeasts colonise habitats we rarely think of as microbial: floral nectar, where Metschnikowia reukaufii and relatives are dispersed by pollinators and alter nectar sugar composition, temperature and scent enough to change pollinator behaviour; insect guts and fermentation chambers; tree exudates; the marine deep subsurface; and human skin, where Malassezia makes up the overwhelming majority of the cutaneous mycobiome on sebaceous sites, with the feet a striking exception (a much more diverse community including Aspergillus, Cryptococcus and dermatophytes).

    6. Medical mycology

    Fungal disease was long treated as a niche concern. Current estimates put the annual burden at roughly 6.5 million invasive fungal infections and around 3.8 million deaths per year, of which ~2.5 million are directly attributable — comparable to tuberculosis and greater than malaria. Around a billion people have a superficial fungal infection at any time. The drivers are iatrogenic and demographic: HIV, cancer chemotherapy and neutropenia, transplantation and its immunosuppression, ICU care with central lines and broad-spectrum antibacterials, biologics targeting TNF and IL-17, diabetes, and an ageing population. In 2022 the WHO published its first Fungal Priority Pathogens List, ranking 19 fungi into critical, high and medium priority groups to direct research, surveillance and drug development — the critical group being Cryptococcus neoformans, Candida auris, Aspergillus fumigatus and Candida albicans.

    Mycotoxins

    Mycotoxins are secondary metabolites produced by fungi growing on food and feed. Unlike infection, exposure is dietary, and the toxins are heat-stable enough to survive most cooking and processing.

    7. Antifungal drugs

    Antibacterial chemotherapy exploits the vast biochemical distance between a bacterium and a human cell. Antifungal chemotherapy has no such luxury: fungi are eukaryotes with the same ribosomes, the same core metabolism, the same cytoskeleton and the same cell cycle machinery as their hosts. Nearly every clinically useful antifungal therefore attacks one of three things fungi have and we do not — ergosterol, the cell wall, or a fungus-specific enzyme of nucleic acid or amino acid metabolism. There are, in consequence, only about four established antifungal classes against dozens of antibacterial ones, and toxicity is a recurring problem.

    Table 9. Antifungal drug classes
    Class / agentTarget and mechanismSpectrumKey limitations
    Polyenes
    Amphotericin B (deoxycholate, lipid complex, liposomal), nystatin    		Bind ergosterol directly; extract it from the bilayer into extramembranous sponges and form ion-permeable pores → K⁺ leakage and death.     Azoles — triazoles
    Fluconazole, itraconazole, voriconazole, posaconazole, isavuconazole (and topical imidazoles: clotrimazole, miconazole, ketoconazole)    		Inhibit lanosterol 14α-demethylase (Erg11/Cyp51A), a cytochrome P450: ergosterol depletion plus accumulation of toxic 14-methylated sterols. Mostly fungistatic against yeasts, fungicidal against mouldsFluconazole: Candida (not krusei, variable glabrata), Cryptococcus, coccidioidal meningitis — no mould activity. Voriconazole: adds Aspergillus, Scedosporium, Fusarium. Posaconazole and isavuconazole: add MucoralesExtensive CYP450 drug interactions (all), QT prolongation (most; isavuconazole shortens QT), hepatotoxicity. Voriconazole: visual disturbance, photopsia, phototoxicity and squamous skin cancer, periostitis, neurotoxicity — needs therapeutic drug monitoring. Itraconazole: negative inotrope, erratic absorption. Teratogenic at high dose
    Echinocandins
    Caspofungin, micafungin, anidulafungin, rezafungin    		Non-competitive inhibition of β-1,3-glucan synthase (Fks1/Fks2) → wall integrity failure and osmotic lysis. Fungicidal against Candida, fungistatic against Aspergillus (tip lysis only)All Candida including most auris; Aspergillus (salvage/combination). First-line for candidaemia and invasive candidiasisNo activity against Cryptococcus (little glucan exposure), Mucorales, Trichosporon, Fusarium. IV only — no oral formulation. Poor CNS, eye and urine penetration. Very well tolerated otherwise; few interactions
    Pyrimidine analogue
    Flucytosine (5-FC)    		Taken up by cytosine permease (absent in human cells), deaminated by fungal cytosine deaminase to 5-fluorouracil → 5-FUTP into RNA and 5-FdUMP inhibiting thymidylate synthaseCryptococcus, Candida. Essentially always used in combinationRapid resistance as monotherapy. Dose-dependent bone marrow suppression and hepatotoxicity (gut flora convert some to 5-FU); requires level monitoring and renal dose adjustment. Global access is poor — a major cause of avoidable cryptococcal deaths
    Allylamines
    Terbinafine, naftifine    		Inhibit squalene epoxidase (Erg1), early in ergosterol synthesis: ergosterol depletion plus toxic squalene accumulation. FungicidalDermatophytes above all; also some moulds. Oral terbinafine is first-line for onychomycosis and tinea capitisWeak against Candida.     GriseofulvinBinds tubulin and microtubule-associated proteins, disrupting the mitotic spindle; concentrates in keratinDermatophytes only; still used for tinea capitis in children (especially Microsporum)Fungistatic, slow — months of therapy.     Triterpenoid glucan synthase inhibitor

    Long-acting echinocandin
