    Gwt1 inhibitor
    Fosmanogepix (investigational)    		Prodrug of manogepix, which inhibits Gwt1, an enzyme of GPI-anchor biosynthesis → mislocalised wall mannoproteins, loss of adhesion and virulenceVery broad: Candida (incl. auris), Aspergillus, Fusarium, Scedosporium, Lomentospora. Weak against Mucorales and C. kruseiPhase 3; both IV and oral. Among the most promising new-mechanism agents
    Adjuncts: potassium iodide, topical ciclopirox, amorolfine, efinaconazole; sulfonamides for paracoccidioidomycosis; co-trimoxazole for PneumocystisVariousNiche indicationsCo-trimoxazole is not a classical antifungal — it targets folate synthesis, which Pneumocystis retains

    Azole resistance in Aspergillus fumigatus: a resistance problem made in the field

    A. fumigatus is not a host-adapted pathogen. It lives in compost and decaying vegetation, and every human infection is an environmental acquisition. That means resistance can be selected entirely outside the clinic. Azole fungicides — tebuconazole, epoxiconazole, propiconazole, difenoconazole and relatives — are used at enormous scale in agriculture, in timber preservation and in flower bulb production, and they are structurally close enough to medical triazoles to select cross-resistance in the same Cyp51A target.

    The signature resistance mechanisms are TR₃₄/L98H (a 34-base-pair tandem repeat in the cyp51A promoter, driving overexpression, combined with a leucine-98-to-histidine substitution in the coding sequence) and TR₄₆/Y121F/T289A, both first characterised in the Netherlands. Their hallmark is that they appear in patients who have never received an azole, and that identical genotypes are recovered from soil, compost and flower beds. TR₃₄/L98H confers pan-azole resistance and roughly doubles mortality in invasive aspergillosis; it has now been reported from more than 30 countries across five continents. Resistance can also be acquired in-host during chronic pulmonary aspergillosis, typically via cyp51A point mutations such as G54, M220 and G138 substitutions, or through non-cyp51A routes (HapE, HMG-CoA reductase mutations, efflux pump upregulation).

    The parallel with agricultural antibacterial use is close, and the policy problem is the same: the fungicides in question are essential to crop yields, and the selective pressure is exerted on an organism nobody is trying to kill. The response so far is surveillance, resistance-guided therapy (voriconazole plus an echinocandin, or liposomal amphotericin B), and development of agents with no agricultural analogue — which is much of the appeal of olorofim and fosmanogepix.

    Resistance elsewhere follows familiar mechanisms: in Candida, ERG11 point mutations, ERG11 overexpression via Upc2 gain-of-function, and efflux pump upregulation (CDR1/CDR2 ABC transporters via Tac1, MDR1 via Mrr1) drive azole resistance; FKS1/FKS2 hot-spot mutations drive echinocandin resistance; loss of ergosterol biosynthesis genes (ERG3, ERG6) can produce amphotericin resistance at a fitness cost. Aneuploidy — whole-chromosome or segmental duplication, notably isochromosome 5L in C. albicans — is a fast, reversible route to resistance that classical mutation models miss. Biofilms on catheters and prosthetics add a further layer, with matrix-sequestered drug and persister cells raising effective MICs by orders of magnitude.

    8. Beneficial and industrial fungi

    For every fungus that kills, several underpin the food supply, the pharmacopoeia, or the practice of biology itself.

    Fermented foods and drinks

    Saccharomyces cerevisiae converts sugar to ethanol and CO₂ and is therefore responsible for bread, beer, wine, sake and distilled spirits — the CO₂ in the first case, the ethanol in the rest. Lager uses the cold-tolerant interspecies hybrid S. pastorianus (S. cerevisiae × S. eubayanus, the latter identified in Patagonian Nothofagus forests only in 2011). Brettanomyces (Dekkera) produces the volatile phenols — 4-ethylphenol, 4-ethylguaiacol — described as "barnyard", "horse blanket" or "band-aid": a catastrophic spoilage organism in a Bordeaux cellar and a defining, deliberately cultivated character in lambic, gueuze and many farmhouse and American wild ales.

    Table 10. Fungi in food production
    ProductFungusRole
    BreadSaccharomyces cerevisiae (+ wild yeasts and lactobacilli in sourdough, e.g. Kazachstania humilis)CO₂ for leavening; flavour compounds
    Beer, wine, cider, sake, spiritsS. cerevisiae, S. pastorianus (lager), Brettanomyces, TorulasporaEthanol and aroma; malolactic conversion is bacterial
    Soy sauce, miso, sake, mirin, rice vinegarAspergillus oryzae (koji), A. sojae; then yeasts and lactobacilliMassive amylase and protease secretion saccharifies starch and liberates glutamate. Domesticated from A. flavus but has lost aflatoxin production; Japan's national fungus (kokkin)
    TempehRhizopus oligosporus / R. oryzaeMycelium binds cooked soybeans into a cake; reduces phytate, raises B-vitamins and digestibility
    Blue cheeses (Roquefort, Stilton, Gorgonzola)Penicillium roquefortiLipolysis to methyl ketones (2-heptanone) for the characteristic flavour; grows in the low-O₂ interior
    Bloomy-rind cheeses (Camembert, Brie)Penicillium camemberti, Geotrichum candidumWhite surface mycelium; deacidifies the rind and ripens the paste from outside in. P. camemberti is a clonal domesticated albino lineage now suffering fertility collapse
    Washed-rind and smear cheesesGeotrichum, Debaryomyces hansenii + BrevibacteriumYeasts raise surface pH so that bacteria can colonise
    Salami and dry-cured meatsPenicillium nalgiovenseProtective white surface flora; excludes toxigenic moulds, regulates drying
    Quorn / mycoproteinFusarium venenatum A3/5Continuous fermentation of an aligned hyphal biomass, RNA-reduced and texturised into a meat analogue; ~45% protein and high fibre
    Cultivated mushroomsAgaricus bisporus, Lentinula edodes, Pleurotus, Flammulina>40 million tonnes a year worldwide; grown on agricultural waste

    Pharmaceuticals and industrial chemistry

    • Citric acid — Aspergillus niger in manganese-limited, high-sugar, low-pH submerged culture yields citrate at nearly the theoretical maximum. World production is roughly 2 million tonnes a year, essentially all of it fungal, feeding the soft drink, food, detergent and pharmaceutical industries. The process, developed by Pfizer from 1919, broke the Italian lemon cartel and is one of the founding achievements of industrial microbiology.
    • Penicillin — Fleming's 1928 observation of Penicillium (P. rubens) lysing staphylococci; Florey, Chain and Heatley's development in Oxford; and the wartime US programme, where deep-tank fermentation with corn steep liquor and the high-yielding P. chrysogenum NRRL 1951 strain from a mouldy Peoria cantaloupe (plus X-ray and UV mutagenesis) raised titres more than a thousand-fold. Modern strains yield >50 g/L.
    • Cephalosporins — from Acremonium chrysogenum (Cephalosporium), isolated by Brotzu from a Sardinian sewage outfall in 1945.
    • Statins — lovastatin from Aspergillus terreus (and Monascus ruber, hence red yeast rice), mevastatin from Penicillium citrinum: competitive inhibitors of HMG-CoA reductase, and the origin of the best-selling drug class in history.
    • Cyclosporine A — a cyclic undecapeptide from Tolypocladium inflatum, found in a Norwegian soil sample in 1969. By binding cyclophilin and inhibiting calcineurin it blocks IL-2 transcription and T-cell activation, and it made routine solid-organ transplantation possible.
    • Griseofulvin from Penicillium griseofulvum; mycophenolic acid from Penicillium brevicompactum (as mycophenolate mofetil, a mainstay of transplant immunosuppression); ergot alkaloids from Claviceps; fingolimod, derived from myriocin of Isaria sinclairii, for multiple sclerosis; echinocandins themselves, semisynthetic from Glarea lozoyensis and Aspergillus natural products.
    • Enzymes — a multi-billion-dollar industry dominated by fungal sources: amylases and glucoamylases (A. niger, A. oryzae) for starch and baking; cellulases and hemicellulases (Trichoderma reesei) for textile stonewashing, detergents, animal feed and cellulosic ethanol; lipases (Rhizomucor miehei, Thermomyces) for detergents, fat interesterification and chiral synthesis; pectinases (A. niger) for juice clarification; proteases; phytases; and chymosin, now made recombinantly in Aspergillus and Kluyveromyces for the great majority of the world's cheese.
    • Organic acids and other products — itaconic acid (Aspergillus terreus) for polymers; gluconic acid; kojic acid; carotenoids and lycopene (Blakeslea trispora); riboflavin (Ashbya gossypii); omega-3 and arachidonic acid from Mortierella; ethanol at industrial scale.

    Yeast as the model eukaryote

    Saccharomyces cerevisiae is arguably the most important experimental organism in modern biology. It is safe, grows in hours on cheap medium, is stable as both a haploid and a diploid (so recessive mutations are easy to find and complementation easy to test), and undergoes homologous recombination so efficiently that any genomic locus can be replaced precisely with a designed construct — a capability other eukaryotes did not acquire until CRISPR.

    • In 1996 it became the first eukaryotic genome ever sequenced: 12.1 Mb, 16 chromosomes, ~6,000 genes, the work of an international consortium of some 600 scientists. Roughly a quarter of its genes have a human homologue with a known disease association, and hundreds of essential yeast genes can be functionally replaced by their human counterparts.
    • The yeast two-hybrid system (Fields and Song, 1989) exploits the modularity of the Gal4 transcription factor: bait fused to the DNA-binding domain, prey to the activation domain, and reporter expression only when the two proteins interact. It generated the first genome-scale protein interaction maps.
    • The yeast deletion collection (systematic knockouts of every gene, each barcoded), the GFP- and TAP-tagged libraries, and synthetic genetic array analysis made yeast the first organism in which genetic interactions could be mapped exhaustively.
    • Nobel-recognised discoveries made in yeast include cell cycle control (Hartwell, Nurse, Hunt — 2001), vesicle trafficking (Schekman — 2013), telomeres and telomerase (Blackburn, Greider, Szostak — 2009, substantially in yeast and Tetrahymena), and autophagy (Ohsumi — 2016). Yeast prions ([PSI⁺], [URE3]) provided decisive support for the protein-only inheritance hypothesis.
    • Sc2.0, the synthetic yeast genome project, has now assembled designer versions of all sixteen chromosomes — the first synthetic eukaryotic genome.

    Recombinant production and biotechnology

    Yeasts are excellent hosts for recombinant protein manufacture: they perform eukaryotic folding and disulfide formation, secrete into the medium, carry no endotoxin, and scale to enormous fermenters. The recombinant hepatitis B vaccine (1986) was the first recombinant vaccine licensed for humans, made by expressing HBsAg in S. cerevisiae, which self-assembles into virus-like particles — a landmark in both vaccinology and cancer prevention, since it prevents a leading cause of hepatocellular carcinoma. Recombinant human insulin, HPV vaccines (VLPs made in yeast for Gardasil), albumin and many enzymes follow the same route; Komagataella phaffii (Pichia pastoris) is the methylotrophic yeast workhorse for high-density secreted expression. Engineered yeast also performs feats of synthetic biology — semisynthetic artemisinin precursor production (Amyris/Keasling), opioid biosynthesis from sugar, and heme protein for plant-based meat (P. pastoris-derived soy leghemoglobin).

    Fungi as materials and environmental tools

    Mycoremediation uses ligninolytic fungi's non-specific radical chemistry to degrade pollutants their enzymes were never selected for: polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons, PCBs, dioxins, synthetic dyes, explosives such as TNT, some pesticides, and pharmaceuticals in wastewater. Mycofiltration passes contaminated water through mycelial mats; fungal biosorption removes heavy metals and radionuclides. Field performance is more variable than laboratory results, and competition with indigenous soil microbes is the usual limiting factor.

    Mycelium materials are a newer application. Grown on agricultural waste (straw, hemp hurd, sawdust) in a mould and then heat-killed, fungal mycelium forms a light, rigid, fire-resistant, compostable composite used for protective packaging (Ecovative, replacing expanded polystyrene), acoustic panels and insulation. Grown as a pure aerial mycelial mat and tanned, it yields leather alternatives (Mylo from Ganoderma, Reishi from MycoWorks) that have reached commercial fashion products. Related work covers mycelium-bound construction bricks, self-healing concrete additives, and fungal chitosan as a biodegradable packaging film.

    Fungi in one paragraph

    Fungi digest first and eat second, which forces them into a filamentous body plan of enormous surface area and turns them into the planet's principal recyclers — the only organisms that dismantle lignin efficiently, and therefore a necessary condition for the carbon cycle to close. The same absorptive lifestyle drives them to secrete chemical weapons against competitors, which is why the pharmacopoeia is so heavily indebted to moulds. Their eukaryotic nature makes them nutritionally versatile partners for 80% of land plants and superb factories for enzymes, acids and recombinant proteins — and simultaneously makes them very difficult to kill inside a human being, which is the central problem of medical mycology and the reason a small handful of drug targets carries the entire clinical burden of a disease group that now kills more people each year than malaria.

    Protists, Protozoa & Microalgae

    Everything eukaryotic that is not an animal, a land plant, or a fungus has historically been swept into a single bin labelled "protist." That bin contains organisms as different from one another as a whale is from a mushroom: predators that engulf other cells whole, photosynthesisers that fix a substantial share of the planet's carbon, parasites responsible for hundreds of thousands of deaths every year, amoebae that aggregate into a crawling multicellular slug, ciliates with two kinds of nucleus, and single cells that build glass or limestone shells of geometric precision. This section explains why "Protista" collapsed as a taxon, rebuilds the eukaryotic tree along modern supergroup lines, tours the cell biology that makes protists so strange, works systematically through the pathogens and the primary producers, and closes with the microscopic animals — rotifers, tardigrades, nematodes, mites — that share the same drop of water and the same microscope slide.

    1. What Protists Are — and Why "Protista" Died

    A protist is a eukaryote that is not a land plant, a multicellular animal, or a true fungus. That is a definition by exclusion, and it is the only honest one available. Ernst Haeckel proposed the kingdom Protista in 1866 as a home for organisms that fitted neither the animal nor the plant kingdom; Robert Whittaker's influential five-kingdom scheme of 1969 kept it. Molecular phylogenetics dismantled the idea within a generation.

    The problem is that Protista is paraphyletic — it contains a common ancestor and only some of its descendants. Animals and fungi are nested deep inside what would be "protists," sharing a recent common ancestor with choanoflagellates and nucleariid amoebae respectively. Land plants are nested inside the green algae. Any grouping that includes Chlamydomonas but excludes an oak tree, or includes a choanoflagellate but excludes a human, is not a natural group. "Protist" survives as a useful ecological and practical word — the way "tree" or "shrub" survives — but it names a grade of organisation, not a clade.

    Two further legacy terms deserve the same treatment. Protozoa ("first animals") historically meant heterotrophic, motile, animal-like protists; it is deeply polyphyletic, lumping amoebae, ciliates, flagellates and apicomplexans that are separated by more than a billion years of divergence. Algae means any photosynthetic organism that is not a land plant; it spans cyanobacteria (prokaryotes), red and green algae, diatoms, dinoflagellates, euglenids and haptophytes — lineages whose photosynthesis was acquired independently through separate endosymbiotic events. Both words remain in daily use, including in this encyclopedia, and both should be understood as descriptions of lifestyle rather than of ancestry.

    The modern supergroup framework

    Since the early 2000s, eukaryotes have been organised into a small number of large clades — "supergroups" — supported by multigene phylogenomics and by rare genomic changes such as gene fusions. The scheme is still moving, particularly at its deepest nodes, and the position of the eukaryotic root remains genuinely unsettled. The table below reflects broad consensus rather than certainty.

    The major eukaryotic supergroups and where protists sit within them
    SupergroupMajor subdivisionsRepresentative protistsKey features
    Sar (or SAR)Stramenopiles (heterokonts), Alveolata, RhizariaDiatoms, brown algae, oomycetes; ciliates, dinoflagellates, apicomplexans; foraminifera, radiolariaProbably the most species-rich eukaryotic clade; united chiefly by phylogenomics rather than obvious shared morphology
    ArchaeplastidaGlaucophyta, Rhodophyta (red algae), Chloroplastida (green algae + land plants)Chlamydomonas, Chlorella, Volvox, Porphyridium, CyanophoraDescendants of the single primary endosymbiosis that captured a cyanobacterium; plastids bounded by two membranes
    Discoba (part of former Excavata)Euglenozoa, Heterolobosea, JakobidaEuglena, Trypanosoma, Leishmania, NaegleriaDiscoidal mitochondrial cristae; jakobids retain the most gene-rich, bacteria-like mitochondrial genomes known
    Metamonada (part of former Excavata)Fornicata, Parabasalia, PreaxostylaGiardia, Trichomonas, Histomonas, MonocercomonoidesAnaerobes and microaerophiles; classic mitochondria replaced by hydrogenosomes or mitosomes, or lost entirely
    AmoebozoaTubulinea, Discosea, Evosea (incl. slime moulds)Amoeba, Entamoeba, Acanthamoeba, Dictyostelium, PhysarumPredominantly lobose pseudopodia; includes cellular and plasmodial slime moulds
    Obazoa (incl. Opisthokonta)Holozoa (animals + choanoflagellates), Holomycota (fungi + nucleariids), Apusomonadida, BreviateaChoanoflagellates (Monosiga, Salpingoeca), Capsaspora, microsporidiaSingle posterior flagellum in the ancestral state; the clade from which animals and fungi emerged
    HaptistaHaptophyta, CentrohelidaEmiliania/Gephyrocapsa huxleyi, Phaeocystis, ChrysochromulinaHaptonema (a third appendage distinct from flagella); coccolithophores are globally important calcifiers
    CryptistaCryptophyta, Katablepharidacea, PalpitomonasGuillardia theta, CryptomonasRetain a nucleomorph — the vestigial nucleus of an engulfed red alga
    Orphan / incertae sedis lineagesCRuMs, Ancyromonadida, Hemimastigophora, Malawimonadida, ProvoraHemimastix kukwesjijk, Ancoracysta, RhodelphisSmall, poorly sampled groups whose discovery repeatedly reshapes the deep tree; Hemimastix (2018) may represent a supergroup-level lineage
    Higher-level groupings are often used: Amorphea = Amoebozoa + Obazoa (broadly, organisms with a "unikont" ancestral flagellar apparatus); Diaphoretickes = Archaeplastida + Sar + Haptista + Cryptista. Almost all photosynthetic eukaryotes fall within Diaphoretickes; the euglenids are the notable exception.

    How endosymbiosis built plastid diversity

    Photosynthesis entered the eukaryotes exactly once by direct capture of a bacterium, and then spread sideways by eukaryote-eats-eukaryote. Understanding this cascade explains why a diatom's chloroplast has four membranes while a green alga's has two.

    Levels of plastid endosymbiosis
    LevelWhat was engulfedMembranesResulting lineagesDiagnostic traces
    PrimaryA free-living cyanobacterium, engulfed by a heterotrophic eukaryote roughly 1.5–1.9 billion years ago2Glaucophytes, red algae, green algae (and hence land plants)Glaucophyte "muroplasts" retain a peptidoglycan wall between the two membranes — a direct relic of the bacterial cell wall
    Primary (independent, recent)A separate cyanobacterial capture, ~90–140 million years ago, in the rhizarian amoeba Paulinella chromatophora2Paulinella "chromatophores"Genome still relatively large (~1 Mb, vs ~150 kb for plant plastids) — an endosymbiosis caught in progress
    Secondary (green)A green alga engulfed by a heterotrophic eukaryote3 (euglenids) or 4 (chlorarachniophytes)Euglenophyta; ChlorarachniophytaChlorarachniophytes retain a nucleomorph — the shrunken green algal nucleus, ~0.3–1 Mb across three chromosomes
    Secondary (red)A red alga engulfed, on one or more occasions4 (usually)Cryptophytes, haptophytes, stramenopiles, alveolates (incl. the apicomplexan apicoplast)Cryptophytes retain a red algal nucleomorph; the outermost plastid membrane is often continuous with the ER
    Tertiary / serialA secondary-plastid alga engulfed by a dinoflagellate that had already lost or replaced its own plastidVariable, 3–5Karenia, Karlodinium (haptophyte-derived); "dinotoms" such as Durinskia (diatom-derived); Dinophysis (cryptophyte-derived)Dinoflagellates are the champions of plastid replacement; some retain a full engulfed nucleus and mitochondria
    KleptoplastyPlastids stolen, used temporarily, and not inheritedas sourceMesodinium rubrum (a ciliate), Dinophysis, some foraminifera, the sea slug ElysiaThe stolen plastid degrades over days to months and must be re-acquired — not a stable symbiosis

    The chromalveolate hypothesis and its retirement. It was once proposed that all red-plastid lineages (cryptophytes, haptophytes, stramenopiles, alveolates) descended from a single secondary endosymbiosis in a common ancestor. Phylogenomics has not supported that: the host lineages do not form a clade, and the current preference is for one or more red algal captures followed by serial higher-order transfers between eukaryotes. The details remain actively contested — this is one of the genuinely open problems in eukaryotic evolution.

    2. Protist Cell Biology

    A protist must accomplish inside one cell everything a multicellular organism distributes across tissues: feeding, digestion, osmoregulation, sensing, locomotion, defence, and reproduction. The result is a set of organelles with no counterpart in plant or animal cells.

    Distinctive protist organelles and structures
    StructureFound inFunctionNotes
    PellicleEuglenids, ciliates, apicomplexansFlexible proteinaceous cortex giving shape while permitting deformationEuglenid pellicle consists of interlocking proteinaceous strips; sliding between them produces the writhing "euglenoid movement" or metaboly
    Cortical alveoliAll Alveolata (ciliates, dinoflagellates, apicomplexans)Flattened membrane sacs beneath the plasma membrane; structural support and calcium storageThe defining synapomorphy of the group; in dinoflagellates they house cellulosic thecal plates, in apicomplexans they form the inner membrane complex that anchors the gliding motor
    Contractile vacuole complexFreshwater protists broadlyOsmoregulation — collects and expels excess water entering by osmosisA radiating spongiome of tubules feeds the central vacuole; V-type H+-ATPases and aquaporins drive water in osmotically. A Paramecium may expel a volume equal to its own body every 15–30 minutes. Marine and parasitic protists in isotonic environments usually lack them
    Cytostome / cytopharynxCiliates, some flagellatesA fixed "cell mouth" through which food is taken into food vacuolesCiliary currents sweep bacteria into the oral groove; food vacuoles then follow a stereotyped cyclosis route, acidifying, digesting, and finally voiding residue at the cytoproct
    Extrusomes (trichocysts, toxicysts, ejectisomes, mucocysts)Ciliates, dinoflagellates, cryptophytesExplosively discharged organelles for defence, prey capture, or anchoringParamecium trichocysts fire as needle-like protein crystals within milliseconds; Didinium toxicysts paralyse Paramecium prey; polykrikoid dinoflagellates possess convergently evolved nematocysts
    Macronucleus / micronucleusCiliates onlyNuclear dualism: a silent diploid germline nucleus and a highly polyploid, transcriptionally active somatic nucleusThe macronucleus is generated from a micronuclear copy by massive, programmed genome rearrangement — excision of internal eliminated sequences, chromosome fragmentation, telomere addition, and amplification to hundreds or thousands of copies. It divides amitotically and is discarded and rebuilt after conjugation
    KinetoplastTrypanosomatids and bodonidsA dense mass of mitochondrial DNA within the single mitochondrionA topologically catenated network of a few dozen maxicircles (encoding cryptic mitochondrial genes) and thousands of minicircles (encoding guide RNAs). Guide RNAs direct uridine insertion/deletion RNA editing, without which several mitochondrial mRNAs are untranslatable
    ApicoplastMost Apicomplexa (lost in Cryptosporidium and gregarines)A non-photosynthetic relict plastid of red algal secondary origin, bounded by four membranesRetains a ~35 kb circular genome; hosts type II fatty acid synthesis, the MEP/DOXP isoprenoid pathway, and part of heme synthesis. Essential and therefore druggable — the "delayed death" phenotype of clindamycin and doxycycline against Plasmodium reflects apicoplast inheritance failure
    Apical complexApicomplexaInvasion machinery at the anterior polePolar rings, a tubulin-based conoid, and secretory organelles: micronemes (adhesins for attachment), rhoptries (moving junction and host-cell remodelling factors), dense granules (post-invasion vacuole modification). Motility uses an actin–myosin "glideosome" anchored in the inner membrane complex, not pseudopodia or flagella
    HydrogenosomeTrichomonas, some anaerobic ciliates and fungiAnaerobic mitochondrion-derived organelle generating ATP and molecular hydrogenPyruvate:ferredoxin oxidoreductase and hydrogenase replace pyruvate dehydrogenase and the respiratory chain. Most have lost their genome; the ciliate Nyctotherus ovalis retains one, proving the mitochondrial ancestry directly. Metronidazole is reductively activated inside them — the basis of its selectivity
    MitosomeGiardia, Entamoeba, Cryptosporidium, microsporidiaThe most reduced mitochondrion known: no ATP synthesis, no genome, no cristaeUsually retains only iron–sulfur cluster assembly (ISC) machinery, which appears to be the single non-negotiable mitochondrial function. Monocercomonoides (a metamonad) is the only eukaryote known to have lost the organelle entirely, having replaced ISC with a laterally acquired SUF system
    CystVery widespreadDormant, wall-enclosed resistant stage for survival and transmissionEncystment involves dehydration, organelle reduction and wall deposition; excystation is triggered by host cues such as gastric acid followed by duodenal bicarbonate and bile. Cysts are the transmissive form of Giardia, Entamoeba and Acanthamoeba and the reason simple boiling or filtration matters more than chlorination for some waterborne parasites

    Locomotion

    Modes of protist movement
    ModeStructureMechanismExamples
    LobopodiaBlunt, finger-like pseudopodiaSol–gel transitions of the actin cortex with hydrostatic pressure driving the advancing frontAmoeba proteus, Entamoeba histolytica
    FilopodiaFine, tapering, often branchedBundled actin filamentsVampyrella, Euglypha, many cercozoans
    ReticulopodiaAnastomosing nets of thin pseudopodiaBidirectional cytoplasmic streaming along microtubule tracks within a fused networkForaminifera — the net functions as an extracellular digestion and capture web
    AxopodiaStiff radiating arms with an internal microtubule axonemeRapid, controlled microtubule disassembly allows collapse onto captured preyHeliozoa (Actinophrys, Actinosphaerium), radiolaria
    CiliaNumerous short 9+2 appendages, often in coordinated rows (kineties)Dynein-driven microtubule sliding; metachronal waves propagate across the cell surfaceParamecium (~4,000–6,000 cilia), Stentor, Vorticella. Ciliary reversal in Paramecium is triggered by Ca2+ action potentials — a genuine, if minimal, excitable membrane
    Flagella (undulipodia)One to a few long 9+2 appendagesSame axoneme and dynein machinery as cilia; the distinction is one of number and beat pattern, not constructionChlamydomonas (two, breaststroke), Trypanosoma (one, attached along the body forming an undulating membrane), heterokonts (one hairy "tinsel" and one smooth "whiplash" flagellum — the feature that names the group)
    GlidingGlideosome beneath the inner membrane complexMyosin A translocates actin filaments against surface adhesins that are secreted forward and shed at the rearPlasmodium sporozoites (~1–2 μm/s), Toxoplasma tachyzoites
    Spasmoneme contractionCa2+-binding spasmin filaments in a stalkCalcium-triggered coiling, entirely ATP-independentVorticella — stalk contraction at up to ~8 cm/s, among the fastest movements in biology relative to size

    The 9+2 axoneme — nine outer microtubule doublets around a central pair, with axonemal dyneins, radial spokes and nexin links — is essentially unchanged from protists to human airway cilia and sperm. This deep conservation is why Chlamydomonas reinhardtii, a pond alga, became the principal model for human ciliopathies: intraflagellar transport was discovered in it, and mutations in its flagellar genes map onto primary ciliary dyskinesia, Bardet–Biedl syndrome and polycystic kidney disease.

    3. A Systematic Tour of the Protists

    Metamonada — anaerobes without conventional mitochondria

    Giardia duodenalis (also called G. lamblia or G. intestinalis) is a binucleate, pear-shaped flagellate with eight flagella and a ventral adhesive disc of the contractile protein giardin, by which it clamps onto duodenal microvilli. Its trophozoites are memorably described as having the face of an old man; their tumbling progress is called "falling leaf" motility. It has mitosomes rather than mitochondria and lacks a conventional Golgi. Infection follows ingestion of as few as ~10–25 cysts from contaminated water, producing foul-smelling steatorrhoea, bloating and malabsorption rather than dysentery — there is no invasion and no blood in the stool. Cysts are relatively chlorine-tolerant, which is why "beaver fever" persists in treated but unfiltered supplies.

    Trichomonas vaginalis is a parabasalid with four anterior flagella, a fifth recurrent flagellum forming an undulating membrane, and an axostyle running through the cell. It has hydrogenosomes, and critically it has no cyst stage — it cannot survive drying, so transmission is essentially always sexual. It is the most common non-viral sexually transmitted infection worldwide (WHO estimates on the order of 150 million new cases annually), often asymptomatic in men, and it measurably increases HIV acquisition risk. It also harbours its own symbionts: Mycoplasma hominis bacteria and dsRNA trichomonasviruses that modulate its virulence. Histomonas meleagridis, another parabasalid, causes blackhead disease in turkeys and is delivered inside the eggs of the caecal nematode Heterakis gallinarum — a parasite transmitted by a parasite.

    Discoba — euglenids, kinetoplastids, and a brain-eating amoeba

    Euglena gracilis is the textbook chimera: it swims with a flagellum, senses light with a paraflagellar body shaded by a carotenoid-rich eyespot (stigma), photosynthesises using a green-algal secondary plastid with three membranes, stores carbohydrate as paramylon rather than starch, and will happily feed osmotrophically in the dark, losing its plastids permanently if bleached. It belongs firmly in neither the "plant" nor the "animal" camp, which is precisely why it broke the two-kingdom system.

    Trypanosoma brucei causes human African trypanosomiasis (sleeping sickness), transmitted by tsetse flies (Glossina). Two subspecies matter clinically: T. b. gambiense in West and Central Africa causes a chronic illness accounting for over 90% of reported cases, while T. b. rhodesiense in East and Southern Africa is acute and can kill within weeks. Uniquely among major pathogens it lives extracellularly in blood and lymph, fully exposed to antibody — and survives by antigenic variation. The cell is coated with ~107 copies of a single variant surface glycoprotein (VSG). The genome carries roughly 2,000 VSG genes and pseudogenes, only one of which is expressed at a time from a telomeric expression site; switching by recombination or transcriptional silencing occurs often enough that each antibody wave clears one population while a variant escapes, producing the characteristic undulating parasitaemia. Once parasites cross into the CNS, the sleep–wake cycle disintegrates. Historically stage-2 disease required melarsoprol, an arsenical dissolved in propylene glycol that killed ~5% of recipients through reactive encephalopathy; the oral drug fexinidazole (2018) transformed treatment, and single-dose acoziborole is in late-stage trials.

    Trypanosoma cruzi causes Chagas disease across Latin America (6–7 million infected). Triatomine "kissing bugs" defecate while feeding; the parasite is in the faeces and is rubbed into the bite wound or conjunctiva — hence Romaña's sign, unilateral periorbital swelling. Acute illness is mild or unnoticed; then, after 10–30 years of silence, 20–30% of those infected develop dilated cardiomyopathy with apical aneurysm and arrhythmias, or megaoesophagus and megacolon from destruction of enteric ganglia. Oral outbreaks via contaminated açaí or sugarcane juice are increasingly reported, and congenital and transfusion transmission have spread the disease well beyond the vector's range. Benznidazole and nifurtimox cure acute and congenital infection reliably; their benefit in established chronic cardiomyopathy is limited (the BENEFIT trial cleared parasitaemia without altering cardiac outcomes).

    Leishmania species are transmitted by phlebotomine sandflies (Phlebotomus in the Old World, Lutzomyia in the New) and live as amastigotes inside macrophage phagolysosomes — the Leishman–Donovan bodies of stained smears. Clinical form tracks species: cutaneous leishmaniasis (L. major, L. tropica, L. mexicana) produces chronic ulcers that often self-heal with scarring; mucocutaneous disease or espundia (L. braziliensis) can destroy the nasopharyngeal cartilage years later; visceral leishmaniasis or kala-azar (L. donovani, L. infantum) invades spleen, liver and marrow, causing massive splenomegaly, pancytopenia and, untreated, death in over 95% of cases.

    Naegleria fowleri, a heterolobosean, is not a trypanosomatid but sits in the same supergroup. It is a free-living amoeboflagellate of warm freshwater. When water is forced up the nose — diving, water sports, ritual ablution, or improperly prepared neti-pot rinses — trophozoites migrate along the olfactory nerve through the cribriform plate and produce primary amoebic meningoencephalitis, a fulminant haemorrhagic necrotising infection that kills more than 97% of those affected within about a week. It is exceedingly rare (a few cases per year in the United States) but almost uniformly fatal; the handful of survivors received early combination therapy including amphotericin B and miltefosine with aggressive management of intracranial pressure. Drinking contaminated water is harmless — only nasal entry matters.

    Amoebozoa

    Entamoeba histolytica is transmitted by faecal–oral ingestion of quadrinucleate cysts. Most infections stay luminal, but invasive disease produces flask-shaped ulcers in the colon, bloody dysentery with mucus, and in a minority haematogenous spread to the liver, where a solitary right-lobe abscess of "anchovy paste" necrotic material may form months to years later. Critically, it is microscopically identical to the harmless E. dispar and the ambiguous E. moshkovskii, so stool microscopy alone systematically over-diagnoses amoebiasis; antigen detection or PCR is required. Treatment requires both a tissue amoebicide (metronidazole or tinidazole) and a luminal agent (paromomycin or diloxanide furoate) — omitting the latter leaves cysts in the gut and invites relapse.

    Acanthamoeba spp. are ubiquitous soil and water amoebae with characteristic spiny acanthopodia and double-walled cysts. In contact lens wearers — especially those rinsing lenses in tap water or wearing them while swimming — they cause Acanthamoeba keratitis: an exquisitely painful, ring-shaped corneal infiltrate frequently misdiagnosed as herpetic or fungal keratitis for weeks, with vision loss or the need for corneal transplantation as the price of delay. In the immunocompromised they can cause granulomatous amoebic encephalitis, a subacute and usually fatal illness. They are also environmentally important as reservoirs and training grounds for intracellular bacteria: Legionella pneumophila replicates inside them, and the giant mimiviruses were discovered inside them. Balamuthia mandrillaris causes a similar encephalitis but, unusually, strikes immunocompetent people too, often following soil exposure.

    Dictyostelium discoideum, the social amoeba, is a premier model for the evolution of cooperation. Solitary amoebae feed on bacteria; on starvation they secrete cAMP in relayed pulses, streaming into an aggregate of ~105 cells that forms a migrating "slug" with anterior–posterior polarity, then culminates into a fruiting body in which roughly 20% of the cells die to form a cellulose stalk that lifts the remaining 80% as spores. That altruism, and the cheating strategies that exploit it, make it a workhorse for kin selection theory as well as for chemotaxis and phagocytosis research. Physarum polycephalum, a plasmodial slime mould, takes the opposite route: a single giant multinucleate cell, sometimes metres across, that streams cytoplasm rhythmically and can solve maze and network-optimisation problems — famously reproducing an approximation of the Tokyo rail network when oat flakes were placed at station positions.

    SAR: Alveolata — Apicomplexa

    All apicomplexans are obligate intracellular parasites with the apical invasion complex described above. They include the single most consequential group of eukaryotic pathogens.

    The human malaria parasites compared

    P. vivaxWidest geographic range; dominant outside Africa (Asia, Latin America, Horn of Africa)~48 h (benign tertian)Reticulocytes only → lower parasitaemiaYesRelapsing; requires Duffy antigen in most strains, historically sparing much of West Africa, though Duffy-negative infections are now documented. Increasingly recognised as capable of severe disease
    P. ovale (curtisi and wallikeri)West Africa, islands of the western Pacific~48 hReticulocytesYesMild, relapsing; two cryptic sympatric species now recognised
    P. malariaePatchy worldwide~72 h (quartan)Senescent RBCsNo (but true long-term recrudescence)Can persist subclinically for decades; associated with immune-complex nephrotic syndrome in children
    P. knowlesiSoutheast Asia, especially Malaysian Borneo~24 h (quotidian)All agesNoZoonosis from macaques; rapid cycle allows dangerous parasitaemia. Microscopically mimics P. malariae, so it is routinely misidentified without PCR

    The malaria life cycle. A female Anopheles mosquito injects sporozoites with her saliva; within about 30 minutes they reach the liver and enter hepatocytes. There they undergo exo-erythrocytic schizogony, each sporozoite yielding tens of thousands of merozoites over 5–15 days — a clinically silent phase. In P. vivax and P. ovale, a subset instead becomes dormant hypnozoites that reactivate weeks to years later, causing relapse; this is why radical cure demands an 8-aminoquinoline (primaquine or single-dose tafenoquine) and therefore G6PD testing, since these drugs cause haemolysis in deficient individuals. Merozoites released from the liver invade erythrocytes and begin the erythrocytic cycle: ring stage → trophozoite (digesting haemoglobin and crystallising toxic heme into inert haemozoin) → schizont → rupture, releasing 8–32 merozoites. Synchronous rupture and the resulting cytokine surge produce the classic paroxysm of rigor, fever and drenching sweat. A fraction of parasites instead differentiate into male and female gametocytes, which are inert in humans but, once taken up in a blood meal, exflagellate and fertilise in the mosquito midgut. The motile ookinete traverses the midgut wall and encysts as an oocyst; thousands of sporozoites burst out and migrate to the salivary glands, completing the cycle in 10–18 days depending on temperature.

    Drug resistance is the recurring crisis. Chloroquine resistance (pfcrt mutations) spread from Southeast Asia and South America in the 1950s–80s; sulfadoxine–pyrimethamine followed. Artemisinin partial resistance, driven by pfkelch13 propeller mutations, emerged in the Greater Mekong and has now been documented in East Africa (Rwanda, Uganda, Eritrea) with independent origins. Artemisinin-based combination therapy remains first line; intravenous artesunate is unambiguously superior to quinine for severe malaria. The RTS,S/AS01 and R21/Matrix-M vaccines, recommended by WHO in 2021 and 2023, are the first licensed vaccines against any human parasite, though efficacy is partial and wanes.

    Toxoplasma gondii may infect a quarter to a third of all humans. Felids are the only definitive hosts — only in cat intestine does the sexual cycle occur, producing environmentally robust oocysts shed for a week or two after a cat's first infection. Humans acquire it from undercooked meat containing tissue cysts, from oocyst-contaminated soil, water or produce, or transplacentally. In immunocompetent adults infection is usually asymptomatic or mononucleosis-like, after which bradyzoite tissue cysts persist for life in brain and muscle. The danger lies at the extremes: primary infection in pregnancy can cause chorioretinitis, hydrocephalus and intracranial calcifications in the fetus, and reactivation in advanced HIV or transplant immunosuppression causes ring-enhancing cerebral lesions. Its behavioural manipulation of rodents is real and well replicated — infected rats and mice lose their innate aversion to feline odour, a plausible transmission adaptation mediated by cyst burden and dopaminergic/limbic changes. Extrapolations to human personality, risk-taking, entrepreneurship or road-traffic accidents rest largely on small observational studies with confounding and publication bias, and the large, well-controlled cohort analyses have generally not supported them. Treat that literature with caution.

    Cryptosporidium parvum (zoonotic, from calves) and C. hominis (anthroponotic) cause profuse watery diarrhoea. Three features make them formidable: oocysts are already sporulated and infectious the moment they are shed, the infectious dose can be fewer than 10 oocysts, and the thick-walled oocyst is essentially resistant to chlorine at the concentrations used in drinking water and swimming pools. Control therefore depends on filtration, ozone or UV. The 1993 Milwaukee outbreak, following a treatment-plant failure, affected an estimated 403,000 people — the largest documented waterborne outbreak in United States history. In the immunocompetent the illness is self-limiting; in advanced HIV it can be chronic and life-threatening, and nitazoxanide performs poorly without immune reconstitution. Uniquely, the parasite lives in an intracellular but extracytoplasmic niche under the host cell membrane, and it has lost both its apicoplast and its mitochondrial genome.

    Other apicomplexans: Cyclospora cayetanensis causes prolonged relapsing diarrhoea and, in contrast to Cryptosporidium, sheds unsporulated oocysts that need days in the environment to become infectious — hence no direct person-to-person spread and a characteristic epidemiology of imported fresh produce (raspberries, basil, cilantro, salad mixes). Cystoisospora belli (formerly Isospora) is an AIDS-associated diarrhoeal agent. Babesia microti is a tick-borne intraerythrocytic parasite producing a malaria-like illness with the pathognomonic but uncommon "Maltese cross" tetrad on smear; asplenic and elderly patients are at greatest risk. Theileria parva causes East Coast fever in cattle and is remarkable for transforming host lymphocytes into an immortalised, reversibly cancer-like state. Eimeria species cause coccidiosis in poultry, one of the costliest diseases in global agriculture.

    SAR: Alveolata — Ciliophora

    Ciliates are the most morphologically elaborate single cells known. Paramecium swims by metachronal ciliary waves, feeds through an oral groove, osmoregulates with two contractile vacuoles, defends itself with trichocysts, and exchanges micronuclear haploid gametes during conjugation. Some strains carry Caedibacter endosymbionts conferring the "killer" trait — they release R-bodies lethal to sensitive conspecifics. Tetrahymena thermophila has an outsized place in molecular biology: the self-splicing rRNA intron discovered in it established that RNA can be catalytic (Cech, Nobel 1989), and telomeres and telomerase were characterised in it (Blackburn, Greider and Szostak, Nobel 2009) — a pond ciliate underwriting two Nobel Prizes and, indirectly, much of cancer biology. Stentor coeruleus, a trumpet-shaped giant up to 2 mm long, regenerates from fragments containing a piece of macronucleus and displays behavioural habituation to repeated mechanical stimuli. Vorticella anchors by a spasmoneme stalk that coils with extraordinary speed. Balantioides coli (long known as Balantidium coli) is the only ciliate that infects humans, causing a dysentery resembling amoebiasis; pigs are the main reservoir and it is treatable with tetracycline or metronidazole.

    SAR: Alveolata — Dinoflagellata

    Dinoflagellates typically bear two dissimilar flagella — one in a transverse girdle producing spin, one trailing longitudinally — and armoured forms encase themselves in cellulose thecal plates within their alveoli. Their nucleus, the dinokaryon, is unlike any other eukaryotic nucleus: chromosomes remain permanently condensed through the cell cycle and are packaged largely by dinoflagellate viral nucleoproteins and histone-like proteins rather than by a standard histone octamer. Genomes are enormous, up to ~200 Gb in some species. Roughly half are photosynthetic, half heterotrophic, and many are mixotrophic.

    They matter ecologically out of proportion to their numbers. Symbiodinium and its relatives (family Symbiodiniaceae) live inside coral tissue, supplying up to 90% of the coral's fixed carbon; thermal stress disrupts the symbiosis and the coral expels them, causing bleaching. Alexandrium produces saxitoxins responsible for paralytic shellfish poisoning; Karenia brevis produces brevetoxins and forms the Florida red tides whose aerosolised toxin causes respiratory irritation along beaches; Gambierdiscus, a benthic species on reef macroalgae, produces the ciguatoxin precursors that bioaccumulate up reef food chains; Dinophysis produces okadaic acid. Bioluminescence, generated by a luciferin–luciferase system in scintillons and controlled by a circadian clock, is why breaking waves glow blue in Lingulodinium and Noctiluca blooms. Pfiesteria piscicida deserves a caveat: it was reported in the 1990s as a toxic "cell from hell" implicated in fish kills and human neurocognitive harm, but subsequent work failed to reproduce the toxin findings, and the consensus now is that its role was substantially overstated — a useful case study in how a dramatic microbiological claim can outrun its evidence.

    SAR: Stramenopiles

    Diatoms may be the most beautiful organisms on Earth and are certainly among the most consequential. Each cell builds a two-part silica shell, the frustule, whose halves (epitheca and hypotheca) fit like a petri dish and are patterned with species-specific nanoscale pores. Because each daughter cell inherits one valve and builds a smaller new one inside it, average cell size shrinks over successive vegetative divisions (the MacDonald–Pfitzer rule) until sexual reproduction and auxospore formation restore full size. Centric diatoms are radially symmetric and mostly planktonic; pennate diatoms are bilateral and often benthic, gliding on mucilage extruded through a raphe. Thalassiosira pseudonana was the first diatom genome sequenced (2004), revealing a urea cycle in a photosynthetic organism — borrowed from the animal-like host lineage and used for nitrogen management. Pseudo-nitzschia species produce domoic acid. Diatoms account for roughly a fifth to two-fifths of marine primary production and dominate the silica cycle and, through their ballasted, fast-sinking cells, a large share of carbon export.

    Oomycetes — water moulds — are stramenopiles that converged spectacularly on fungal morphology: they grow as filamentous hyphae and absorb nutrients osmotrophically. They are not fungi. Their walls contain cellulose and β-glucans rather than chitin; their vegetative state is diploid, not haploid; they synthesise lysine by a different pathway; they produce biflagellate zoospores with the tinsel-and-whiplash heterokont arrangement; and they generally cannot synthesise sterols, scavenging them instead. This matters practically: antifungals targeting ergosterol synthesis, such as the azoles, are useless against them, and their control requires distinct chemistry (metalaxyl, phosphonates). Phytophthora infestans caused the late blight epidemic that destroyed the Irish potato crop from 1845, contributing to roughly a million deaths and the emigration of a million more — a catastrophe rooted in a genetically uniform clonal crop. P. ramorum causes sudden oak death; P. cinnamomi devastates Australian native vegetation; Plasmopara viticola is grapevine downy mildew, the disease that produced Bordeaux mixture, the first widely used fungicide; Saprolegnia destroys fish eggs and salmonids; Pythium causes damping-off of seedlings worldwide.

    Also stramenopile: the golden algae (Chrysophyceae, e.g. Ochromonas, often mixotrophic), the yellow-green algae, the labyrinthulomycetes and thraustochytrids (Schizochytrium, an industrial omega-3 source), and the multicellular brown algae — kelps such as Macrocystis reach 45 m, showing that "protist" ancestry does not preclude large multicellular bodies. Blastocystis, an anaerobic intestinal stramenopile carried by perhaps a billion people, sits at the edge of pathogenicity: its subtypes are inconsistently associated with symptoms and it is increasingly regarded as a common commensal rather than a pathogen.

    SAR: Rhizaria

    Foraminifera extend granuloreticulopodia through apertures in a chambered test, most often of secreted calcium carbonate. Their tests accumulate on the seafloor as Globigerina ooze covering vast areas of the ocean floor, and they are the single most important microfossil group in geology: biostratigraphy for dating strata, and oxygen and carbon isotope ratios (δ18O, δ13C) in their calcite as proxies for past ocean temperature, ice volume and circulation. Essentially the entire Cenozoic palaeoclimate curve is built on foraminiferal isotopes. Large benthic forms such as Nummulites built the limestone of the Egyptian pyramids. Radiolaria build intricate opaline silica skeletons — the subjects of Haeckel's famous plates — and, like foraminifera, provide biostratigraphic markers, particularly in deep-sea cherts. Acantharia uniquely build skeletons of strontium sulfate (celestite), making them significant in the oceanic strontium cycle. Cercozoa is a diverse assemblage including the chlorarachniophytes, the euglyphid testate amoebae, and Paulinella. Plasmodiophora brassicae, the clubroot pathogen of brassicas, and Spongospora, which vectors potato mop-top virus, are obligate biotrophic plant parasites long misclassified as fungi — another case, like the oomycetes, where lifestyle misled taxonomy.

    Archaeplastida microalgae

    Chlamydomonas reinhardtii

    A biflagellate green alga ~10 μm across, the "green yeast." Haploid, easily crossed, transformable in nucleus, chloroplast and mitochondrion. Its eyespot — carotenoid pigment layers acting as a quarter-wave reflector behind rhodopsin photoreceptors — enables phototaxis, and the channelrhodopsins that mediate it became the founding tools of optogenetics. It is also the reference organism for flagellar assembly and intraflagellar transport.

    Chlorella

    Small, non-motile, spherical green algae with a tough cell wall. Chlorella was the organism in which Melvin Calvin's group traced the path of carbon in photosynthesis, and it remains a mainstay of commercial algal biomass. Cell-wall disruption is required for digestibility in food products.

    Dunaliella salina

    A wall-less flagellate thriving in hypersaline brines up to saturation, osmoregulating with intracellular glycerol. Under high light and salt stress it accumulates β-carotene to as much as 10% of dry weight, turning salt ponds pink — the basis of natural β-carotene production.

    Volvox carteri

    A hollow sphere of ~2,000 biflagellate somatic cells plus ~16 large reproductive gonidia, embedded in glycoprotein matrix. Its volvocine relatives (Gonium, Pandorina, Eudorina) form a graded series from 4 to thousands of cells, making the lineage the best available window on the transition to multicellularity and germ–soma separation. Development includes a dramatic inversion in which the embryo turns itself inside out.

    Haematococcus pluvialis

    Under nitrogen starvation and high light it forms red aplanospores packed with astaxanthin at up to ~4% of dry weight — the richest natural source, used in aquaculture feed to pigment salmon flesh and in human supplements.

    Ostreococcus and Micromonas

    Prasinophyte picoplankton. Ostreococcus tauri, ~0.8 μm in diameter with one chloroplast, one mitochondrion and a ~12.6 Mb genome, is the smallest known free-living eukaryote — smaller than many bacteria — and a model for minimal eukaryotic cell architecture and circadian rhythms.

    Haptista — the coccolithophores

    Coccolithophores armour themselves in coccoliths: individually sculpted calcite plates made intracellularly in a Golgi-derived vesicle and then exported to the cell surface. Emiliania huxleyi (formally transferred to Gephyrocapsa huxleyi) is the most abundant coccolithophore, forming blooms that can exceed 100,000 km2 and are visible from orbit as milky turquoise swirls, because shed coccoliths scatter light intensely. The White Cliffs of Dover and the Cretaceous chalk of northern Europe are largely coccolith debris — the period is named for it (creta, chalk). Blooms are terminated in part by giant coccolithoviruses (EhV). Coccolithophores are also the main producers of dimethylsulfoniopropionate (DMSP), discussed below, and they complicate the ocean's carbon budget: their calcification consumes alkalinity and actually releases CO2 even as their organic carbon export removes it — the "carbonate counter-pump."

    4. Protozoan Pathogens of Humans

    5. Microalgae and Global Ecology

    Marine phytoplankton — diatoms, dinoflagellates, coccolithophores, picoeukaryotes, and the cyanobacteria Prochlorococcus and Synechococcus — constitute less than 1% of Earth's photosynthetic biomass yet perform roughly half of global net primary production, on the order of 45–50 gigatonnes of carbon fixed per year. They achieve this through turnover: the standing stock is replaced every few days, whereas a forest turns over across decades.

    "Phytoplankton produce half the oxygen you breathe" — what this actually means. The statement is true about gross oxygen production and misleading about oxygen supply. Nearly all of the organic matter phytoplankton produce is respired within the ocean — by the algae themselves, by grazers, and above all by heterotrophic bacteria remineralising sinking material. That respiration consumes almost exactly as much O2 as photosynthesis released. The net contribution of the ocean to atmospheric oxygen is therefore close to zero, and the same is true of mature forests including the Amazon.

    Atmospheric O2 accumulates only when organic carbon escapes respiration by being buried in sediment, which is a small fraction of a percent of primary production. The ~21% oxygen in the air is a vast reservoir accumulated over hundreds of millions of years of that slow leak, buffered by the far larger cycle of rock weathering. If all photosynthesis stopped tomorrow, oxygen depletion would take thousands of years; food-web collapse would kill us long before suffocation did. The honest version of the claim is: about half of the oxygen molecules released each year by photosynthesis come from the ocean — which is remarkable enough without the embellishment.

    The biological carbon pump

    Photosynthesis at the surface, followed by sinking of dead cells, faecal pellets and aggregated "marine snow," transports carbon from the atmosphere-connected surface into the deep ocean where it is isolated for centuries to millennia. Export production is roughly 5–12 Gt C/yr, with only a small percentage reaching the seafloor. Diatoms and coccolithophores punch above their weight because their mineral shells act as ballast, accelerating sinking and shielding organic matter from bacterial attack. Two counteracting processes complicate the picture: the carbonate counter-pump, in which calcification by coccolithophores and foraminifera shifts seawater carbonate chemistry and releases CO2, and ocean acidification, which is expected to impair calcification with effects that remain difficult to project.

    CylindrospermopsinHepato- and nephrotoxicosisRaphidiopsis raciborskii and relativesInhibits protein synthesis; genotoxicLiver and kidney injury; implicated in the 1979 Palm Island "mystery disease"Highly water-soluble and persists after cells lyse, so filtration of cells alone is insufficient

    Eutrophication and dead zones

    Nutrient loading — nitrogen and phosphorus from fertiliser runoff, sewage and animal waste — drives excessive algal growth. When the bloom dies, bacterial decomposition consumes dissolved oxygen faster than it can be resupplied across a stratified water column, producing hypoxia (below ~2 mg O2/L) and mass mortality of anything that cannot swim away. The northern Gulf of Mexico dead zone, driven by the Mississippi basin, has typically covered 15,000–22,000 km2 each summer. The Baltic Sea contains the largest human-caused hypoxic area in the world, and its recovery is slowed by internal phosphorus release from anoxic sediments — a self-sustaining trap. Globally, more than 400 coastal dead zones have been documented and the number has grown roughly exponentially since the 1960s.

    DMSP, sulfur and clouds

    Many marine algae, particularly coccolithophores and dinoflagellates, accumulate dimethylsulfoniopropionate as an osmolyte, antioxidant and possibly grazing deterrent. When cells are grazed, lysed by viruses, or senesce, DMSP is cleaved to dimethyl sulfide (DMS), a volatile gas that ventilates to the atmosphere and is oxidised to sulfate aerosol. Those aerosols act as cloud condensation nuclei over the remote ocean, where other sources are scarce. The CLAW hypothesis (Charlson, Lovelock, Andreae and Warren, 1987) proposed this as a planetary thermostat: warming → more DMS → more cloud → cooling. Three decades of work have not confirmed a strong regulatory feedback; the sign and magnitude are uncertain, and other aerosol sources including sea spray organics matter substantially. DMS is nonetheless a major term in the global sulfur cycle and the source of the characteristic smell of the seaside — a smell that also guides foraging seabirds toward productive water.

    6. Microscopic Animals

    These are animals — multicellular, with true tissues and organs, sitting inside Metazoa within Opisthokonta. They are not microorganisms in the strict sense and no microbiologist would classify them as such. They are included here because they inhabit the same microhabitats, appear in the same samples, and are studied with the same instruments; a pond-water slide contains more rotifers than most people expect.

    Microscopic animals and their extreme adaptations
    GroupSizeHabitatNotable biologyWhy it matters

    Bdelloid rotifers~150–700 μmEphemeral freshwater, moss, lichen crustsSurvive desiccation by anhydrobiosis; extraordinarily radiation-tolerant as a by-product of desiccation-induced DNA repair; ~8–10% of their genes are of foreign (bacterial, fungal, plant) origin acquired by horizontal transfer, apparently across membranes made leaky by dryingLong cited as an "ancient asexual scandal" — over 60 million years with no observed males.     Microscopic larvae~50–500 μmPlanktonTrochophores (annelids, molluscs), veligers (molluscs), nauplii (crustaceans), pluteus larvae (echinoderms), planulae (cnidarians); also parasitic stages such as Schistosoma miracidia and cercariaeMeroplankton links benthic adult populations across ocean basins and dominates seasonal plankton samples

    Tardigrades: what is true and what is folklore. The verified facts are impressive enough. In the desiccated tun state, tardigrades have survived exposure to the vacuum and unfiltered solar UV of low Earth orbit (the TARDIS experiment aboard FOTON-M3 in 2007 — vacuum alone was well tolerated; combined vacuum plus full-spectrum solar UV killed most specimens), temperatures near absolute zero in laboratory tests, brief exposure above 150 °C, ionising radiation of several thousand grays, and pressures of ~6,000 atmospheres.

    What is not true: tardigrades are not indestructible. Every one of those feats requires prior dehydration into a tun, usually with slow, controlled drying — a hydrated, active tardigrade is a soft-bodied animal killed easily by squashing, drying too fast, or sustained heat. A 2020 study found active tardigrades dying after 24 hours at only ~37 °C, and even desiccated specimens showed reduced survival after prolonged exposure near 82 °C, which has real implications for their tolerance of a warming climate. They do not survive indefinitely, they cannot reproduce in space, and the population aboard the Beresheet lander that crashed on the Moon in 2019 is not a lunar colony — nothing about a hypervelocity impact and lunar surface conditions supports viability, let alone revival.

    7. Biotechnology and Applications

    Commercial and research applications of protists and microalgae

    Omega-3 fatty acidsSchizochytrium, Aurantiochytrium (thraustochytrid stramenopiles); Crypthecodinium cohniiDHA and EPA by heterotrophic fermentation in the dark on sugarA commercial success and one of the clearest wins: DHA in infant formula, vegan supplements, and increasingly aquafeed, relieving pressure on wild forage-fish stocks
    BiofuelsNannochloropsis, Chlorella, Botryococcus brauniiLipids for biodiesel; whole-biomass hydrothermal liquefactionNot commercially viable at fuel scale. The US DOE Aquatic Species Program (1978–1996) concluded costs were far above petroleum. ExxonMobil's high-profile programme with Synthetic Genomics, begun in 2009 with a stated goal of 10,000 barrels/day by 2025, was scaled back and effectively wound down by 2023. The persistent obstacles are the energy cost of harvesting and dewatering a dilute culture, pond crashes from grazers and contaminants, nutrient (N and P) demand, and the fact that lipid accumulation and fast growth are inversely related. The science is sound; the economics are not, absent a carbon price far above current levels
    CO2 captureVarious microalgae in photobioreactors and flue-gas systemsBiofixation of point-source CO2Real but frequently overstated. Carbon is only sequestered if the biomass is durably stored; if it is burned as fuel or eaten, the CO2 returns within months. Land, freshwater and fertiliser requirements at climate-relevant scale are enormous, and manufacturing the nitrogen fertiliser itself emits CO2. Algal capture is best understood as emissions displacement or as feedstock production, not as sequestration
    Wastewater treatmentChlorella, Scenedesmus, Micractinium with bacterial partnersHigh-rate algal ponds: algae supply O2 to nitrifying bacteria while stripping N and P; biomass is harvestedWell established and economically sensible, because the value is in avoided treatment cost and nutrient recovery rather than in the biomass itself. This is where algal cultivation genuinely pays
    Diatomaceous earth (kieselguhr)Fossil diatom frustulesFiltration aid for beer, wine and swimming pools; mild abrasive; mechanical insecticide; absorbentA large, long-standing industry. As an insecticide it works physically — the microscopic silica abrades the insect cuticle and causes fatal desiccation, with no chemical resistance possible. Crystalline-silica dust is a genuine respiratory hazard; food-grade amorphous grades are used for handling safety. Alfred Nobel used it to stabilise nitroglycerine into dynamite
    PhycocolloidsGelidium, Gracilaria (agar/agarose); Chondrus crispus, Kappaphycus (carrageenan); brown algae (alginate)Microbiological agar and electrophoresis agarose; food gelling and stabilising agentsNote these come from macroalgae, not microalgae. Agar's introduction to bacteriology — suggested to Robert Koch's laboratory by Angelina and Walther Hesse in 1881 — made solid-medium colony isolation possible and is arguably the single most important material contribution of algae to microbiology
    OptogeneticsChlamydomonas reinhardtii; Volvox; archaeal and bacterial rhodopsins for complementary toolsChannelrhodopsin-1 and -2 — light-gated cation channels from the algal eyespot, characterised by Nagel, Hegemann and colleagues in 2002–2003The transformative case. Expressing ChR2 in neurons allowed Boyden, Deisseroth and others (2005) to trigger action potentials with millisecond precision using blue light, with halorhodopsin and archaerhodopsin providing inhibition. Optogenetics reshaped systems neuroscience within a decade, and a ChrimsonR-based therapy produced partial visual recovery in a blind patient with retinitis pigmentosa (2021). None of it would exist without curiosity-driven work on how a pond alga swims toward light
    Model organisms and reagentsTetrahymena, Dictyostelium, Paramecium, PhysarumRibozymes, telomerase, chemotaxis, cytokinesis, network optimisationTwo Nobel Prizes rest on Tetrahymena alone. Telomerase, discovered in a ciliate, is now a central target in oncology and ageing research
    Biological pest and vector controlVarious; also Lagenidium giganteum (an oomycete)Mosquito larvicideNiche and largely superseded by Bacillus thuringiensis israelensis, but a reminder that oomycetes can be deployed as well as fought

    Why protists deserve more attention than they get. They caused the greatest infectious mortality in human history after tuberculosis and now stand behind roughly 600,000 malaria deaths a year. They fix roughly half the planet's carbon. They wrote the fossil record from which the entire Cenozoic climate curve is reconstructed. They gave molecular biology ribozymes, telomerase, and the light-gated channels that let neuroscientists switch neurons on and off. They contain the vast majority of eukaryotic genetic diversity — animals, plants and fungi together represent a few thin twigs on a tree overwhelmingly composed of single-celled branches. Environmental sequencing surveys such as Tara Oceans routinely find that the majority of eukaryotic sequences in seawater cannot be assigned to any described species. The protists are not a leftover category. They are most of what eukaryotic life actually is.

    Microbes & the World

    Everything in the preceding sections — the cell walls, the genomes, the metabolic pathways, the capsids — exists in the world doing work. Microorganisms run the elemental cycles that keep the atmosphere breathable and soils fertile, they occupy every surface of the human body in numbers comparable to our own cells, they cause the infectious diseases that shaped human history, and they are the industrial workhorses behind antibiotics, vaccines, enzymes, fuels and half the world's fermented foods. This closing pillar takes the biology outward: into the human microbiome, into infection and immunity, into the pharmacology of antimicrobials and the accelerating crisis of resistance, into the practical control of microbes, into food and environment and biogeochemistry, out to the physical limits of life and the search for it elsewhere, and finally to the frontiers where microbiology is being deliberately rebuilt. Throughout, where a popular claim outruns the evidence, this section says so.

    1. The Human Microbiome

    The microbiota is the community of microorganisms living in and on a host; the microbiome strictly denotes the collective genomes and gene products of that community, though in practice the two words are used interchangeably. A healthy adult human carries roughly 3.0 × 1013 bacterial cells, overwhelmingly in the colon, alongside archaea, fungi, protists and an enormous virome dominated by bacteriophages.

    Retiring the 10:1 myth

    For four decades textbooks repeated that microbial cells outnumber human cells ten to one. The figure traces to a 1972 back-of-envelope estimate by Thomas Luckey, propagated uncritically thereafter. In 2016 Sender, Fuchs and Milo re-derived both terms carefully for a reference 70 kg adult male: about 3.8 × 1013 bacteria against about 3.0 × 1013 human cells — a ratio close to 1:1, with an uncertainty of roughly 25% and substantial day-to-day variation (a single defecation shifts it measurably). The human cell count is itself dominated by small anucleate red blood cells; by mass, microbes total only about 0.2 kg, not the often-quoted 1–2 kg. None of this diminishes the microbiome's importance — genetically it still contributes perhaps 100–200 times more distinct genes than the human genome — but the 10:1 claim should be retired wherever it appears.

    Body-site communities: conditions, dominant taxa and densities
    SitePhysicochemical conditionsCharacteristic dominant taxaApproximate density
    Colon / distal gutAnaerobic, pH 6–7, rich in host- and diet-derived polysaccharidesBacteroides, Prevotella, Faecalibacterium prausnitzii, Roseburia, Eubacterium, Blautia, Akkermansia muciniphila; archaeon Methanobrevibacter smithii1011–1012 per gram of content

    Oral cavity (multiple distinct habitats)Saliva flow, biofilm on non-shedding enamel, pH swings after mealsStreptococcus (mitis, sanguinis, mutans), Actinomyces, Veillonella, Fusobacterium nucleatum (a bridging organism), Porphyromonas gingivalis in disease108–109 per mL saliva; ~1011 per gram plaque
    Upper respiratory tractMucociliary clearance, humidified air, IgAMoraxella, Corynebacterium, Dolosigranulum, Staphylococcus; carriage of Streptococcus pneumoniae, Neisseria meningitidis, Haemophilus influenzae103–106 per swab
