    Vagina (reproductive age)Glycogen from oestrogenised epithelium → lactate; pH commonly <4.5Lactobacillus crispatus, L. gasseri, L. iners, L. jensenii; or diverse anaerobes (Gardnerella, Atopobium, Prevotella)107–108 per mL fluid

    Vaginal communities are conventionally sorted into community state types (CSTs): I (L. crispatus-dominated), II (L. gasseri), III (L. iners), IV (diverse, low-Lactobacillus, anaerobe-rich, subdivided IV-A/IV-B and associated with bacterial vaginosis and elevated risk of preterm birth and STI acquisition) and V (L. jensenii). CST prevalence differs markedly by ancestry, with CST IV common in asymptomatic women of African and Hispanic ancestry — a reminder that "dysbiosis" defined against a mostly European reference population is a shaky construct.

    The placental and in utero microbiome: contested. A run of studies in the 2010s reported bacterial DNA in placenta, amniotic fluid and meconium and proposed that colonisation begins before birth. Subsequent work with rigorous negative controls showed that most or all of these signals are explicable by DNA contamination of extraction kits and reagents (the "kitome"), operating-theatre air, and index-hopping on sequencers. Large controlled studies (notably de Goffau et al., Nature 2019) found no consistent placental microbiome beyond contaminants and occasional Streptococcus agalactiae. The prevailing view is that the healthy term womb is essentially sterile and that meaningful colonisation begins at birth — but this is a live argument, and low-biomass microbiome work of any kind should be read with the contamination question front of mind.

    Colonisation and assembly

    Vaginally delivered neonates acquire an initial community resembling the maternal vaginal and faecal microbiota (lactobacilli, Prevotella, Bacteroides); caesarean-delivered infants acquire skin- and environment-associated taxa (Staphylococcus, Corynebacterium, Cutibacterium) and show delayed Bacteroides establishment. Epidemiological associations exist between caesarean birth and later asthma, allergy, obesity and coeliac disease, but effect sizes are small and confounded by the indication for surgery, intrapartum antibiotics and breastfeeding rates; "vaginal seeding" (swabbing neonates with maternal vaginal fluid) is not recommended outside trials, as it partially and transiently shifts community composition while risking transmission of group B Streptococcus, HSV and gonococcus.

    Breast milk is the strongest single determinant of early-life community structure. Human milk oligosaccharides (HMOs) — more than 150 structures, at 5–15 g/L, the third most abundant milk solid — are indigestible by the infant and function as selective substrates. Bifidobacterium longum subsp. infantis carries a ~43 kb gene cluster encoding HMO transporters and intracellular glycosidases allowing it to consume intact HMOs, and it typically dominates the breast-fed infant gut, acidifying the lumen with acetate and lactate and suppressing Enterobacteriaceae. Weaning onto solid food drives the transition toward an adult-like, Bacteroidetes/Firmicutes-dominated, fibre-degrading community, broadly stabilising by around age 3 and remaining individually distinctive but perturbable thereafter.

    What the microbiome actually does

    • Short-chain fatty acid production. Colonic fermentation of resistant starch and non-starch polysaccharides yields SCFAs in roughly a 60:20:20 molar ratio of acetate : propionate : butyrate, totalling perhaps 400–600 mmol/day and supplying on the order of 5–10% of human energy requirements. Butyrate (from Faecalibacterium, Roseburia, Eubacterium rectale) is the preferred fuel of colonocytes, supports epithelial barrier integrity and tight junctions, inhibits histone deacetylases, and promotes regulatory T cell induction. Propionate is largely taken up by the liver as a gluconeogenic substrate. Acetate enters the systemic circulation and peripheral lipogenesis, and signals via GPR41/GPR43 (FFAR3/FFAR2).
    • Vitamin synthesis. Gut bacteria produce menaquinones (vitamin K2) and B vitamins including folate, riboflavin, biotin and cobalamin. The contribution is real but partial: colonic B12 is largely unavailable because the intrinsic-factor-dependent absorption site is the terminal ileum, upstream of the main production zone. Do not overstate this.
    • Bile acid transformation. Bacterial bile salt hydrolases deconjugate primary bile acids; 7α-dehydroxylation by a small guild (Clostridium scindens and relatives) generates secondary bile acids such as deoxycholic and lithocholic acid, which act as FXR and TGR5 ligands regulating host lipid and glucose metabolism — and which inhibit Clostridioides difficile germination and outgrowth.
    • Colonisation resistance. The resident community excludes incomers by consuming nutrients (niche exclusion), occupying adhesion sites, acidifying the lumen, producing bacteriocins and secondary bile acids, and maintaining a low-oxygen, low-nitrate luminal environment that disfavours Enterobacteriaceae blooms. Antibiotic disruption of this resistance is the proximate cause of most C. difficile infection.
    • Immune education. Germ-free animals have hypoplastic Peyer's patches and mesenteric lymph nodes, few IgA-secreting plasma cells, reduced antimicrobial peptide output and skewed Th1/Th2 balance. Specific signals are known: polysaccharide A of Bacteroides fragilis drives regulatory T cells; segmented filamentous bacteria induce Th17 cells in mice; Clostridia clusters IV and XIVa induce colonic Tregs via butyrate.
    • Xenobiotic and drug metabolism. Gut bacteria activate sulfasalazine, inactivate digoxin (Eggerthella lenta), deconjugate irinotecan metabolites causing diarrhoea via bacterial β-glucuronidases, and modulate levodopa availability (Enterococcus faecalis tyrosine decarboxylase).

    Dysbiosis, disease and the honest caveats

    Dysbiosis denotes a community configuration associated with disease — typically reduced diversity, loss of obligate anaerobe butyrate producers, and expansion of facultative anaerobes. It is a descriptive, not mechanistic, term, and there is no agreed quantitative definition. Reported associations include:

    • C. difficile infection — the clearest causal story. Antibiotics collapse colonisation resistance; spores germinate; toxins A and B (and binary toxin in hypervirulent ribotype 027) cause pseudomembranous colitis. Causality here is supported by intervention: restoring the community cures the disease.
    • Inflammatory bowel disease — reproducible reductions in F. prausnitzii and overall diversity, expansion of Enterobacteriaceae and adherent-invasive E. coli. Whether these are cause or consequence of inflammation is genuinely unresolved; inflammation itself generates nitrate and oxygen that select for Enterobacteriaceae, so reverse causation is plausible and probably operates.
    • Obesity and metabolic disease — the original Firmicutes:Bacteroidetes ratio claim has not replicated; meta-analyses find no consistent ratio difference. Germ-free mouse transplant experiments showing transfer of adiposity phenotypes are striking but were performed in a highly artificial system with controlled diets, and human faecal transplant trials for metabolic syndrome show at best modest, transient insulin-sensitivity effects.
    • Allergy and asthma — the hygiene/"old friends"/biodiversity hypotheses are supported by consistent farm-exposure epidemiology and by early-life antibiotic associations, but mechanism in humans remains inferential.
    • The gut–brain axis — bidirectional signalling via the vagus, enteroendocrine and immune mediators, microbial neurotransmitter and SCFA production. Rodent data (germ-free anxiety phenotypes, Lactobacillus rhamnosus JB-1 vagus-dependent behavioural effects) are robust within their models. Human "psychobiotic" trials are small, heterogeneous, frequently underpowered and rarely pre-registered; the honest summary is that plausible mechanisms exist and clinically meaningful human effects are unproven.

    Correlation, causation and replication. Most microbiome disease literature is cross-sectional 16S rRNA sequencing of case–control cohorts. Such designs cannot distinguish cause from consequence, and disease itself — through diet change, transit time, medication, and inflammation — reshapes the microbiota. Additional problems: stool faecal samples poorly represent mucosal and small-intestinal communities; compositional data require specialised statistics that are often not used; batch effects, primer choice, variable region selection and bioinformatic pipeline all change results; effect sizes are usually small relative to the dominant confounders of diet, medication (metformin, PPIs, laxatives), transit time and BMI; and a 2018 analysis suggested stool consistency alone explains more variance than most disease signals. Established criteria for causal inference (temporality, dose-response, gnotobiotic transfer, and above all intervention) are met for C. difficile and few other conditions. Treat direct-to-consumer microbiome tests offering personalised dietary advice as commercially motivated and not clinically validated.

    Therapeutic manipulation

    Faecal microbiota transplantation (FMT) transfers processed donor stool to a recipient, by colonoscopy, nasoenteric tube, enema, or oral capsule. For recurrent C. difficile infection it is strikingly effective: randomised and cohort data support cure rates of roughly 85–90%, substantially exceeding repeated vancomycin courses, and the landmark 2013 van Nood trial in NEJM was stopped early for efficacy. Regulatory-grade products now exist: Rebyota (fecal microbiota, live-jslm; rectal suspension, approved by the FDA in November 2022) and Vowst (fecal microbiota spores, live-brpk; oral capsules of purified Firmicutes spores, approved April 2023). Risks are non-trivial — transmission of multidrug-resistant organisms has caused death, prompting mandatory donor screening — and evidence for FMT in ulcerative colitis is modest and inconsistent, while for IBS, autism, obesity and metabolic disease it does not currently support clinical use.

    Microbiome-directed products: definitions and evidence status
    CategoryDefinitionExamplesWhat the evidence actually supports
    ProbioticLive microorganisms which, administered in adequate amounts, confer a health benefit on the host (ISAPP/FAO-WHO)Lactobacillus rhamnosus GG, Saccharomyces boulardii, Bifidobacterium spp., multi-strain VSL#3-type blendsStrain- and indication-specific. Reasonable evidence: prevention of antibiotic-associated diarrhoea, reduction of necrotising enterocolitis in preterm infants, some acute infectious diarrhoea, pouchitis maintenance. Weak or absent: general "gut health", immunity, mood, weight loss. Most products do not use the strains that were trialled, and effects do not generalise across strains or species.
    PrebioticA substrate selectively utilised by host microorganisms conferring a health benefitInulin, fructo-oligosaccharides, galacto-oligosaccharides, resistant starch, HMOsReliably shift community composition and raise SCFA output; clinical endpoints beyond stool frequency and calcium absorption are less well established. Dose-dependent bloating is common.
    SynbioticA mixture of live microorganisms and substrate(s), either complementary or synergisticB. infantis EVC001 plus HMOs; various commercial blendsSome strong niche results (infant gut colonisation); most combinations untested as combinations rather than as parts.
    PostbioticPreparation of inanimate microorganisms and/or their components conferring a health benefitHeat-killed Lactobacillus, bacterial lysates, purified SCFAs, exopolysaccharidesAttractive on safety and shelf-stability grounds; clinical evidence base is early and much thinner than marketing implies.
    Live biotherapeutic product (LBP)Regulated biological drug containing live organisms, for prevention/treatment of diseaseRebyota, Vowst; defined consortia in trialsApproved for recurrent CDI only. Everything else is investigational.

    2. Infection and Immunity

    Vocabulary that is routinely muddled

    • Colonisation — a microbe is present and multiplying without host damage or response. Around 30% of people carry S. aureus in the nares at any time.
    • Infection — the organism has invaded and is multiplying in tissue, provoking a response; may be subclinical.
    • Disease — infection producing recognisable signs and symptoms and impaired function. Most infections by most pathogens do not produce disease in most hosts.
    • Contamination — mere presence on a surface or in a specimen, with no implication of growth.
    • Primary pathogen — capable of causing disease in an immunocompetent host (Mycobacterium tuberculosis, Salmonella Typhi, Bordetella pertussis).
    • Opportunistic pathogen — causes disease only given breached barriers, immunosuppression, altered microbiota or devices (Pseudomonas aeruginosa, Candida albicans, S. epidermidis on prosthetics, Pneumocystis jirovecii).
    • Infectivity — ability to establish infection; virulence — the degree of pathology caused once established; pathogenicity — the qualitative capacity to cause disease. Highly infectious organisms may be of low virulence (rhinovirus) and vice versa (rabies virus).
    • ID50 — dose infecting 50% of a test population; LD50 — dose killing 50%. Shigella has an ID50 of ~10–100 organisms; Vibrio cholerae requires ~108 unless gastric acid is neutralised. Lower ID50 means greater infectivity, not greater virulence.
    The chain of infection and where it can be broken
    LinkDescriptionExamplesInterventions that break it
    Infectious agentThe pathogen itself, with its virulence factors and doseBacteria, viruses, fungi, protozoa, helminths, prionsAntimicrobials; attenuation; genetic surveillance

    Stages of infectious disease

    A typical acute infection proceeds through incubation (agent multiplying, no symptoms; hours for staphylococcal food poisoning toxin, decades for prion disease), prodromal (vague early symptoms, often the most transmissible phase for respiratory viruses), illness/acme (peak signs, peak immune response), decline (falling pathogen burden, waning symptoms, ongoing susceptibility to secondary infection), and convalescence (repair; shedding may persist — consider chronic Salmonella Typhi gallbladder carriage). Some infections instead become latent (herpesviruses in neurons, M. tuberculosis in granulomas) or chronic (HBV, HCV, HIV without treatment).

    Innate immunity

    The first tier is not immunological at all: intact stratified keratinised skin, desquamation, sebum fatty acids and pH ~5, lysozyme in tears and saliva, mucociliary escalator, gastric acid, bile, urinary flushing, defensins and cathelicidins from epithelia, lactoferrin sequestering iron, and the resident microbiota itself.

    Beyond barriers, innate recognition depends on germline-encoded pattern recognition receptors (PRRs) detecting pathogen-associated molecular patterns (PAMPs) — conserved structures absent from host cells — and damage-associated molecular patterns (DAMPs) from injured tissue. Toll-like receptors are the best-characterised family: TLR4 with MD-2/CD14 senses lipopolysaccharide; TLR5 flagellin; TLR2 (with TLR1 or TLR6) lipopeptides, peptidoglycan fragments and lipoteichoic acid; and the endosomal TLR3, TLR7/8 and TLR9 sense dsRNA, ssRNA and unmethylated CpG DNA respectively. Cytosolic sensors include NOD1/NOD2 (peptidoglycan fragments), RIG-I and MDA5 (viral RNA), cGAS–STING (cytosolic DNA), and NLRP3, whose inflammasome activates caspase-1 to mature IL-1β and IL-18 and drive pyroptosis.

    The complement system is a proteolytic cascade converging on C3. The classical pathway is triggered by C1q binding antigen-bound IgG or IgM; the lectin pathway by mannose-binding lectin or ficolins recognising microbial carbohydrate; the alternative pathway by spontaneous C3 hydrolysis ("tickover") amplified on surfaces lacking host regulators. All three generate C3b (opsonisation, via CR1 and CR3 on phagocytes), C3a and C5a (anaphylatoxins and chemoattractants), and the C5b–9 membrane attack complex, whose lytic role is critical mainly against Neisseria — terminal complement deficiency and eculizumab therapy both predispose specifically to meningococcal disease.

    Cellular effectors: neutrophils arrive first and kill by phagolysosomal fusion, the NADPH-oxidase respiratory burst (superoxide → hydrogen peroxide → hypochlorite via myeloperoxidase), granule proteases, and neutrophil extracellular traps. Macrophages phagocytose, present antigen and orchestrate via cytokines (TNF-α, IL-1, IL-6, IL-12). Dendritic cells are the professional bridge to adaptive immunity. Natural killer cells kill by perforin/granzyme when inhibitory receptors fail to engage MHC class I — the "missing self" logic that catches virus-infected and tumour cells that have downregulated MHC I to evade cytotoxic T cells. Type I interferons (IFN-α/β) induce an antiviral state via ISGs (PKR, OAS/RNase L, Mx GTPases) in neighbouring cells; type II interferon (IFN-γ, from NK and T cells) is the principal macrophage-activating cytokine. Inflammation (redness, heat, swelling, pain, loss of function) delivers effectors to tissue; fever, driven by IL-1, IL-6, TNF and prostaglandin E2 acting on the hypothalamus, modestly impairs many pathogens and enhances immune function — which is why reflexive antipyresis in mild infection has an uncertain risk–benefit balance.

    Adaptive immunity

    Adaptive immunity is antigen-specific, clonal, and remembering. Antigen is presented on MHC molecules: MHC class I, on all nucleated cells, presents cytosolic (typically viral) peptides of 8–10 residues processed by the proteasome and loaded via TAP, to CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes; MHC class II, restricted to professional antigen-presenting cells, presents 13–25-residue peptides from the endolysosomal pathway to CD4+ helper T cells. Cross-presentation by dendritic cells routes exogenous antigen to MHC I, enabling CTL priming against viruses that do not infect DCs. Full T cell activation requires signal 1 (TCR–peptide–MHC), signal 2 (co-stimulation, CD28–B7) and signal 3 (polarising cytokines); signal 1 without signal 2 induces anergy.

    CD4+ subsets: Th1 (IL-12-driven, T-bet, secreting IFN-γ) activates macrophages against intracellular bacteria and protozoa; Th2 (IL-4, GATA3, secreting IL-4/5/13) drives IgE and eosinophils against helminths and underlies allergy; Th17 (IL-6+TGF-β, RORγt, secreting IL-17/22) recruits neutrophils and defends mucosae against extracellular bacteria and Candida — hence the mucocutaneous candidiasis of IL-17 pathway defects; Tfh supports germinal-centre B cell help; Treg (FoxP3, IL-10/TGF-β) enforces tolerance. CD8+ CTLs kill infected cells by perforin/granzyme and Fas–FasL. B cells recognise native antigen, receive T help, undergo somatic hypermutation and affinity maturation with class switching in germinal centres, and differentiate into plasma cells and memory B cells. Polysaccharide antigens elicit T-independent responses — IgM-dominated, low affinity, without memory, and poor in children under 2 — which is precisely why conjugate vaccines (Hib, pneumococcal, meningococcal) chemically link the polysaccharide to a carrier protein to recruit T help.

    3. Antimicrobial Agents

    Paul Ehrlich's selective toxicity is the founding principle: an antimicrobial must damage the microbe far more than the host. The best targets are structures or pathways present in the pathogen and absent in humans — peptidoglycan, the 70S ribosome, bacterial DNA gyrase, folate synthesis de novo, ergosterol. Selectivity is quantified by the therapeutic index (toxic dose / therapeutic dose); it is high for β-lactams and low for aminoglycosides, polymyxins and amphotericin B, which is why the latter require monitoring. Antifungals, antivirals and antiparasitics are harder precisely because their targets are eukaryotic or host-encoded.

    Two further distinctions govern use. Bactericidal agents kill (MBC within about four-fold of MIC) and are required when host defences cannot finish the job — endocarditis, meningitis, neutropenia, osteomyelitis. Bacteriostatic agents inhibit growth and rely on the immune system for clearance; the clinical importance of the distinction is often overstated, and outcome trials rarely find a difference outside those special sites. Spectrum ranges from narrow (penicillin G, chiefly streptococci and anaerobes above the diaphragm) to extended and broad; broad-spectrum agents are convenient, collaterally damaging to the microbiota, and the principal driver of both C. difficile infection and resistance selection. Pharmacodynamics divides agents into time-dependent killers (β-lactams: maximise time above MIC, hence frequent or prolonged infusions) and concentration-dependent killers (aminoglycosides, fluoroquinolones: maximise Cmax/MIC or AUC/MIC, hence once-daily aminoglycoside dosing exploiting the post-antibiotic effect).

    Rifamycins (rifampicin/rifampin, rifabutin, rifapentine, rifaximin)Bind the β subunit of bacterial DNA-dependent RNA polymerase (rpoB), blocking transcription initiationMycobacteria, staphylococci (especially biofilm/prosthetic infection), meningococcal prophylaxis; rifaximin is non-absorbed for hepatic encephalopathy and IBS-DCidalNever use as monotherapy — single-step rpoB point mutations confer high-level resistance rapidly.     Nitroimidazoles (metronidazole, tinidazole)Prodrug reduced by anaerobic electron transport to nitro radical anions that fragment DNA — requires a low-redox environment, giving inherent selectivityObligate anaerobes and microaerophiles: Bacteroides, C. difficile, H. pylori regimens; protozoa Trichomonas, Giardia, EntamoebaCidalMetallic taste, disulfiram-like reaction with alcohol (evidence for this is weaker than the warning implies), peripheral neuropathy on prolonged use
    NitrofurantoinMultiple reduced intermediates damaging ribosomes, DNA and enzymes — a multi-target mechanism that has kept resistance rare for 70 yearsUncomplicated cystitis: E. coli, Enterococcus, S. saprophyticusCidalUrinary concentration only — useless for pyelonephritis or bacteraemia; pulmonary fibrosis on long-term use; avoid in significant renal impairment
    Antimetabolites (folate pathway)
    Sulfonamides (sulfamethoxazole, sulfadiazine)Structural analogues of PABA competitively inhibiting dihydropteroate synthase. Selective because humans take up preformed dietary folate and lack this enzymeBroad; Nocardia, Toxoplasma, PneumocystisStatic aloneRash, Stevens–Johnson syndrome, haemolysis in G6PD deficiency, kernicterus in neonates
    Trimethoprim; combination co-trimoxazole (TMP-SMX)Inhibits bacterial dihydrofolate reductase with ~50,000-fold selectivity over the human enzyme; sequential blockade with sulfonamide is synergisticUTI, MRSA skin infection, Pneumocystis jirovecii (treatment and prophylaxis), Stenotrophomonas, Listeria in penicillin allergyCidal in combinationHyperkalaemia, creatinine rise (tubular secretion inhibition, not true injury), marrow suppression, hypersensitivity
    Membrane-active agents
    Polymyxins (colistin/polymyxin E, polymyxin B)Polycationic cyclic lipopeptides binding the lipid A phosphates of LPS, displacing divalent cations and permeabilising the outer then inner membraneAerobic Gram-negative rods only, including carbapenem-resistant Acinetobacter, Pseudomonas, KlebsiellaCidal, concentration-dependentAbandoned in the 1970s for nephro- and neurotoxicity, resurrected as a last-resort agent. Plasmid-borne mcr-1 resistance (China, 2015) spread globally within a year
    DaptomycinCalcium-dependent lipopeptide inserting into Gram-positive membranes, oligomerising and causing depolarisation and potassium efflux without lysisGram-positive: MRSA bacteraemia and right-sided endocarditis, VRERapidly cidalInactivated by pulmonary surfactant — never use for pneumonia. Monitor creatine kinase for myopathy; eosinophilic pneumonia is a rare idiosyncratic reaction

    Beyond bacteria. Antifungals exploit the narrow eukaryote–eukaryote gap: polyenes (amphotericin B) bind ergosterol and form pores; azoles inhibit lanosterol 14α-demethylase (CYP51); echinocandins (caspofungin, micafungin) inhibit β-1,3-glucan synthase in the fungal wall — the most selective target available; flucytosine is deaminated to 5-fluorouracil intracellularly; terbinafine inhibits squalene epoxidase. Antivirals are mostly nucleoside analogues and enzyme inhibitors requiring viral activation (aciclovir needs viral thymidine kinase) or targeting virus-specific enzymes (protease, polymerase, integrase, neuraminidase). Antiparasitics span artemisinins, quinolines, benzimidazoles, ivermectin (glutamate-gated chloride channels) and praziquantel.

    4. Antimicrobial Resistance

    Resistance is not a modern invention: β-lactamase genes are ancient, and 30,000-year-old permafrost DNA and isolates from caves cut off for four million years both carry resistance determinants. Antibiotics are microbial weapons and signals; resistance is the co-evolved counterpart. What is modern is the selection pressure — millions of tonnes of antimicrobials applied to humans, animals and crops — and the mobilisation of these genes onto plasmids circulating among clinically relevant pathogens.

    Distinguish intrinsic resistance (a species-wide property: Pseudomonas' impermeable outer membrane and constitutive efflux; anaerobes' inability to take up aminoglycosides; mycoplasmas' lack of a cell wall making β-lactams pointless) from acquired resistance (mutation or horizontal gene transfer), and both from tolerance/persistence, in which a genetically susceptible population survives exposure by not growing.

    Mechanisms of antimicrobial resistance
    MechanismHow it worksClinical examples
    Enzymatic inactivationHydrolysis or chemical modification of the drugβ-lactamases (see below); aminoglycoside-modifying acetyl-, phospho- and nucleotidyl-transferases; chloramphenicol acetyltransferase; erythromycin esterases
    Target modification — enzymeAltered target retains function but loses drug affinitymecA/mecC on the SCCmec cassette encodes PBP2a with very low β-lactam affinity → MRSA; altered PBPs in penicillin-resistant pneumococcus; gyrA/parC QRDR mutations → fluoroquinolone resistance; rpoB → rifampicin; katG/inhA → isoniazid
    Target modification — ribosomeMethylation or mutation of rRNA blocks bindingerm genes methylate 23S A2058 conferring MLSB resistance (macrolides, lincosamides, streptogramin B); cfr confers linezolid resistance; 16S methyltransferases (armA, rmtB) abolish all aminoglycosides
    Target replacement / bypassAcquisition of a resistant substitute enzyme or altered precursorvanA/vanB operons remodel D-Ala-D-Ala → D-Ala-D-Lac (VRE); acquired dfrA dihydrofolate reductases and sul dihydropteroate synthases bypass TMP-SMX; mcr genes add phosphoethanolamine to lipid A, reducing colistin binding
    Efflux pumpsActive export, often multidrug and often chromosomal with mutational upregulation. Five superfamilies: RND, MFS, ABC, SMR, MATEMexAB-OprM and MexXY in P. aeruginosa; AcrAB-TolC in Enterobacterales; TetA/TetK for tetracyclines; NorA in S. aureus
    Reduced permeability / porin lossLoss or mutation of outer-membrane channels restricts entry; combined with a weak β-lactamase this can produce full carbapenem resistance without a carbapenemaseOmpK35/OmpK36 loss in Klebsiella; OprD loss → imipenem resistance in Pseudomonas; mycobacterial waxy envelope
    Target protectionA protein binds the target and dislodges the drugQnr proteins protecting gyrase (plasmid-mediated quinolone resistance); TetM/TetO ribosomal protection proteins
    Biofilm toleranceMatrix limits penetration; nutrient and oxygen gradients create dormant zones; up to 1000-fold apparent MIC increase without any resistance genotypeProsthetic joint and catheter infection, cystic fibrosis airways, chronic wounds, endocarditis vegetations
    Persister cellsA stochastic, non-inherited dormant subpopulation (~10−6–10−4) that survives bactericidal exposure and regrows afterwards; explains relapse despite a susceptible isolateRecurrent S. aureus and M. tuberculosis infection; toxin–antitoxin and stringent-response linked
    β-lactamases: the Ambler classes
    ClassActive siteKey enzymesSubstrates & clinical significance
    ASerineTEM-1, SHV-1 (narrow); ESBLs CTX-M (now globally dominant, especially CTX-M-15), TEM/SHV variants; KPC carbapenemasesESBLs hydrolyse penicillins, and 1st–4th generation cephalosporins and aztreonam, but not carbapenems — hence carbapenems became the treatment of choice, and hence carbapenem resistance followed. KPC additionally destroys carbapenems; inhibited by avibactam and vaborbactam
    BZinc (metallo-β-lactamase)NDM (NDM-1 first described 2008), VIM, IMPHydrolyse all β-lactams except aztreonam; not inhibited by any clavulanate/avibactam-type inhibitor. Treated with aztreonam plus a serine-β-lactamase inhibitor combination, or cefiderocol. Often plasmid-borne with other resistance genes
    CSerineAmpC, chromosomal and inducible in Enterobacter, Citrobacter freundii, Serratia, Morganella, Providencia, P. aeruginosa; also plasmid-borne (CMY, DHA)Cephalosporins including cephamycins; not inhibited by clavulanate. Stably derepressed mutants emerge during third-generation cephalosporin therapy — a classic cause of treatment failure, so cefepime or a carbapenem is preferred for serious infection
    DSerine (oxacillinase)OXA family; OXA-48-like in Enterobacterales, OXA-23/-24/-58 in Acinetobacter baumanniiVariable, often weak carbapenemase activity that nonetheless produces resistance when combined with porin loss; difficult to detect phenotypically

    How resistance spreads

    • Plasmids — self-transmissible conjugative elements, often carrying multiple resistance genes plus heavy-metal and biocide resistance, so that any one of those selects for all. IncF and IncX plasmids carry much of the CTX-M and mcr burden.
    • Integrons — site-specific recombination platforms (intI integrase, attI site, promoter) that capture and express gene cassettes in arrays; class 1 integrons are ubiquitous in Gram-negative clinical isolates.
    • Transposons and insertion sequences — move genes between replicons; Tn4401 carries blaKPC, ISAba1 supplies strong promoters upstream of OXA genes.
    • Transformation and transduction — natural competence in Streptococcus, Neisseria, Acinetobacter creating mosaic PBP and target genes; phage-mediated transfer in staphylococci.
    • Clonal expansion — a handful of high-risk global lineages account for a disproportionate share of the problem: E. coli ST131, K. pneumoniae ST258/ST307/ST11, MRSA USA300 and ST22 (EMRSA-15), A. baumannii global clones. Success reflects fitness and transmissibility, not resistance alone.

    The ESKAPE pathogens — Enterococcus faecium, Staphylococcus aureus, Klebsiella pneumoniae, Acinetobacter baumannii, Pseudomonas aeruginosa, Enterobacter species — are so named because they "escape" the action of antibacterials and account for most serious hospital-acquired resistant infections. The WHO Bacterial Priority Pathogens List (first published 2017, revised 2024) ranks targets for R&D: critical priority includes carbapenem-resistant A. baumannii and Enterobacterales, third-generation-cephalosporin-resistant Enterobacterales, and rifampicin-resistant M. tuberculosis; high priority includes fluoroquinolone-resistant Salmonella and Shigella, MRSA, drug-resistant N. gonorrhoeae, and vancomycin-resistant E. faecium. Notably, the 2024 revision downgraded carbapenem-resistant P. aeruginosa from critical to high.

    The numbers, handled carefully. The GRAM Project (Lancet, 2022) estimated that in 2019 bacterial AMR was directly attributable for about 1.27 million deaths (95% UI 0.911–1.71 million) and associated with about 4.95 million deaths (3.62–6.57 million) worldwide. The two figures answer different counterfactual questions — "if resistant infections were replaced by susceptible ones" versus "if they were replaced by no infection" — and quoting the larger one as deaths "caused by" AMR is a common error. Both rest on statistical modelling across 204 countries with sparse primary data from precisely the regions carrying the greatest burden, and the uncertainty intervals are wide. The frequently cited projection of 10 million deaths per year by 2050 comes from the 2016 O'Neill Review, was derived by extrapolation from a small number of pathogen–drug combinations, was explicitly framed as illustrative rather than predictive, and has been criticised as methodologically weak by several groups including its own contributors. A more careful 2024 GRAM forecast projected roughly 1.91 million attributable and 8.22 million associated deaths in 2050. AMR is a genuine and worsening crisis; it does not need inflated numbers, and using them corrodes credibility.

    Drivers, economics and responses

    Roughly two-thirds of global antimicrobial tonnage by weight goes to food-producing animals, historically much of it for growth promotion at subtherapeutic doses. The EU banned growth-promotion use in 2006 and the US ended it for medically important drugs in 2017, though "disease prevention" indications provide a route for continued mass administration. Resistance genes move between animal and human reservoirs — mcr-1 is the exemplar, emerging under agricultural colistin use in China before appearing in human isolates worldwide — but quantifying the human health impact of agricultural use remains genuinely difficult, and the relative contribution versus human clinical overuse is contested.

    The pipeline is broken for economic, not scientific, reasons. A new antibiotic that works is deliberately reserved, so it sells little; it is priced comparably to generics; and it is used for short courses, unlike a chronic-disease drug. Development costs approach $1–1.5 billion. Companies that succeeded technically have failed commercially: Achaogen filed for bankruptcy in 2019 within a year of plazomicin's approval; Melinta followed; large pharmaceutical companies have almost entirely exited the field. Proposed remedies are "pull" incentives that decouple revenue from volume — market entry rewards, transferable exclusivity vouchers, and subscription models such as the UK NHS scheme paying fixed annual sums for access regardless of quantity used.

    Antimicrobial stewardship aims to optimise outcomes while minimising selection: right drug, dose, route and duration; prompt de-escalation from empirical broad-spectrum to targeted narrow-spectrum once cultures return; IV-to-oral switch; shorter courses (a substantial body of trials shows 5 days is non-inferior to 10 for community-acquired pneumonia, 7 days for Gram-negative bacteraemia, and the traditional advice to "always finish the course" has no evidential basis for most indications); rapid diagnostics; procalcitonin-guided cessation; penicillin allergy de-labelling; and infection prevention and control, since an infection prevented needs no antibiotic. Vaccination is an underrated AMR intervention: pneumococcal conjugate vaccines reduced resistant pneumococcal disease directly, and any vaccine that prevents febrile illness reduces antibiotic prescribing.

    Alternatives under active development include bacteriophage therapy (highly specific, self-amplifying, effective in compassionate-use cases such as the 2019 Mycobacterium abscessus treatment in a cystic fibrosis patient, but hampered by narrow host range, phage-resistance evolution, immune clearance, and regulatory frameworks built for fixed-composition drugs; controlled trials to date have been largely disappointing); monoclonal antibodies (bezlotoxumab against C. difficile toxin B); antivirulence agents targeting quorum sensing, toxins or secretion systems, which in principle exert weaker selection; lysins (phage-derived peptidoglycan hydrolases); microbiome restoration; and CRISPR-based sequence-specific antimicrobials. None is close to replacing conventional antibiotics.

    5. Control of Microorganisms

    Terminology of microbial control
    TermMeaningTypical setting
    SterilisationComplete destruction or removal of all microorganisms and spores. An absolute term — nothing is "almost sterile". Defined operationally as a sterility assurance level of 10−6: no more than one chance in a million that a unit is non-sterileSurgical instruments, implants, culture media, parenteral products
    Commercial sterilityDestruction of all organisms capable of growing in the product under normal storage — thermophilic spores may survive but cannot germinateCanned foods
    DisinfectionRemoval or killing of vegetative pathogens on inanimate surfaces; spores may survive. Graded high-, intermediate- and low-levelBench tops, floors, endoscopes (high-level)
    AntisepsisReduction of microbial load on living tissueSurgical hand scrub, skin preparation, chlorhexidine mouthwash
    SanitisationReduction of microbial numbers to levels judged safe by public health standardsRestaurant dishwashing, dairy equipment
    DegermingMechanical removal of microbes from a limited area — largely a physical, not chemical, effectAlcohol swab before venepuncture; handwashing with plain soap
    -cide / -cidalKills (bactericide, sporicide, virucide, fungicide, germicide)The distinction matters: a bacteriostatic agent that is diluted or removed permits regrowth
    -stasis / -staticInhibits growth without killing
    AsepsisPractices that prevent contamination in the first placeAseptic technique, laminar flow cabinets

    The microbial death curve is logarithmic: under constant lethal conditions a fixed proportion of the surviving population dies per unit time, not a fixed number. Two derived parameters govern process design. The D-value (decimal reduction time) is the time at a given temperature to kill 90% of the population — one log10 reduction. The z-value is the temperature increase required to reduce the D-value tenfold. For Clostridium botulinum spores in low-acid canned food, D121°C is about 0.21 minutes and the industry standard is the "12D" bot cook — twelve decimal reductions, about 2.5 minutes at 121 °C — deliberately far in excess of any realistic starting load. Effectiveness always depends on initial bioburden, organism type (prions > endospores > mycobacteria and non-enveloped viruses > fungi and vegetative bacteria > enveloped viruses, in decreasing order of resistance), concentration and contact time, temperature, pH, and the presence of organic matter, which inactivates many disinfectants (hence "clean before you disinfect").

    Physical methods of microbial control
    MethodConditionsMechanismApplications and limits
    Moist heat — autoclaving121 °C, 15 psi (103 kPa) above atmospheric, 15 minutes at the load centre; or 134 °C for 3–4 minutesSaturated steam under pressure denatures and coagulates proteins and hydrolyses nucleic acids; latent heat of condensation transfers energy efficientlyThe reference sterilisation method. Steam must contact every surface — trapped air, overpacking, sealed containers and oils or powders defeat it. Not for heat-labile plastics or solutions. Prions require 134 °C for 18 minutes plus NaOH
    Moist heat — boiling100 °C, 10–30 minProtein denaturationDisinfection, not sterilisation — endospores and some viruses survive
    Dry heat — hot air oven170 °C for 1 h; 160 °C for 2 hOxidation and desiccation rather than coagulation — far less efficient, so higher temperature and longer timeGlassware, powders, oils, metal instruments that corrode in steam
    Dry heat — incineration / flaming>500 °CComplete combustionInoculating loops, clinical and carcass waste
    Pasteurisation — LTLT (batch)63 °C for 30 minReduces vegetative pathogens and spoilage load; does not steriliseMilk, historical standard. Target organisms historically Mycobacterium bovis and Coxiella burnetii (the most heat-resistant non-spore-forming pathogen in milk)
    Pasteurisation — HTST (flash)72 °C for 15 sAs above, with better flavour retentionModern commercial milk, juices; refrigerated shelf life ~2–3 weeks
    UHT / ultrapasteurisation138–150 °C for 1–4 sCommercial sterilityAseptically packaged milk stable unrefrigerated for months; cooked flavour from Maillard products
    Filtration0.22 μm membrane (0.45 μm for general bioburden reduction); HEPA filters remove ≥99.97% of 0.3 μm particlesPhysical exclusion — no heat, no chemistryHeat-labile solutions: antibiotics, vitamins, serum, media supplements. Does not remove viruses, mycoplasmas or prions; 0.1 μm filters are used where mycoplasma is a concern. HEPA for laminar flow hoods, operating theatres, isolation rooms
    Ionising radiationGamma (60Co), electron beam, X-ray; typically 25 kGyHigh penetration; ionises water to hydroxyl radicals that break DNA strandsCold sterilisation of disposable plastics, sutures, gloves; spices; some foods. Capital-intensive and regulated
    Non-ionising radiation — UVUV-C, peak germicidal ~254 nm (and increasingly 222 nm far-UVC)Forms pyrimidine (thymine) dimers, blocking replication and transcription; photolyase-mediated repair can reverse thisSurface and air disinfection, biosafety cabinets, water treatment. Very poor penetration — will not pass glass, plastic or through dirt; shadowed surfaces are untreated; hazardous to eyes and skin
    DesiccationAir- or freeze-dryingRemoves the water essential for metabolism — bacteriostatic, not bactericidalDried foods; lyophilisation is used to preserve cultures, which shows the limitation
    Osmotic pressureHigh salt (≥10–20%) or sugar concentrationsPlasmolysis; lowers water activitySalted fish, jams, honey, cured meats. Halophiles, osmophilic yeasts and S. aureus (salt-tolerant to ~10%) resist
    Low temperatureRefrigeration 0–7 °C; freezing −20 to −80 °CSlows enzymatic and metabolic ratesBacteriostatic. Listeria monocytogenes and Yersinia enterocolitica grow at refrigeration temperature; freezing does not reliably kill and is used to preserve strains
    High pressure processing400–600 MPa, ambient temperatureDenatures proteins and disrupts membranes without heatCold-pressed juices, guacamole, ready meals; spores survive
    Chemical antimicrobial agents
    Agent classExamplesMechanismUse, level and limitations
    PhenolicsPhenol (the historical Lister standard), cresols, hexachlorophene, chlorhexidine (a bisbiguanide, functionally grouped here), triclosanDisrupt membranes and denature proteinsIntermediate/low level; retain activity in organic matter, hence use in hospital and lab disinfectants. Chlorhexidine has excellent substantivity (persists on skin) — standard for surgical site preparation and central-line care. Triclosan use has been restricted for resistance and endocrine concerns
    AlcoholsEthanol and isopropanol, 60–80% (optimum ~70%)Denature proteins and dissolve membrane lipidsIntermediate level; rapid, no residue. Why 70% beats 100%: water is required for protein denaturation, and absolute alcohol dehydrates and fixes the cell surface, coagulating an outer protein layer that blocks further penetration; water also slows evaporation, lengthening contact time. Not sporicidal, and poor against non-enveloped viruses — alcohol rub does not adequately handle C. difficile or norovirus, where soap-and-water washing is required
    Halogens — chlorineHypochlorite (bleach), chloramines, chlorine dioxide, chlorine gasHypochlorous acid oxidises sulfhydryl groups and enzymesHigh level at sufficient concentration; sporicidal at 5000–10,000 ppm (the standard for C. difficile environmental decontamination and blood spills). Rapidly inactivated by organic matter; corrosive; never mix with acids or ammonia (chlorine and chloramine gas). Municipal water and pool disinfection
    Halogens — iodineTincture of iodine; iodophors (povidone-iodine)Iodinates tyrosine residues and oxidises proteinsIntermediate level antiseptic; iodophors release iodine slowly, reducing staining and irritation. Paradoxically, dilute povidone-iodine can be more active than concentrated because free iodine is higher
    Heavy metalsSilver (nitrate, sulfadiazine, nanoparticle dressings), copper surfaces, mercurials, zincOligodynamic action — metal ions bind sulfhydryl groups and denature proteins at very low concentrationSilver in burn dressings and catheters; copper-alloy touch surfaces reduce hospital surface bioburden. Environmental toxicity; metal resistance genes are frequently co-carried on resistance plasmids, providing co-selection for antibiotic resistance
    Quaternary ammonium compounds ("quats")Benzalkonium chloride, cetylpyridinium chlorideCationic surfactants that disrupt membranesLow level: good against Gram-positives and enveloped viruses, weaker against Gram-negatives, not sporicidal, not effective against mycobacteria or non-enveloped viruses. Non-corrosive, low odour, cheap — hence ubiquitous in domestic and food-industry sanitisers. Pseudomonas can grow in dilute quat solutions
    AldehydesGlutaraldehyde (2%), ortho-phthalaldehyde (OPA), formaldehydeCross-link amino and sulfhydryl groups in proteins and nucleic acidsHigh level / chemical sterilants: glutaraldehyde sterilises in 10 h, high-level disinfects in 20–45 min — the traditional agent for endoscopes and respiratory equipment. Toxic, irritant, sensitising; formaldehyde is a carcinogen; require ventilation and thorough rinsing
    Gaseous sterilantsEthylene oxide (EtO); hydrogen peroxide gas plasma; ozone; vaporised hydrogen peroxideEtO alkylates DNA and proteinsSterilisation of heat- and moisture-sensitive items: catheters, pacemakers, endoscopes, plastic disposables. EtO cycles take hours plus lengthy aeration; the gas is explosive, mutagenic and carcinogenic, and emissions are increasingly regulated. Hydrogen peroxide plasma is faster and safer but cannot handle cellulose or long narrow lumens
    PeroxygensHydrogen peroxide (3–6% antiseptic, 6–25% sterilant), peracetic acid, benzoyl peroxideGenerate hydroxyl free radicals oxidising lipids, proteins and DNASporicidal at higher concentration; environmentally benign breakdown products (water, oxygen, acetic acid). Peracetic acid is used for endoscope reprocessing and food-contact surfaces. Catalase-positive organisms partially degrade H2O2, and its value on wounds is doubtful because it damages host tissue
    Biguanides & othersChlorhexidine (above), polyhexanide, octenidine, sodium hypochlorite-based wound irrigantsMembrane disruptionAntiseptics; chlorhexidine bathing reduces bloodstream infection in ICU populations

    Validating sterilisation. Three tiers are used together. Physical monitors record time, temperature and pressure. Chemical indicators (autoclave tape, Bowie–Dick air-removal test packs, integrator strips) change colour on exposure and confirm the cycle was run — tape alone proves exposure, not sterility, a distinction routinely misunderstood. Biological indicators are definitive: spore preparations of Geobacillus stearothermophilus (formerly Bacillus stearothermophilus) for steam and hydrogen peroxide, and Bacillus atrophaeus for dry heat and ethylene oxide, are placed in the most challenging position in the load, then incubated; no growth confirms the process achieved its lethality. G. stearothermophilus is chosen precisely because its spores are far more heat-resistant than any pathogen likely to be present. Rapid-readout BIs detect spore enzyme activity fluorimetrically within 1–3 hours.

    6. Food Microbiology

    Spoilage

    Spoilage is the microbially driven deterioration of food to an unacceptable state; it is usually an aesthetic and economic problem rather than a safety one, and the organisms responsible are generally different from the pathogens. Roughly a quarter of world food production is lost to spoilage. Which organisms dominate is determined by the intrinsic properties of the food (water activity aw, pH, nutrients, redox potential, antimicrobial constituents, physical structure) and extrinsic conditions (temperature, gas atmosphere, packaging, relative humidity).

    • Fresh meat and fish, aerobic chilled: Pseudomonas species dominate, producing slime and off-odours by degrading amino acids to volatile sulfur compounds and amines; Shewanella putrefaciens reduces trimethylamine oxide to trimethylamine, the characteristic "fishy" smell.
    • Vacuum-packed and modified-atmosphere meat: lactic acid bacteria (Lactobacillus, Leuconostoc, Brochothrix thermosphacta) causing souring and gas.
    • Milk: psychrotrophic Pseudomonas secreting heat-stable proteases and lipases that survive pasteurisation and limit UHT shelf life; Bacillus cereus spores causing "sweet curdling"; lactic acid bacteria souring.
    • Bread and baked goods: Rhizopus, Penicillium, Aspergillus moulds; ropiness from Bacillus subtilis spores surviving baking.
    • Fruits and acidic foods: moulds and yeasts (Zygosaccharomyces, Botrytis, Penicillium expansum producing patulin), tolerant of low pH where most bacteria are not.
    • Canned foods: spore-formers — thermophilic Geobacillus stearothermophilus flat-sour spoilage, Clostridium thermosaccharolyticum gas production; swollen cans must be treated as a botulism risk.

    Preservation is the systematic manipulation of these determinants: heat (canning, pasteurisation), chilling and freezing, drying and salting and sugaring (aw reduction), acidification and fermentation (pH), chemical preservatives (benzoates, sorbates, nitrite in cured meats — specifically for C. botulinum control — propionate, sulfite, natamycin, nisin), modified atmosphere packaging, irradiation, and high-pressure processing.

    Water activity and hurdle technology. Water activity (aw) is the ratio of the vapour pressure of water in a food to that of pure water; pure water is 1.00, fresh meat ~0.99, bread ~0.95, jam ~0.80, dried fruit ~0.60, honey ~0.55. Most spoilage bacteria stop below 0.91; S. aureus grows down to 0.86 (aerobically), moulds to ~0.80, and xerophilic and osmophilic fungi to ~0.61. Below 0.60 essentially nothing grows. Hurdle technology (Leistner) is the principle that several sublethal barriers — mild heat plus reduced aw plus lowered pH plus nitrite plus refrigeration plus competitive starter cultures — combine to give microbial stability that no single hurdle could achieve, while preserving sensory quality. Salami, for example, relies on acidification, curing salts, drying and competitive flora simultaneously.

    Fermented foods and their microorganisms
    FoodSubstratePrincipal organismsTransformation
    YoghurtMilkStreptococcus thermophilus + Lactobacillus delbrueckii subsp. bulgaricus (an obligate protocooperative pair)Homolactic fermentation to lactic acid; pH drop to ~4.5 coagulates casein; acetaldehyde gives the characteristic flavour
    CheeseMilk curdStarters: Lactococcus lactis, S. thermophilus, lactobacilli.     KefirMilkKefir grains — a symbiotic matrix of lactobacilli (L. kefiranofaciens), Leuconostoc, acetic acid bacteria and yeasts (Kluyveromyces marxianus, Saccharomyces) in a kefiran polysaccharideMixed lactic and alcoholic fermentation; slightly effervescent and mildly alcoholic (<1%)
    Sourdough breadFlour and waterFructilactobacillus sanfranciscensis and other heterofermentative lactobacilli with Kazachstania humilis/Saccharomyces yeasts in a stable ~100:1 consortiumLactic and acetic acid for flavour and shelf life; CO2 leavening; phytate degradation improving mineral availability

    TempehDehulled cooked soybeansRhizopus oligosporus (and R. oryzae)Fungal mycelium binds beans into a cake; reduces phytate and oligosaccharides, raises protein digestibility

    KombuchaSweetened teaSCOBY: Komagataeibacter xylinus and other acetic acid bacteria plus yeasts (Brettanomyces, Zygosaccharomyces, Saccharomyces)Yeasts invert sucrose and produce ethanol; bacteria oxidise it to acetic and gluconic acids. Health claims far outrun evidence; commercial products must be monitored for alcohol content
    CocoaCacao pulp and beansYeasts (Hanseniaspora, Saccharomyces) → lactic acid bacteria → acetic acid bacteriaA mandatory spontaneous succession: pulp degradation, heat and acid kill the bean embryo, triggering the enzymatic reactions that generate chocolate flavour precursors. Unfermented beans cannot make chocolate
    Coffee (wet process)Coffee cherry mucilageLeuconostoc, Lactiplantibacillus, yeasts, pectinolytic organismsPectin degradation removes mucilage; contributes to cup acidity and aroma
    Dry-fermented sausage (salami, chorizo)Meat, fat, salt, nitriteStarters Latilactobacillus sakei/curvatus plus Staphylococcus carnosus/xylosus; surface Penicillium nalgiovenseAcidification and drying; staphylococcal nitrate reductase generates nitrite for cured colour; lipolysis for flavour

    HACCP and indicator organisms

    HACCP (Hazard Analysis and Critical Control Points) was developed in the 1960s by Pillsbury with NASA and the US Army to guarantee food safety for spaceflight, where end-product testing was unacceptable. It is preventive and systems-based rather than inspection-based, and rests on seven principles: (1) conduct a hazard analysis; (2) determine critical control points — steps at which control is essential and is the last opportunity to prevent, eliminate or reduce a hazard; (3) establish critical limits (a cooking temperature, a pH, a time); (4) establish monitoring procedures; (5) establish corrective actions when limits are breached; (6) establish verification procedures; (7) establish record-keeping and documentation. HACCP presupposes prerequisite programmes (GMP, sanitation, pest control) and is now embedded in regulation worldwide, including the US FSMA and EU food hygiene regulations.

    Indicator organisms are surrogates: easier, cheaper and safer to test for than pathogens, and correlated with the conditions that permit pathogens. Total coliforms (Gram-negative, non-spore-forming, lactose-fermenting with gas at 35 °C) indicate general sanitation and treatment failure but occur naturally in soil and vegetation. Thermotolerant (faecal) coliforms and specifically E. coli indicate faecal contamination and are the preferred indicator; drinking water standards require zero E. coli per 100 mL. Enterococci are more salt- and desiccation-tolerant and are preferred for marine recreational water. Methods include the multiple-tube fermentation most probable number (MPN), membrane filtration onto selective media, and defined-substrate chromogenic/fluorogenic tests (ONPG for total coliforms, MUG fluorescence for E. coli). Limitations are real: viruses and protozoan cysts (Cryptosporidium, Giardia) are more chlorine- and environment-resistant than coliforms and can be present when indicators are absent — which is how the 1993 Milwaukee Cryptosporidium outbreak infected an estimated 400,000 people from water meeting coliform standards.

    7. Environmental and Biogeochemical Microbiology

    Microorganisms are the chemical engineers of the planet. Most of the reactions that move carbon, nitrogen, sulfur, phosphorus and metals between oxidation states and between the atmosphere, oceans, soils and biomass are catalysed exclusively or predominantly by prokaryotes. Several of these transformations exist nowhere else in biology: nitrogen fixation, nitrification, denitrification to N2, anammox, methanogenesis and dissimilatory sulfate reduction are all prokaryote-only. Remove the microbes and the biosphere stops within a few years; remove the plants and animals and the microbes carry on largely unperturbed.

    The carbon cycle

    Photoautotrophs fix roughly 100–120 Gt of carbon per year, with marine phytoplankton — diatoms, coccolithophores, and the cyanobacteria Prochlorococcus and Synechococcus — contributing about half of global net primary production. Prochlorococcus, at perhaps 1027 cells, is likely the most abundant photosynthetic organism on Earth. Return flux is by respiration and decomposition: fungi and bacteria are the principal degraders of the recalcitrant polymers — cellulose, hemicellulose, chitin and above all lignin, whose degradation by white-rot basidiomycete peroxidases and laccases is the rate-limiting step of terrestrial carbon turnover. The microbial loop in aquatic systems returns dissolved organic carbon, much of it released by viral lysis (the "viral shunt", killing perhaps 20–40% of marine bacteria daily), back into the food web rather than allowing it to sink — a process with large consequences for carbon export. In anoxic settings, fermentation, syntrophy, acetogenesis and methanogenesis complete mineralisation.

    Sulfur, phosphorus and methane

    The sulfur cycle runs between sulfate, elemental sulfur and sulfide. Dissimilatory sulfate reduction by Desulfovibrio, Desulfobacter and the archaeon Archaeoglobus generates H2S in anoxic sediments — the black, rotten-egg-smelling layer of salt marshes, and the process responsible for most organic matter oxidation in marine sediments. Sulfide is reoxidised by chemolithotrophs (Beggiatoa, Thiothrix, Thiobacillus, Acidithiobacillus) and by anoxygenic phototrophs (purple and green sulfur bacteria) that use H2S as an electron donor for photosynthesis without producing oxygen. Marine algae and bacteria also cycle dimethylsulfoniopropionate to dimethyl sulfide, the dominant natural sulfur flux to the atmosphere and a cloud condensation nucleus precursor — the basis of the CLAW hypothesis linking plankton to climate, which remains an intriguing but unconfirmed feedback.

    The phosphorus cycle is unusual in having no significant gaseous phase; it is dominated by weathering, solubilisation and mineralisation rather than redox chemistry. Phosphate-solubilising bacteria (Bacillus, Pseudomonas) release organic acids that liberate phosphate from insoluble calcium, iron and aluminium complexes; phosphatases and phytases mineralise organic phosphorus; and arbuscular mycorrhizal fungi extend the effective root absorptive surface enormously, supplying up to 80% of plant phosphorus uptake. Polyphosphate-accumulating organisms (Candidatus Accumulibacter) are exploited in enhanced biological phosphorus removal at wastewater plants.

    Methane is produced solely by methanogenic archaea (Methanobrevibacter, Methanosarcina, Methanococcus) via hydrogenotrophic (CO2 + 4H2), acetoclastic or methylotrophic routes in wetlands, rice paddies, landfills, ruminants and termites. Roughly half of gross production is consumed before reaching the atmosphere by aerobic methanotrophs (Methylococcus, Methylosinus, using methane monooxygenase) and by anaerobic methane oxidisers (ANME archaea in syntrophy with sulfate-reducing bacteria), which constitute a globally important biofilter over marine seeps. Permafrost contains an estimated 1400–1600 Gt of organic carbon, roughly twice the atmospheric carbon pool; thaw exposes it to microbial respiration, and whether the resulting flux is dominated by aerobic CO2 release or by anaerobic methanogenesis in waterlogged thermokarst determines the strength of the feedback. This is a genuine positive feedback with a large uncertainty range, not a settled catastrophe or a settled non-issue; a "methane bomb" of abrupt massive release is not supported by current evidence, while a substantial gradual feedback is.

    Wastewater treatment

    Sewage treatment is applied microbial ecology at industrial scale. Preliminary and primary treatment is physical: screening, grit removal, and sedimentation removing ~50–60% of suspended solids and ~30–40% of BOD. Secondary treatment is biological oxidation of dissolved organics, usually by the activated sludge process — aerated tanks holding a dense mixed community of heterotrophs, nitrifiers, protozoa and rotifers, with flocs settled in a clarifier and partly recycled as inoculum; the settling behaviour of the floc is the process's chief vulnerability, with "bulking" caused by filamentous overgrowth (Sphaerotilus natans, Microthrix parvicella, Thiothrix) a chronic operational problem. Trickling filters and rotating biological contactors are fixed-film alternatives; membrane bioreactors combine biological treatment with microfiltration. Secondary treatment removes 85–95% of BOD. Tertiary treatment targets nutrients — nitrification/denitrification or anammox for nitrogen, chemical or enhanced biological removal for phosphorus — plus filtration and disinfection by chlorination, UV or ozone. Sludge is stabilised by anaerobic digestion at 35 °C (mesophilic) or 55 °C (thermophilic), a four-stage microbial relay of hydrolysis, acidogenesis, acetogenesis and methanogenesis, yielding biogas of roughly 60–70% methane and 30–40% CO2, which commonly makes a large treatment works energy self-sufficient. Digestion is delicate: methanogens are slow-growing and acid-sensitive, so organic overload causes volatile fatty acid accumulation, pH crash and process souring.

    Bioremediation, corrosion and biomining

    • Hydrocarbon degradation. Petroleum-degrading bacteria (Alcanivorax borkumensis, Marinobacter, Cycloclasticus for aromatics, Colwellia in the cold deep sea) are present at low abundance everywhere and bloom on spills. After the 2010 Deepwater Horizon blowout, which released ~4.9 million barrels, deep plume communities shifted within weeks to be dominated by a few psychrophilic hydrocarbon degraders, and a substantial fraction of the dispersed alkanes and gases was biodegraded at depth. This was a real and well-documented demonstration of microbial capacity — but it should not be read as "the microbes cleaned it up": heavy fractions persisted, oiled marshes and deep-sea benthos suffered lasting damage, and the effect of dispersants on biodegradation remains disputed. Bioremediation strategies are biostimulation (adding limiting N and P, as at the 1989 Exxon Valdez shoreline) and bioaugmentation (adding organisms — usually less successful, because introduced strains rarely compete with natives).
    • Chlorinated solvents. Dehalococcoides mccartyi performs reductive dechlorination of tetrachloroethene through to harmless ethene and is used in engineered in situ bioremediation of groundwater plumes; incomplete strains stall at vinyl chloride, a more potent carcinogen than the parent compound.
    • Metals. Metals cannot be degraded, only immobilised, mobilised or transformed. Microbes achieve biosorption to cell surfaces, bioaccumulation, redox change (Geobacter and Shewanella reducing soluble U(VI) to insoluble U(IV), or Cr(VI) to Cr(III)), precipitation as sulfides by sulfate reducers, and volatilisation (mercuric reductase). Note that microbial methylation of mercury by sulfate- and iron-reducing bacteria makes it far more toxic and bioaccumulative — microbes are not always the solution.
    • Plastics. Ideonella sakaiensis 201-F6, isolated from a Japanese recycling site in 2016, secretes PETase, which hydrolyses polyethylene terephthalate to mono(2-hydroxyethyl) terephthalate, and MHETase, which completes hydrolysis to terephthalic acid and ethylene glycol. Engineered variants (FAST-PETase, and the leaf-branch compost cutinase variants used by Carbios) achieve high depolymerisation of pretreated, amorphous PET within hours and support genuine enzymatic recycling at pilot and demonstration scale. Honest limits: these enzymes work on amorphous, micronised, pre-heated PET, not on crystalline bottles in seawater; they do essentially nothing to polyethylene, polypropylene, PVC or polystyrene, whose all-carbon backbones offer no hydrolysable bond; rates in the environment are negligible; and no enzyme is going to clear the ocean. The realistic application is industrial closed-loop chemical recycling.
    • Acid mine drainage. Where sulfide minerals such as pyrite (FeS2) are exposed by mining, Acidithiobacillus ferrooxidans, Leptospirillum ferrooxidans and archaea such as Ferroplasma catalyse iron and sulfur oxidation, accelerating pyrite dissolution by orders of magnitude and generating sulfuric acid. The result is water at pH 1–3 loaded with dissolved metals, an autocatalytic process (ferric iron regenerated biologically attacks more pyrite) that can persist for centuries and is one of the most persistent legacies of mining. Iron Mountain, California, has recorded pH values below zero.
    • Biomining. The same chemistry, deliberately applied. Bioleaching solubilises metal from low-grade ore — roughly 15–20% of world copper is produced by heap or dump leaching with acidophilic iron oxidisers, and the approach is used for uranium and, via biooxidation pretreatment, for gold locked in refractory arsenopyrite. It is slower than smelting but works on ores too poor to smelt, at lower energy cost and without SO2 emissions.
    • Microbially influenced corrosion (MIC). Estimated to account for around 20% of all corrosion damage, with costs in the tens of billions annually. Sulfate-reducing bacteria are the classic culprits, producing H2S that forms iron sulfide and, in electrical MIC, drawing electrons directly from the metal surface; also involved are iron-oxidising bacteria forming tubercles, acid producers, and any biofilm that creates differential aeration cells. Consequences include pipeline failures, "microbially influenced" souring of oil reservoirs, ship ballast tank damage, and concrete sewer crown corrosion by Acidithiobacillus thiooxidans generating sulfuric acid on the pipe roof.

    Soil, the rhizosphere and biofertilisers

    A gram of fertile soil holds on the order of 109–1010 bacterial cells from tens of thousands of taxa, plus fungi whose hyphal length can reach hundreds of metres per gram. The rhizosphere — the millimetres of soil under the chemical influence of a root — receives 10–40% of a plant's photosynthate as exudates (sugars, organic acids, amino acids, secondary metabolites), and supports microbial populations 10–100 times denser than bulk soil. Plants recruit specific communities; the Streptomyces and Pseudomonas of disease-suppressive soils protect against pathogens by antibiosis, siderophore competition and induced systemic resistance. Plant growth-promoting rhizobacteria fix nitrogen, solubilise phosphate, produce indole-3-acetic acid and ACC deaminase (lowering stress ethylene), and prime plant defences. Mycorrhizae — arbuscular (Glomeromycota) in ~80% of land plant families, ectomycorrhizae in most temperate and boreal trees — are ancient, dating to the Ordovician colonisation of land, and trade phosphorus and water for carbon. Biofertilisers commercialise this: Rhizobium legume inoculants (a mature, genuinely effective technology), Azospirillum and Azotobacter preparations, phosphate solubilisers, and mycorrhizal inocula. Field performance of the non-rhizobial products is inconsistent, because introduced strains must survive and compete against an established indigenous community — the same barrier that limits bioaugmentation generally.

    8. Extreme Environments and Astrobiology

    "Extreme" is anthropocentric. Extremophiles do not merely tolerate their conditions — obligate extremophiles require them and die under what we call normal. The adaptations are instructive: thermophilic proteins gain stability from increased ionic pairs, compact hydrophobic cores and chaperones; hyperthermophiles use reverse gyrase to introduce positive supercoils protecting DNA, and archaeal ether-linked, sometimes membrane-spanning tetraether lipids that resist hydrolysis. Psychrophiles use flexible enzymes with reduced activation enthalpy, unsaturated and short-chain membrane lipids, antifreeze and ice-nucleation proteins, and cold-shock chaperones. Acidophiles maintain near-neutral cytoplasm through impermeable membranes, reversed membrane potentials and proton export. Halophiles either accumulate compatible solutes (glycine betaine, ectoine — the "salt-out" strategy) or, like the haloarchaea, use the "salt-in" strategy, accumulating molar KCl and evolving an entire proteome of acidic, highly hydrated proteins that consequently denature in dilute solution.

    Extremophile categories, limits and representative organisms
